<meta>This page continues a discussion on the moral and political influence of music, transitioning from general Platonic theory to specific historical examples of Spartan censorship and Pythagorean therapy.</meta>

to the strong and the simple. And here, one must especially hold fast to the principle that if, by some means, even the slightest changes were made to alter anything here, the sensation would at first be minimal; but afterward, it would create a great difference and glide through the ears into the very soul. For <note>Plato, *Republic* IV</note> **Plato** believed that the greatest safeguard of the state was Music that was well-ordered and modestly composed. It should be moderate, simple, and masculine; neither effeminate, nor wild, nor vain. This is what the **Lacedaemonians** <note>The Spartans</note> preserved with the greatest effort. For among them, **Thaletas of Crete** was summoned from Gortyn at a great price so that he might imbue the youth with the discipline of the art of Music; this was done according to ancient custom and persisted for a long time.

<margin>Timothy</margin> But it was **Timothy of Miletus** <note>A famous and controversial Greek musician (c. 446–357 BC) known for innovating the lyre</note> who, beyond the strings he had found, added one more. By this complexity, he made Music multifaceted; for this, they expelled him from Laconia, and a decree was made concerning him. Because the language of the Spartans is distinguished by the turning of the letter *m* into *p*, I have set down the Greek text of that decree in those very words.

That decree certainly contains the fact that the Spartans were angry with **Timothy of Miletus** because, by rendering Music complex, he hindered the minds of the boys he had received to be educated, and blocked them from the modesty of beautiful virtue. And because he had turned the harmony, which he had received as modest, into the **chromatic** <term>chromatic: a style of music using half-steps, often considered "colorful" or "soft" and decadent by ancients</term> genus, which is softer. Such, therefore, was the diligence regarding Music among them, that they believed it also took hold of souls. Indeed, it is common knowledge how often a song has repressed fits of anger, and how many wondrous things music has done for the affections of either the body or the soul. Who is unaware of the story that **Pythagoras** restored a drunken young man of Tauromenium—who had been incited by the sound of the **Phrygian mode** <note>A musical scale associated with excitement and passion</note>—to a milder state and to self-control by having a **Spondee** <note>A slow, solemn musical rhythm</note> played? For when a prostitute was locked in the house of a rival, and the youth in his madness wanted to burn the house down, Pythagoras was observing the courses of the stars (as was his nightly custom). When he realized the youth was incited by the sound of the Phrygian mode and would not cease from his fury despite many warnings from friends, he ordered the mode to be changed; and thus he tempered the mind of the raging youth to a state of most peaceful mental calm. **Marcus Tullius [Cicero]** records this in the book he composed *On Counsels*—in a different way, but to this effect: "But to compare the smallest things with the greatest, led by some similarity: as when a piper, by his song, incited a hot-headed youth (as happens) to break down the doors of a modest woman, he [Pythagoras] advised..."

<summary>This page discusses the moral power of music, citing the Spartan decree against Timothy of Miletus for his "effeminate" musical innovations and Pythagoras's use of solemn rhythms to calm a violent, drunken youth.</summary>
<keywords>Music theory, Sparta, Timothy of Miletus, Pythagoras, Plato, Cicero, Chromatic genus, Phrygian mode, Spondee, moral education</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the ethical and medicinal powers of music, likely from Boethius's "De Institutione Musica" (The Fundamentals of Music). It transitions from the general effects of music to specific historical anecdotes of its power over the human mind and body.</meta>

Pythagoras said that a flute player should play a spondee <note>A slow, solemn poetic meter consisting of two long syllables.</note>: because when he perceived that the youth's raging wantonness was stirred by the speed of the rhythms and the gravity of the player, he calmed them. But to briefly touch upon similar examples:
<margin>
Terpander
Arion
Ismenias
</margin>
Terpander and Arion of Methymna, of Lesbos and Ionia, [helped] the most serious diseases by the aid and protection of song. Ismenias the Theban is said to have wiped away all the troubles of many Boeotians who were tormented by the pains of sciatica <note>original: "ischiadia," referring to hip or sciatic nerve pain</note> through his musical modes.
<margin>Empedocles</margin>
But also Empedocles, when a certain guest in a fury was about to attack his host with a sword because the host's father had condemned him by accusation: Empedocles is said to have changed the mode of his singing, and thus he restrained the youth's resentment. Indeed, the power of the art of Music became so well known <term>mnotuit</term> <note>likely "innotuit" (became known)</note> to the studies of ancient philosophy that Pythagoras and his followers, when they released their daily cares in sleep, would use certain songs so that a gentle and quiet slumber might creep over them. Likewise, when they woke up, they purged the stupor and confusion of sleep with certain other modes. They did this, no doubt, because the whole of our soul and body is joined together by a musical adaptation. For as the affections of the body are, so also the pulses of the heart are mixed with movements and modes.
<margin>
Democritus
Hippocrates
</margin>
This is what Democritus is said to have passed on to the physician Hippocrates, when Hippocrates visited him while he was being held in custody for the sake of medical treatment, as all the citizens of Democritus thought he was insane.

But to what purpose are these things? Because it cannot be doubted that the state of our soul and body seems to be composed, in a certain way, by the same proportions. Hence it is that even infants are delighted by a sweet song; but something harsh and mixed suspends them from the pleasure of hearing.

Every age and every sex also experiences this excessively. Although they may be divided by their actions, they are nevertheless joined by one delight in Music. For what happens when, in mourning, they modulate their very laments willingly? This is especially characteristic of women: that the cause of weeping might become sweeter through a certain song. Indeed, it was the custom among the ancients that the song of the flute should lead the mourning.
<margin>
Custom
and
Statius
</margin>
Papinius Statius is a witness to this in this verse: *The heavy horn bellows with its curved tip; the flute now accustomed to lead the tender spirits of the dead.* <note>original: "Cornu grave mugit adunco. tibia nunc teneros suetum producere manes." From Statius's Thebaid.</note> And he who cannot sing sweetly nevertheless sings something to himself. Not that he performs what he sings with any great pleasure, but because, bringing forth a certain mild sweetness from the mind, they are delighted in whatever way they bring it forth. Is it not also manifest that the minds of those fighting in war are kindled by the song of trumpets? If it is likely that anyone can be moved from a peaceful state to fury and anger, there is no doubt that a more modest mode can restrain a disturbed mind or excessive desire. Because when someone receives a song more willingly with their ears and mind, they are also led, not of their own accord, to the point where the body also shapes some movement similar to the song being heard. And the mind itself, remembering, plucks some melody it has heard. So that from all these things it appears clearly and without doubt: that Music is so naturally joined to us that we could not be without it even if we wished to. Therefore, the intention of the mind must be directed so that what is implanted by nature can also be held and understood by science. For just as in sight it is not enough to see raw colors and shapes unless one also investigates what their properties are; so it is not enough to be delighted by musical songs unless one also learns by what true proportion they are joined together.

<summary>This page details historical anecdotes of philosophers and musicians using specific musical modes to heal diseases, calm anger, and regulate sleep. It argues that music is fundamentally linked to the human soul and body through mathematical proportions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introductory discussion of musical theory, likely following a definition of music itself. The text is a classic passage from Boethius’s "De institutione musica," a foundational text for medieval music theory.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<sig>C iii</sig>

## On the Three Kinds of Music. Chapter Two

**I**n the beginning, therefore, regarding the distinctions of Music, it seems necessary to speak for a moment on how many kinds of Music we know have been identified by its students. There are, in fact, three: the first is the music of the world <note>original: "mundana"; refers to the "music of the spheres" or cosmic harmony</note>; the second is human music; the third is that which is established in certain instruments, such as the lyre <note>original: "cithara"</note>, or pipes <note>original: "tibijs"</note>, and others which serve the purpose of song. And first, that which is of the world is to be observed especially in those things which are seen in the heaven itself, or in the structure of the elements, or the variety of the seasons. For how can it be that such a swift machine of heaven is moved in a silent and quiet course? And even if that sound does not reach our ears—which must happen for many reasons—it is nonetheless impossible that such a very swift motion of such great bodies should stir no sounds at all, especially since the courses of the stars are joined together by such a fitting adaptation <note>original: "coaptatione"</note> that nothing so well-structured, nothing so blended together, can be imagined. For some are carried higher, others lower, and yet all are revolved with an equal impulse, so that through their diverse inequalities a fixed order of their courses is maintained. Whence a fixed order of modulation cannot be absent from this celestial rotation. Now truly, unless a certain harmony <note>original: "armonia"</note> joined the four varieties of the elements and their contrary powers, how could it be that they should come together into one body and machine? But all this diversity produces such a variety of seasons and fruits that it nevertheless makes up the single body of the year. Whence, if you were to withdraw in your mind and thought any of these things which provide such variety to the world, they would all perish and, so to speak, preserve nothing harmonious <note>original: "consonum"</note>. And just as in the low strings there is a limit to the voice, so that the depth does not descend too far, and in the high strings that limit of sharpness is guarded so that the strings do not break from the thinness of too much tension, but the whole is consistent and fitting with itself; so also in the Music of the world we perceive that nothing is so excessive that it destroys another by its own excess. Rather, whatever it is, it either brings forth its own fruits or helps others to bring them forth. For what winter constricts, spring loosens, summer parches, and autumn ripens. The seasons in turn either bring forth their own fruits or supply the means for others to bring them forth. Concerning these things, we must dispute more diligently later.

Truly, anyone who descends into himself understands Human Music. For what is it that mixes that incorporeal vitality of reason with the body, unless it is a certain fitting adaptation and a tempering, as if making one consonance of low and high voices? What else is it that joins the parts of the soul together, or the soul with the mind? Which, as it pleases Aristotle, is joined from the rational and the irrational. Truly, what is it that mixes the elements of the body, or holds the parts together in a fixed adaptation? But concerning this also, I shall speak later.

The third is the Music which is said to reside in certain instruments. This is administered either by tension, as in strings; or by breath, as in pipes or those which are moved by water; or by a certain percussion, as in those which are struck upon certain hollow brass vessels, and from there diverse sounds are produced. Concerning this instrumental Music, it seems necessary to dispute first in this work. But this is enough of a preface; now we must discuss the elements of Music themselves.

## On Voices and the Elements of Music
->C iiii.<-

**C**onsonance, which governs every
modulation of Music, is the...

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the physical properties of sound, specifically how motion and frequency determine pitch, likely following Boethius's "De institutione musica" (The Fundamentals of Music).</meta>

...does not produce a certain strike and percussion. Indeed, a strike or percussion can in no way exist unless motion has preceded it. For if all things were immobile, one thing could not run into another so that one might be struck by the other. But with all things standing still and lacking motion, it is necessary that no sound be produced. Therefore, sound is defined as a percussion of the air that remains unbroken until it reaches the sense of hearing. <note>original: "sonus perculsio aeris indissoluta usque ad auditum"</note>

Of motions, however, some are faster and others are slower. And of those motions, some are more infrequent <note>original: "rariores," referring to low frequency</note> and others are more dense <note>original: "spissiores," referring to high frequency</note>. For if one looks at a continuous motion, one must perceive therein either speed or slowness. If, indeed, someone moves their hand, they will move it with either a frequent or an infrequent motion. And if the motion is slower or more infrequent, it must produce low-pitched sounds by that very slowness and infrequency of striking. But if the motions are swift and dense, high-pitched sounds must be produced.

For this reason, if the same string is tightened further, it sounds higher; if it is loosened, it sounds lower. For the greater the tension, the faster the strike it returns; it recurs more swiftly and hits the air more frequently and densely. However, a string that is looser produces relaxed and slow strikes; these are infrequent due to the very weakness of the striking, and it does not tremble for long.

Nor should it be thought that as often as a single string is struck, only one sound is emitted, or that there is only one percussion in these instances; rather, the air is hit as many times as the trembling string strikes it. But because the speeds of the sounds are joined together, no gap is perceived by the ears. Thus, a single sound strikes the hearing—whether low or high—even though each consists of many strikes: the low sound from slower and more infrequent ones, and the high sound from swifter and dense ones.

It is just as if someone were to carefully fashion a cone—which they call a top <note>original: "turbo," a spinning top</note>—and draw upon it a single small streak of red or another color, and then spin it with as much speed as possible; then the whole cone seems to be stained with the red color. This is not because the whole thing is actually red, but because the speed overtakes the red parts and does not allow them to appear [individually]. But more on these things later.

Therefore, since higher sounds are produced by denser and swifter motions, and lower sounds by slower and infrequent ones, it is clear that by a certain addition of motions, a high pitch is stretched from a low one; whereas by a subtraction of motions, a low pitch is relaxed from a high one. For quantity already consists of a plurality of motions. In those things where a plurality of motions creates a difference, it is necessary that they consist of a certain numerical relationship. Indeed, every small amount relates to a plurality just as a number compared to a number. Of those things which are viewed according to number, some are equal to each other, and some are unequal. For this reason, sounds are also partly equal and partly distant in their inequality. But in those voices which do not disagree by any inequality, there is no consonance at all. For <margin>consonance</margin> consonance is the concord of voices dissimilar among themselves brought back into one.

### On the types of inequality. Chapter 3.

Those things which are unequal maintain the measures of inequality among themselves in five ways. For either one is surpassed by the other by a multiple; or by single parts; or by several parts; or by a multiple and a part; or by a multiple and parts. And the first kind of inequality is called **multiple**. It is a multiple when the larger number contains the smaller within itself entirely, either twice, or three times, or four times, and...

<summary>This page explains the physical basis of sound as air percussion, linking pitch to the frequency and speed of vibrations. It introduces the concept that consonance requires a numerical relationship between unequal sounds.</summary>
<keywords>acoustics, pitch, frequency, Boethius, consonance, musical ratios, percussion of air, spinning top analogy</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of mathematical ratios and their relationship to musical harmony, likely following a section on the basic definitions of equality and inequality in numbers.</meta>

...thereafter nothing is missing, and nothing is in excess. It is called either double, or triple, or quadruple, and in this order it proceeds into infinity. ¶ The second kind of inequality is that which is called **superparticular** <note>Latin: *superparticulare*. A ratio where the larger number contains the smaller number plus one part (fraction) of it, such as 3:2 or 4:3.</note>. That is, when the larger number has the smaller within itself in its entirety, plus one certain part of it. And if that part is a half, such as three to two, it is called a **sesquialter** proportion <note>The ratio 3:2, which in music forms the interval of a Perfect Fifth.</note>. Or if it is a third, such as four to three, it is called **sesquitertian** <note>The ratio 4:3, which forms the interval of a Perfect Fourth.</note>. And in this manner, even in later numbers, some part is contained by the larger over the smaller numbers. ¶ The third kind of inequality is whenever the larger number contains the whole smaller number within itself and some parts of it besides. And if it contains two parts above, it shall be called a **superbipartient** proportion, such as 5 to 3. If indeed it contains three parts above, it shall be called **supertripartient**, such as 7 to 4. And in other cases, the same similarity can exist. ¶ The fourth kind of inequality is that which is joined from the **multiple** and the **superparticular**. This is when the larger number has the smaller number within itself either twice or thrice or as many times as you like, plus one certain part of it. And if it has it twice plus its half part, it shall be called **double sesquialter**, such as 5 to 2. But if the smaller is contained twice plus its third part, it shall be called **double sesquitertian**, such as 7 to 3 <note>The OCR text says 7 to 4, but mathematically 7:3 fits the definition of 2 + 1/3.</note>. If it is contained three times plus its half part, it shall be called **triple sesquialter**, such as 7 to 2. And in the same way in other cases, the names of multiplicity and superparticularity are varied. ¶ The fifth is the kind of inequality called **multiple superpartient**, when the larger number has the smaller number within itself in its entirety more than once, and more than one certain part of it. And if the larger number contains the smaller twice, plus two parts of it besides, it shall be called **double superbipartient**, such as 8 to 3. And again, **triple superbipartient**, such as 11 to 3. And concerning these things, we now explain strictly and briefly, because we have written of them in the books on the *Fundamentals of Arithmetic* <note>original: "Arithmetica institutione". This refers to Boethius's own earlier work, *De institutione arithmetica*.</note>, where we have unraveled them more diligently. Chapter V.

## Which types of inequality are suited to consonants

<image-desc>Large blue decorative initial E with red penwork flourishes.</image-desc>
From these kinds of inequality, therefore, the last two are left aside because they are mixed from the ones above; but a study must be made of the three superior ones. The **multiple** <note>e.g., 2:1, 3:1</note> seems to hold the greater power for **consonances** <note>Musical intervals that sound "stable" or "pleasing" to the ear.</note>, and consequently the **superparticular**. The **superpartient**, however, is separated from the containment of harmony, as it seems to everyone except **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century astronomer and music theorist.</note>.

## Why multiplicity and superparticularity are assigned to consonances · Chapter VI.

<image-desc>Large red decorative initial E with blue penwork flourishes.</image-desc>
For those things are proven to agree in comparison which are simple by nature. And since unity and numbers consist in quantity, those things will especially seem to contain a sweet nature which are able to preserve the property of **discrete quantity** <note>original: "discretae quantitatis". Quantity made of distinct, separate units (like integers), as opposed to continuous quantity (like a line).</note>. For while one quantity is indeed discrete and another is **continuous**, that which is discrete is smallest in its beginning <note>i.e., the number 1</note>, but proceeds through larger things into infinity. For in this, the smallest is unity, and the same is finite. But the mode of plurality is increased into infinity by numbers which, since they begin from finite unity, have no end of growing. Conversely, that which is continuous is finite as a whole, but is diminished through the finite. For a line, which is continuous, is always diminished in infinite partition, whether its total is a foot long or any other defined measure. Wherefore number always grows together into infinity, but continuous quantity is diminished into infinity. Much...

<summary>This page categorizes mathematical ratios (multiple, superparticular, etc.) and argues that only the simplest ratios—multiples and superparticulars—are suitable for musical consonances. It also introduces the philosophical distinction between discrete quantity (numbers) and continuous quantity (geometry).</summary>
<keywords>Boethius, music theory, mathematical ratios, sesquialter, sesquitertian, superparticular, discrete quantity, continuous quantity, Ptolemy, consonances</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of mathematical proportions and their relationship to musical intervals, following the Pythagorean tradition of Boethius.</meta>

Multiplicity, therefore, since it has no end to its growth, best preserves the nature of the infinite. <term>Superparticularity</term> <note>original: "superparticularitas"; a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller plus one part of it, e.g., 3:2 or 4:3</note>, however, since it diminishes into the infinitely smaller, preserves the property of continuous quantity. For the smaller always contains the smaller plus either its half part, its third, its fourth, or its fifth. For the part named by the larger number is itself smaller. For since a third is named from three, and a fifth from five, and since five exceeds three, the fifth is diminished more clearly than the third. <term>Superpartience</term> <note>original: "superpariens"; a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller plus more than one part of it, e.g., 5:3</note>, however, already departs in a certain way from simplicity. For it has two or three or four parts in addition, and departing from simplicity, it overflows into a certain plurality of parts. Again, all multiplicity contains itself in integrity. For the double contains the whole smaller part twice; the triple likewise contains the whole smaller part three times; and the others in the same manner. Superparticularity, however, preserves nothing of the whole, but exceeds it by either a half, a third, a fourth, or a fifth. But then it effects division into single and simple parts. Superpartient inequality neither preserves the whole nor takes away single parts. And therefore, according to the <term>Pythagoreans</term> <note>Followers of Pythagoras who believed the universe was governed by numbers</note>, it is least applied to musical consonances. <term>Ptolemy</term> <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the Greco-Roman astronomer and music theorist</note>, however, places even this proportion among the consonances, as I shall show later.

### Which proportions are adapted to which musical consonances. Chapter VII.

This, however, ought to be known: that all musical consonances consist either in double, triple, quadruple, <term>sesquialter</term> <note>3:2 ratio</note>, or <term>sesquitertial</term> <note>4:3 ratio</note> proportions. And they are noted as follows: that which is in a sesquitertial ratio is a <term>diatesseron</term> <note>the interval of a perfect fourth</note> in sounds. That which is in a sesquialter ratio—

---

—is called a <term>diapente</term> <note>the interval of a perfect fifth</note> in sounds. That which is in a double proportion is a <term>diapason</term> <note>the interval of an octave</note> in consonances. The triple is a diapente and diapason combined <note>an octave plus a fifth</note>. The quadruple is a double diapason <note>two octaves</note>. And now indeed let this be said generally and without distinction; later, however, the whole logic of proportions will shine forth.

### What sound is, what an interval is, and what consonance is. Chapter VIII.

Every falling of the voice is therefore <term>emmeleis</term> <note>Greek: "in tune" or "musical"</note>, that is, fit for melody in a single tension. We do not wish to define "sound" in a general sense now, but that which in Greek is called <term>phthongos</term> <note>a musical note of definite pitch</note>, named by likeness to "logos," that is, properly of voices. An **interval** is the distance between a high and a low sound. **Consonance** is the mixture of a high and low sound reaching the ears sweetly and uniformly. **Dissonance**, however, is a harsh and unpleasant percussion of two sounds mixed together coming to the ear. For while they wish to be mixed together and each strives in a certain way to arrive whole, and one gets in the way of the other, it is transmitted unpleasantly to the sense. Not every judgment should be given to the senses, but more should be trusted to reason.

### In which the fallacy of the senses is discussed. Chapter IX.

But concerning these things we propose this: that we do not give every judgment to the senses, although every principle of this art is taken from the sense of hearing. For if there were no hearing, no discussion of voices would exist at all. But hearing holds the principle, in a way, as a substitute for an admonition. The final perfection and the power of recognition, however, consists in **reason**. Which, holding itself to certain rules, never falls into any error. For what should we say about the error of the senses, when the power of perceiving is neither the same for all, nor always equal for the same man?

<summary>This page defines the mathematical ratios (multiplicity, superparticularity, and superpartience) that govern musical intervals like the octave, fifth, and fourth. It also establishes the philosophical priority of mathematical reason over the fallible physical sense of hearing.</summary>
<keywords>musical proportions, Pythagoras, Ptolemy, consonance, dissonance, diapason, diapente, diatesseron, music theory, philosophy of sense</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of mathematical proportions in music, moving from the definitions of ratios (multiplicity and superparticularity) to the philosophical justification for using reason over physical sensation to judge harmony.</meta>

It is in vain, however, that anyone attempts to investigate what they truly desire through varied judgment. Thus, the <term>Pythagoreans</term> <note>Followers of Pythagoras, who believed the universe was governed by numerical patterns</note> travel a middle path. For they do not surrender all judgment to the ears, yet certain things are explored by them only through the ears. Indeed, they measure <term>consonances</term> <note>original: "consonantias"; musical intervals that sound pleasing when played together</note> themselves by ear. But as to the distances by which these consonances differ from one another, they no longer entrust this to the ears—whose judgments are dull—but permit it to rules and calculations. Thus, <term>sense</term> <note>original: "sensus"; physical perception</note> acts as a kind of obedient servant, while <term>reason</term> <note>original: "ratio"; mathematical calculation</note> is the judge and commander. For even though almost all the significant moments of the arts and of life itself are brought forth through the occasion of the senses, there is no certain truth in them, no comprehension of the true, if the power of judgment is absent. For the sense itself is confused by both the very large and the very small. It cannot perceive the smallest things because of the tiny size of the objects themselves, and it is often overwhelmed by the largest. Just as with voices: if they are very small, the hearing can barely capture them; if they are very large, the hearing is deafened by the very intensity of the sound.

### How Pythagoras Investigated the Proportions of Consonances

This, therefore, was the primary cause why, having set aside the judgment of the ears, Pythagoras moved toward the weight of fixed rules. He did not trust human ears, which are changed partly by nature, partly by external accidents, and partly by age itself. Nor did he devote himself to instruments, within which much variety and inconstancy often arise. For instance, if you were to look at strings: either more humid air might dull the strike, or drier air might sharpen it; or the thickness of the string might make the sound coarser, or a thinner string might make it finer, or in some way change the state of its former consistency. And since he found this reliability lacking in other instruments as well, and considering all these things to be least

<column-break/>

worthy of trust, and burning with desire, he inquired by what method he might firmly and constantly explain the weights of consonances. Therefore, by a certain divine providence, while passing a blacksmith's shop, he overheard the struck hammers echoing a single concordance from their diverse sounds. Stunned by this, he approached the work he had long sought. After considering it for a long time, he thought that the strength of those striking the hammers created the diversity of sounds. To prove this more clearly, he ordered the men to exchange hammers with one another. But the property of the sounds did not remain with the strength of the men's arms; instead, it followed the exchanged hammers. Once he noticed this, he examined the weight of the hammers. And as there were by chance five hammers, those that responded to one another in the <term>octave</term> <note>original: "diapason"; the interval spanning eight notes</note> consonance were found to be double in weight. He also discovered that the one which was double another was a <term>sesquitertian</term> <note>original: "sesquitertium"; a 4:3 ratio</note> of yet another hammer, to which it sounded a <term>fourth</term> <note>original: "diatesseron"</note>. To another, which was joined to it by the consonance of a <term>fifth</term> <note>original: "diapenthe"</note>, he found that same double of the higher hammer to be <term>sesquialter</term> <note>original: "sesquialterum"; a 3:2 ratio</note>. Those two to which the aforementioned double hammer was proven to be sesquitertian and sesquialter were found, when compared to each other, to be in a <term>sesquioctave</term> <note>original: "sesquioctaua"; a 9:8 ratio, corresponding to a whole tone</note> proportion. The fifth hammer, however, was rejected, as it was dissonant with all the others. Therefore, although before Pythagoras musical consonances were called partly octave, partly fifth, and partly fourth (which is the smallest consonance), Pythagoras was the first to discover by this method the proportion by which this concord of sounds was joined together. And to make what has been said clearer, let there be, for example, four weights of hammers contained in the numbers written below: **xii. viii. viiii. vi.** <note>12, 8, 9, 6</note> Therefore, these hammers which were of weights 12 and 6...

<summary>This text explains why Pythagoras rejected the unreliable human ear in favor of mathematical ratios to define musical intervals, recounting the famous legend of how he discovered these proportions by weighing blacksmiths' hammers.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, music theory, consonance, ratio, blacksmith's hammers, octave, fifth, fourth, mathematical reason, Boethius</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Pythagoras discovering the mathematical foundations of music through the weights of blacksmiths' hammers, then transitioning into his systematic testing of these ratios and the classification of the human voice.</meta>

...they joined. The <term>diapason</term> <note>original: "diapason"; the octave interval (2:1 ratio)</note> sounded in a double ratio. Truly, a hammer of 12 weights compared to a hammer of 8, and a hammer of 8 weights compared to a hammer of 6, joined the <term>diatessaron</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"; the interval of a fourth (4:3 ratio)</note> consonance according to the <term>epitrite</term> <note>original: "epitrita"; the ratio 4:3</note> proportion. But the 8-weight hammer compared to the 6, and <margin>8</margin> the 12 to the 9, mixed together the <term>diapente</term> <note>original: "diapente"; the interval of a fifth (3:2 ratio)</note> consonance. Finally, the 8-weight hammer compared to the 9 sounded a <term>tone</term> <note>original: "tonum"; the fundamental interval of a major second</note> in a <term>sesquioctave</term> <note>original: "sesquioctava"; the ratio 9:8</note> proportion.

### Chapter XI: In what ways the proportions of consonances were variously weighed by Pythagoras

Returning home from there, he weighed through various examinations whether the whole system of harmonies consisted entirely in these proportions. First, he fitted equal weights to strings, judging their consonances by ear. Then, in the length of pipes, he restored the doubling and halving of the length and fitted the other proportions, gaining the most complete trust through diverse experiments. Often, he would pour liquid into four jars of equal weight according to the measure of the proportions; often, he struck these same jars, formed of different weights, with a copper rod, and was delighted to find he had discovered nothing different. Driven by this, he also set out to examine the length and thickness of strings. Thus, he discovered the <term>rule</term> <note>original: "regulam"; here referring to the monochord, a single-stringed instrument used for measuring musical intervals</note> of which we shall speak later, which took its name from the thing itself. Not that it is merely a wooden rule by which we measure the sizes of bodies or sounds, but because such an inspection is a fixed and firm "rule," so that it might never deceive any inquirer with a doubtful judgment.

### Chapter XII: On the division of voices and their explanation

But enough of these things for now. Now let us collect the

<column-break/>

differences of the voice. Every voice is either **SYNECHE** <note>original: "CYNEXHE"; continuous</note>, which is sustained, or **DIASTEMATIKE** <note>original: "DIACRHEMATIKE"; intervallic</note>, which is suspended by intervals. Indeed, the continuous voice is that which we run through when speaking or reading prose. For then the voice hastens not to linger on high or low sounds, but runs through the words as quickly as possible; the impulse of the continuous voice works to facilitate the senses and express speech. On the other hand, the **DIASTEMATIKE** is that which we sustain in singing. In this, we serve not so much the words as the sounds. The voice itself is slower, and by modulating through varieties, it creates a certain interval—not a path of silence, but rather of a suspended and slow song. To these, as <term>Albinus</term> <note>A 2nd-century philosopher who wrote on music and geometry</note> asserts, a third difference is added which can include intermediate voices: namely, when we read heroic poems, neither in a continuous prose run, nor in the suspended and slower manner of singing.

### Chapter XIII: That human nature limits the infinity of voices

But as for the continuous voice and that which we use for singing, they are by nature infinite. For if one considers it, there is no set limit for drawing out speech, nor for lifting the voice to high pitches or relaxing it in volume. But human nature has established a proper limit for both. For human breath has made a boundary for the continuous voice, beyond which it cannot pass by any means. For each person speaks continuously only as long as their natural breath allows. Likewise, the nature of men makes a limit for the **DIASTEMATIKE** voice, which determines their high and low sounds. For each person is only able to raise or lower the <unclear>peak</unclear> <note>original: "p'acimen"; likely *acumen* (sharpness/height)</note> of their volume as much as the natural measure of their voice permits.

<summary>This page describes Pythagoras's experiments with different physical objects to prove that musical intervals are governed by fixed mathematical ratios, and introduces a classification of the human voice into continuous (speech) and intervallic (song) types.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, musical ratios, diapason, diatessaron, diapente, monochord, syneche, diastematike, Albinus, human voice, acoustics</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues Boethius’s exploration of musical science, moving from the history of Pythagoras's discovery of ratios to the physical mechanism of hearing and the specific mathematical definitions of musical intervals like the octave, fifth, and fourth.</meta>

### In what way hearing occurs. Chapter 14.

Now let us discuss the manner in which hearing takes place. A phenomenon occurs in the air similar to what happens in ponds when a stone, thrown from a distance, sinks into marshes or quiet waters. First, the stone gathers the water into a very small circle. Then, it spreads the waves into larger spheres, and so on, until the motion, exhausted by the surging waves, comes to rest. The later and larger wave is always spread by a weaker pulse. But if there should be something that can block the spreading waves, that motion immediately returns and is rounded back toward the center from which it started, following the same patterns. In this same way, then, when air is struck, it creates a sound: it pushes the next bit of air and excites a somewhat rounded wave of air. Thus, the sound is spread and strikes the hearing of all those standing around. The voice is more obscure to the person who stands further away, because the wave of air reaches them with a weaker pulse.

### On the order of the theories, that is, the speculations. Chapter 15.
<margin>
<gloss>Diatonic</gloss> <note>original: "i dia"</note>
<gloss>Common</gloss> <note>original: "commum"</note>
<gloss>Chromatic</gloss> <note>original: "chromati"</note>
<gloss>or Enharmonic</gloss> <note>original: "l' armoniam"</note>
</margin>
These things having been proposed, it seems necessary to say by what means and through which elements every song is woven. These are the matters considered by the discipline of discovering harmony <note>original: "armoniae"</note>. These elements are the **Diatonic**, the **Chromatic**, and the **Enharmonic** <note>These represent the three "genera" or types of musical scales used in ancient Greek music</note>. We must explain these only after we have discussed the strings <note>likely referring to the strings of the lyre or monochord</note> and how the number of strings increased to the plurality that exists now. This will be done if we first enumerate the proportions by which musical consonances <note>original: "symphoniae"</note> are mixed.

### On consonances, the tone, and the semitone. Chapter 16.
The **octave** <note>original: "Diapason"</note> is the consonance that is created in a double ratio <note>2:1</note>, as is shown here. The **fifth** <note>original: "Diapenthe"</note>, truly, is that which consists of these numbers <note>3:2</note>. The **fourth** <note>original: "Diatesseron"</note>, truly, is...

The **tone**, truly, revolves in this **sesquioctave** <note>9:8</note> ratio, but in this ratio there is not yet a consonance. The **octave**, however, is composed of the **fifth** and the **fourth** <note>The text likely meant to say that adding a fifth (3:2) and a fourth (4:3) results in an octave (2:1). The transcription "tripla opacit" is likely a scribal error for the math of the fourth</note> arranged in this way: the **double octave** <note>original: "bis diapason"</note> is completed by a quadruple comparison <note>4:1</note>. The **fourth** and the **fifth** together complete one **octave** in this way:

| **Triple** <note>3:1 ratio</note> | **Quadruple** <note>4:1 ratio</note> | **Double** <note>2:1 ratio</note> |

**Octave** | **Fifth** | **Fourth** | **Sesquialter** | **Sesquitertial**

For if one voice is twice as high or low as another, an **octave** consonance is made. If a voice is higher or lower than another by a **sesquialter** proportion <note>3:2</note>, or a **sesquitertial** <note>4:3</note>, or a **sesquioctave** <note>9:8</note>, it would return a **fifth**, a **fourth**, or a **tone** consonance. Likewise, if an octave (as 2 and 4) and a fifth (as 6 and 4) are joined: the **triple** ratio <note>3:1</note>, which is a **fifth and an octave** together, creates a consonance. But if a **double octave** is made (as 2 to 4 and 4 to 8), a consonance is made which is the double octave. But if a sesquialter and a sesquitertial—that is, a **fifth** and a **fourth**—are joined (as 2 to 3 and 3 to 4): a **double** ratio, which is...

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration of musical intervals. Having previously discussed the history and basic ratios, Boethius now illustrates how the octave is composed of a fourth and a fifth, and begins a rigorous proof that a whole tone cannot be divided into two equal halves.</meta>

...the **octave** <note>original: "diapason"</note> consonance is surely born. For four to three holds a **sesquitertial** <term>sesquitertia: a ratio of 4:3, where the larger number contains the smaller plus one-third of it</term> proportion. However, three is joined to two in a **sesquialter** <term>sesqualtera: a ratio of 3:2, where the larger number contains the smaller plus one-half of it</term> comparison. And therefore, the number four compared to two is coupled by a **double** <note>original: "dupla," a 2:1 ratio</note> comparison. But the sesquitertial ratio creates the **fourth** <note>original: "diatesseron"</note> consonance; the sesquialter creates the **fifth** <note>original: "diapente"</note> consonance. The double ratio, in turn, effects the **octave** <note>original: "diapason"</note> symphony. Therefore, the fourth and the fifth together join to form one octave consonance.

Again, a **tone** <note>In this context, a "whole tone" or major second</note> cannot be divided into equal parts. Why this is so will be clear later. For now, it suffices to know only this: that a tone is never divided into two equal parts. And so that this may be most easily proved, let there be a **sesquioctave** <term>sesquioctava: a ratio of 9:8, which mathematically defines a whole tone in Pythagorean tuning</term> proportion between 8 and 9. Between these, no natural middle number falls. Therefore, let us multiply these by two: twice 8 becomes 16, and twice 9 becomes 18. Between 16 and 18, one number falls naturally, which is 17. Let these be arranged in the order: 16, 17, 18. 

Therefore, 16 and 18, when compared, retain the sesquioctave proportion, and therefore the tone. But the middle number, 17, does not divide this proportion into equal parts. For when 17 is compared to 16, it contains the whole of 16 and its sixteenth part (namely, the value of one). But if the third number, which is 18, is compared to the number 17, it contains the whole of 17 and its seventeenth part. Therefore, it does not exceed the smaller number by the same parts as it is exceeded by the larger number. The seventeenth part is smaller, while the sixteenth part is larger. Yet both are called **semitones**; not because semitones are entirely equal halves, but because the prefix "semi-" is usually used for that which does not 

<column-break/>

reach full integrity <note>Boethius argues that "semitone" means "incomplete tone" rather than "exactly half a tone."</note>. But between these, one is called a **major semitone** and the other a **minor semitone**.

->**In which smallest numbers the semitone consists. Chapter 17.**<-

What a whole semitone is, or from which first smallest numbers it consists, is now more clearly explained. For what was said regarding the division of the tone does not pertain to showing the types of semitones, but rather to the fact that a tone cannot be separated into two equal divisions. The **fourth** <note>original: "diatesseron"</note>, which is a consonance of voices, encompasses four notes and three intervals. Moreover, it consists of two tones and an incomplete semitone <note>specifically the "minor semitone" or "limma"</note>. 

Let the following description be set down: **192, 216, 243, 256**. If the number 192 is compared to 256, a sesquitertial [4:3] proportion is made, and it will sound as a **fourth** consonance. But if 216 is compared to 192, it is a sesquioctave [9:8] proportion. For their difference is 24, which is the eighth part of 192. It is, therefore, a **tone**. Again, if 243 is compared to 216, it will be another sesquioctave [9:8] proportion. For their difference, 27, is proven to be the eighth part of 216. 

There remains the space from 243 to 256, whose difference is 13; this, when multiplied by eight, does not seem to fulfill the half of 243. Therefore, it is not a [half] semitone, but less than a semitone. For a semitone would be thought of as a whole half if their difference, which is 13, multiplied eight times, could have equaled half of the number 243. Therefore, the comparison of 243 to 256 is the **minor semitone**.

->**Chapter 18.**<-

<margin>ccvii</margin>
<margin>
The fourth differs from the fifth by a tone.
Again, the fifth consonance of voices...
</margin>

<summary>Boethius mathematically demonstrates that an octave is composed of a fourth and a fifth, and proves that a whole tone (9:8 ratio) cannot be split into two equal halves, leading to the distinction between major and minor semitones.</summary>
<keywords>Boethius, music theory, octave, fifth, fourth, whole tone, semitone, mathematical ratios, Pythagorean tuning, sesquioctave, sesquitertia</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration of musical intervals, specifically transitioning from the division of the fourth to the construction of the fifth and the full octave. It then shifts from mathematical proofs to the historical and mythological origins of the musical scale, citing the addition of strings to the lyre.</meta>

Indeed, it is composed of five intervals, consisting of four of these tones and a minor semitone. <note>original: "semitonij minoris"; in Pythagorean tuning, this is the limma, which is slightly smaller than a true half-tone.</note> For let the number 192 be set down and its **sesquialter** <term>sesquialtera: a 3:2 ratio, representing a musical fifth</term> be taken, which creates a **fifth** <note>original: "diapente"</note> consonance compared to it. Thus, the number is 288. Therefore, let these and the numbers found above, 192, have these numbers placed in the middle: 216, 243, 256; <margin>248-256</margin> <margin>288</margin> 288. In the above description, 192 and 256 were shown to contain two tones and a semitone. It remains, therefore, to be seen that 256 compared to 288 is a **sesquioctave** <term>sesquioctava: a 9:8 ratio, the mathematical definition of a whole tone</term>, which is a tone. Their difference is 32, <margin><image-desc>A small hand (manicule) pointing right toward the text.</image-desc></margin> which is the eighth part of 256. Thus, it has been shown that the **fifth** <note>original: "diapente"</note> consonance consists of three tones and a semitone. But whereas previously the **fourth** <note>original: "diatesseron"</note> consonance had reached from the number 192 up to 256, now indeed the **fifth** is extended from the same number 192 up to 288. Therefore, the **fourth** consonance is exceeded by the **fifth** by the proportion that is contained between the numbers 256 and 288. And this is a tone. Therefore, the **fourth** symphony is surpassed by the **fifth** by a whole tone.

### That the Octave is Joined by Five Tones and Two Semitones. 119.

The **octave** <note>original: "diapason"</note> consonance consists of five tones and two semitones, which, however, do not quite fill one whole tone. For since it has been shown that the octave consists of a fourth and a fifth, and the fourth has been proven to consist of two tones and a semitone, while the fifth consists of three tones and a semitone, joined together they produce five tones. But because those two semitones were not of a full half-measure, their joining does not reach a full whole [tone], but rather exceeds a half-measure while falling short of wholeness. And the octave is, according to this reasoning,

<column-break/>

composed of five tones and two semitones, which, just as they do not reach a full tone, so they proceed beyond a single semitone. But because there is a reason or method by which the musical consonances themselves are perceived, it will be explained more clearly later. Meanwhile, for the present discussion, belief should be applied with a moderate understanding. One must proceed with caution regarding the highest faith in these matters until it has shone through its own proper and clear demonstration. With these things thus arranged, let us discuss for a little while the strings of the **cithara** <note>A professional version of the lyre, often associated with Apollo</note> and their names, in what way they were added, and the reason for their names. For once these first come to our knowledge, it will be easy to embrace the science that follows.

### Concerning the Addition of Strings and Their Names. 120.

Nicomachus <note>A Pythagorean mathematician and music theorist (c. 60–120 AD)</note> reports that music was simple in the beginning, so much so that the whole of it consisted of only four strings. This lasted until the time of Orpheus, so that the first, fifth, and fourth strings sounded the octave consonance; the middle strings were tuned to each other and to the extremes as a tone, a fourth, and a fifth, so that nothing in them was out of consonance, namely in imitation of the World, which consists of four elements. Of this **four-stringed instrument** <note>original: "quadrichordi"</note> Mercury is said to be the inventor. Indeed, Chorebus, the son of Atys, added a fifth string; then Hyagnis added a sixth to these. But the seventh string was added by Terpander of Lesbos <note>A famous Greek poet and musician of the 7th century BC</note> according to the likeness of the seven planets. And among all these, the string which was the lowest in pitch was called **hypate** <note>original: "hypate"; Greek for "highest." To the Greeks, this meant the "highest" in rank or the "highest" on the instrument's frame, though it produced the lowest sound.</note>, as if it were the greatest and most honorable. Whence they even call Jupiter *Hypatos*. They also name the Consul by the same name because of the excellence of his dignity. And the Latins...

<summary>This page mathematically demonstrates that a musical fifth is composed of three tones and a semitone, and explains that an octave consists of five tones and two "minor" semitones. It then transitions into the history of the lyre, crediting Mercury with the original four-stringed design and explaining how strings were added to reach seven, mirroring the seven planets.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical scales, moving from the mathematical ratios of intervals to the historical nomenclature and physical arrangement of the strings on the lyre. It specifically explains the transition from the seven-stringed heptachord to the eight-stringed octachord.</meta>

...was attributed because of the slowness of motion and the gravity of the sound <note>In ancient music theory, the "lowest" notes were often associated with slower vibrations or "heavier" sound.</note>. **Parhypate** indeed is the second, as if placed and located next to the *hypate*. **Licanos** is the third, so-called because *licanos* is the name of the finger we call the "index." Among the Greeks, it is called *licanon* from the word for "licking" <note>original: "a lingundo"; a common medieval etymology for the index finger, used for tasting</note>. And because in singing, the index finger—which is the *licanos*—was found at that string which was the third from the *hypate*, for that reason it was called *licanos*. The fourth is called **mese** because it is always the middle among seven. The fifth is **paramese**, as if located next to the *mese*. But the seventh is called **nete**, as if *neate*, that is, the lowest <note>original: "inferior"; though this string produced the highest pitch, it was physically the "lowest" or furthest string on the instrument.</note>. Between this *nete* and *paramese* is the sixth, which is called **paranete**, as if located next to the *nete*. But since *paramese* is the third from the *nete*, it is also notably called **trite**, that is, the "third." This is the description:

| **The Heptachord (Seven-Stringed Scale)** |
| :--- |
| **nete** (highest pitch) |
| **paranete** |
| **paramese** or **trite** |
| **mese** (the middle) |
| **lychanos** |
| **parhypate** |
| **hypate** (lowest pitch) |

**H**is eighth string was added by Lycaon of Samos, and he fitted this middle string between the **paramese** (which is also called **trite**) and the **paranete**, so that it itself would be the third from the *nete*. Consequently, the *paramese* was the only one called by that name, as it was placed after the middle. It lost the name of *trite*, however, because the string located between it and the *paranete* was the third from the *nete*, and it rightfully took the name of **trite** <note>trite simply means "third"</note>. Thus, the eight-stringed scale <term>octocordum: an eight-stringed scale</term> according to the addition of Lycaon is arranged in this way:

<column-break/>

| **The Octachord (Eight-Stringed Scale)** |
| :--- |
| **nete** |
| **paranete** |
| **trite** |
| **paramese** |
| **mese** |
| **lichanos** |
| **parhypate** |
| **hypate** |

**IN** the two previous arrangements, therefore, are the **heptachord** and the **octachord**. The heptachord is called **synemmenon**, which means "conjoined." The octachord, however, is **diezeugmenon**, which means "disjoined." For in the heptachord there is one tetrachord <term>tetrachord: a scale of four notes spanning a perfect fourth</term> consisting of: *hypate*, *parhypate*, *licanos*, and *mese*. Another tetrachord consists of: *mese*, *paramese*, *paranete*, and *nete*. Because we count the *mese* twice <note>original: "tertioe sedo numeramus"; it acts as the top of the first group and the bottom of the second</note>, the two tetrachords are joined together through the *mese*. 

In the octachord, however, since there are eight strings, the upper four—that is, *hypate*, *parhypate*, *lycanos*, and *mese*—complete one tetrachord. From this, a separate and complete one begins at the *paramese*, proceeds through the *trite* and *paranete*, and ends at the *nete*. This separation is called **diezeuxis**, which is the distance of a whole tone between the *mese* and *paramese*. Here, therefore, the *mese* retains its name only by tradition. It is no longer in the middle position, because in an octachord two strings are always found in the middle, but a single middle string cannot be found.

**P**rophastus Periotes, however, added one string to the lower part <note>original: "graviorum partem"; the part of the scale with the lowest/gravest pitches</note> to create a nine-stringed scale <term>enneachordum: a nine-stringed scale</term>. Because this is above the *hypate*, it is called **hyperhypate**. Previously, when the lyre had only eight strings, it was called *hypatehypate*...

Timotheus of Miletus, however, added the eleventh string. Those strings which were added above the **hypate** and **parhypate** are called the **hypate hypaton** <note>original: "hypate hypaton"; literally "the principal of the principals," representing the lowest group of notes</note>, as if they were the greatest of the great, the deepest of the deep, or the most excellent of the excellent. The first among the eleven is called **hypate hypaton**. The second is **parhypate hypaton**, since it is located next to the *hypate hypaton*. The third, which was previously called **hyphypate** in the nine-stringed scale, is now named **lichanos hypaton**. The fourth held the ancient name **hypate**. The fifth is **parhypate**. The sixth is **lichanos**, keeping its ancient name. The seventh is the **mese** <note>original: "mese"; the "middle" note</note>. The eighth is the **paramese**. The ninth is the **trite**. The tenth is the **paranete**. The eleventh is the **nete**.

Therefore, one four-note scale <term>tetrachord</term> consists of the **hypate hypaton**, **parhypate hypaton**, **lichanos hypaton**, and **hypate**. Another is the **hypate**, **parhypate**, **lichanos**, and **mese**. These two are conjoined <note>They are "conjoined" because they share a common note, the Hypate, to link them together.</note>. The third is the **paramese**, **trite**, **para-**

<column-break/>

**-nete**, and **nete**. But since between the higher tetrachord (which is **hypate hypaton**, **parhypate hypaton**, **lichanos hypaton**, and **hypate**) and the lowest one (which is **paramese**, **trite**, **paranete**, and **nete**) there is a middle tetrachord located in between (which is **hypate**, **parhypate**, **lichanos**, and **mese**), this whole middle tetrachord is called the **meson** <note>The manuscript uses "melon," likely a scribal error for the Greek "meson," meaning middle.</note> with the addition of the name: **hypate meson**, **parhypate meson**, **lichanos meson**, and **mese**. 

However, because there is a separation between this middle tetrachord and the lower one (which is the tetrachord of the *netas*), specifically between the **mese** and the **paramese**, every such tetrachord that is separated is called **diezeugmenon** <term>diezeugmenon</term> <note>original: "diezeugmenon"; a Greek term meaning "disjointed" or "separated" by a whole tone gap</note>. This is noted with the addition of the names: **paramese diezeugmenon**, **trite diezeugmenon**, **paranete diezeugmenon**, and **nete diezeugmenon**. The description is in this manner:

**B**ut since in this or the aforementioned eleven-stringed <term>endecachord</term> arrangement, the **middle note** <note>original: "mese"; so named because of its central position in the scale</note> comes too close to the **highest note** <note>original: "nete"</note> and stands too far apart from the lowest **principal notes** <note>original: "hypatis"</note>, it does not retain its proper central place. Therefore, another single four-note scale <term>tetrachord</term> was joined above the "highest of the disjoined notes" <note>original: "nete diezeugmenon"</note>. **B**ut because, once again, the **middle note** <note>original: "mese"</note> was not in a central position but happened to be closer to the lower **principal notes** <note>original: "hypatis"</note>, a single string was added below the **principal of the principals**, which is called the **acquired note** <note>original: "proslambanomenos"</note>. By some, however, it is called the **additional melody note** <note>original: "prosmelodos"</note>, standing a full <term>tone</term> apart from that which is the **principal of the principals**.

And this "acquired note" itself sounds an octave <note>original: "diapason"; the first and most perfect consonance</note> higher at the **middle note**. This same **additional melody note** sounds a fourth <note>original: "diatesseron"</note> at the **index-finger of the principals**. That **index-finger of the principals** <note>original: "licanos hypaton"</note> sounds a fifth <note>original: "diapente"</note> at the **middle note**, which is the fifth note from it.

Again, the **middle note** stands a tone apart from the **next-to-middle note** <note>original: "paramese"</note>. This same **middle note** creates a fifth <note>original: "diapenthe"</note> consonance at the **highest of the disjoined notes** <note>original: "neten diezeugmenon"</note>, and an octave <note>original: "diapason"</note> at the **highest of the extreme notes** <note>original: "neten hyperboleos"</note>. The **highest of the disjoined notes** creates a fourth <note>original: "diatesseron"</note> consonance at the **highest of the extreme notes**. Finally, the **acquired note** <note>original: "proslambanomenos"</note> at the bottom renders a double octave <note>original: "bisdiapason"</note> consonance with the **highest of the extreme notes** at the top, as shown in the following figure:

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical theory, likely from Boethius’s "Fundamentals of Music," moving from general principles to the specific classification of the three melodic genera.</meta>

### On the Kinds of Song, Chapter 21.

Having explained these matters, we must now speak of the kinds of melodies. There are three: the **Diatonic**, the **Chromatic**, and the **Enharmonic**. The **Diatonic** is somewhat harder and more natural; the **Chromatic**, however, departs from that natural tension and falls into a softer sound. The **Enharmonic** is very well and fittingly joined. Since there are five <term>tetrachords</term> <note>a scale of four notes spanning the interval of a fourth</note>—the *hypaton* <note>the "lowest" or principal notes</note>, the *meson* <note>the middle notes</note>, the *synemmenon* <note>the "conjunct" or connected notes</note>, the *diezeugmenon* <note>the "disjunct" or separated notes</note>, and the *hyperboleon* <note>the "highest" or supreme notes</note>—in all of these, according to the **Diatonic** genus, the voice produces song through a semitone, a tone, and a tone in one tetrachord, and then in another through a semitone, a tone, and a tone, and so on. Therefore, it is called "Diatonic," as if it proceeds "through tones" <note>original: "diatoni quasi quod per tonum ac tonum progrediatur"</note>. 

The **Chromatic**, which is named for "color" <note>original: "Chroma quod dicitur color"</note>, is like a first alteration from this natural tension; it is sung through a semitone, another semitone, and three semitones <note>an interval equal to a minor third</note>. For the whole <term>diatessaron</term> <note>original: "diatesseron"; the interval of a perfect fourth</note> consonance consists of two tones and one semitone (though not a full one). This name "Chroma" is derived from the way surfaces change their appearance when they pass into another color. 

The **Enharmonic**, however, is that which is most closely fitted together. In all tetrachords, it consists 

<column-break/>

of a <term>diesis</term>, another <term>diesis</term>, and a <term>ditone</term> <note>a "double tone" or major third</note>. A **diesis** is half of a semitone <note>effectively a quarter-tone</note>, so that the description of the three genera running through all the tetrachords may be seen here in this manner.

### On the Order of the Strings and Their Names in the Three Genera, Chapter 22.

Now, therefore, we must arrange the order of all the strings, which vary across these many genera. They are arranged in a constant order. The first is the **proslambanomenos** <note>original: "proslabanomenos"; the "added note" at the bottom of the scale</note>, which is also called the *prosmelos*. The second is the **hypate hypaton** <note>the lowest of the lowest tetrachord</note>; the third is the **parhypate hypaton** <note>the note next to the lowest</note>. 

The fourth is universally called the **lichanos** <note>the "index finger" string</note>, but if it is fitted <gloss>arranged</gloss> to the **Diatonic** genus, it is called the "Diatonic lichanos hypaton." If, however, it is in the **Chromatic**, it is called <gloss>lichanos</gloss> the "Chromatic lichanos hypaton." If it is in the **Enharmonic**, it is called the "Enharmonic lichanos hypaton." 

After this is called the **hypate meson** <note>lowest of the middle tetrachord</note>; then the **parhypate meson**. From there, the <gloss>lichanos</gloss> is called the "Diatonic meson" (simply in the Diatonic genus), or the "Chromatic meson" in the Chromatic. In the Enharmonic, it is the "Enharmonic Diatonic meson" or "Enharmonic lichanos meson." These are followed by the **mese** <note>the "middle" note, the center of the Greek tonal system</note>. After this, there are two tetrachords, partly...

***

| DIATONIC | CHROMATIC | ENHARMONIC |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| semitone | semitone | diesis |
| tone | semitone | diesis |
| **TONE** | **three semitones** | **DITONE** |
| (Fourth) | (Fourth) | (Fourth) |

***

<meta>This page continues the technical naming of strings and notes within the Greek Greater Perfect System, specifically detailing how the names change across the three musical genera (Diatonic, Chromatic, and Enharmonic) and across the "Conjunct" (Synemmenon) and "Disjunct" (Diezeugmenon) tetrachords.</meta>

...the <term>Synemmenon</term><note>original: "smemenon," referring to the "conjunct" or connected tetrachord</note> part and the <term>Diezeugmenon</term><note>the "disjunct" or separated tetrachord</note>. And the <term>Synemmenon</term> is that which is placed after the <term>Mese</term><note>the central or "middle" note of the ancient Greek scale</note>, that is:
<margin>the <term>Trite</term> and <term>Paranete</term> of the <term>Synemmenon</term></margin>
the <term>Trite Synemmenon</term><note>literally "the third string of the conjunct group"</note>. Next is the <term>Licanos Synemmenon</term><note>also called the <term>Paranete</term>; "Licanos" refers to the index or "licking" finger used to play the string</note>; this is the same in the <term>Diatonic</term> genus: the <term>Diatonos Synemmenon</term>. In the <term>Chromatic</term> genus, however, it is the <term>Licanos Synemmenon Chromatic</term>. In the <term>Enharmonic</term> genus, it is either the <term>Diatonos Synemmenon Enharmonic</term> or the <term>Licanos Synemmenon Enharmonic</term>. After these comes the <term>Nete Synemmenon</term><note>the "last" or highest note of that group</note>. But if the <term>Mese</term> note joins two together, it admits the <term>Synemmenon</term> tetrachord. However, after the <term>Mese</term> [in the disjunct system] is the <term>Paramese</term><note>literally "next to the middle"</note>. Next is the <term>Trite Diezeugmenon</term>, which in the <term>Diatonic</term> is the <term>Diatonos Diezeugmenon</term>; in the <term>Chromatic</term>, the <term>Diatonos Diezeugmenon Chromatic</term> and the <term>Licanos Diezeugmenon Chromatic</term>; and in the <term>Enharmonic</term>, the <term>Diatonos Diezeugmenon Enharmonic</term> and the <term>Licanos Diezeugmenon Enharmonic</term>. This is also the <term>Paranete</term>, with the addition of either the Diatonic, Chromatic, or Enharmonic quality. Above these is the

<column-break/>

<term>Nete Diezeugmenon</term> and the <term>Trite Hyperbolaion</term><note>the "third" string of the "extreme" or highest group</note>. And that which is the <term>Paranete Hyperbolaion</term> is the same in the <term>Diatonic</term>: the <term>Diatonos Hyperbolaion</term>; in the <term>Chromatic</term>: the <term>Chromatic Hyperbolaion</term>; and in the <term>Enharmonic</term>: the <term>Enharmonic Hyperbolaion</term>. The last of these is the <term>Nete Hyperbolaion</term>. Let there be a diagram of this kind so that it contains the arrangement of the three genera. In these, you will note both the similarity of the names and their differences, so that if the similar strings in all cases are joined with those that are different, there are twenty-eight in all. The following diagram shows this.

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical intervals and the structural arrangement of the Greek Greater Perfect System, specifically defining how tetrachords (four-note scales) are linked together or separated.</meta>

It is called "uncomposite" <note>original: *incompositus*; meaning a single, undivided musical leap rather than one made of several smaller steps</note> because it is set down as a whole, and no other interval is joined to it; rather, the whole [tone] is placed in individual intervals. In the <term>Chromatic</term> genus <note>one of the three ancient "genera" or styles of tuning, known for its use of semitones</note>, however, the division is placed in a semitone, a semitone, and an uncomposite <term>trihemitone</term> <note>original: *triemitonum*; an interval of three semitones, similar to a minor third</note>. Therefore, we call this trihemitone "uncomposite" because it is situated within a single interval. For an interval of three semitones can be named in the <term>Diatonic</term> genus when there is a semitone and a tone, but there it is not "uncomposite," for it is completed by two distinct intervals. The same logic applies in the <term>Enharmonic</term> genus. For it consists of a <term>diesis</term> <note>a very small interval, roughly a quarter-tone</note>, another <term>diesis</term>, and an uncomposite <term>ditone</term> <note>an interval of two full tones, similar to a major third</note>, which we call "uncomposite" for the same reason: because it is situated in a single interval.

### What Conjunction (Sinaphe) Is. Chapter 24.

With these <term>tetrachords</term> <note>the basic building blocks of Greek music, consisting of four notes spanning a perfect fourth</note> thus arranged and established, <term>sinaphe</term> <note>Greek for "connection" or "conjunction"</note> is what we can call "conjunction" in Latin. This occurs whenever one note continues and joins the end of two tetrachords, as seen in this group of tetrachords:

->Lowest of the low<-
->Next-to-lowest of the low<-
->Index-string of the low<-
->Lowest of the middle<-
->Next-to-lowest of the middle<-
->Index-string of the middle<-
->Middle<-

Here, therefore, is one tetrachord: the <term>hypate</term>, <term>parhypate</term>, <term>licanos</term>, and <term>hypatemeson</term>; and another: the <term>hypatemeson</term>, <term>parhypatemeson</term>, <term>licanosmeson</term>, and <term>mese</term>. In both tetrachords, the note <term>hypatemeson</term> <note>literally "the lowest of the middle strings"</note> is counted; it is the highest note of the first tetrachord and the lowest note of the second. This conjunction is one and the same string: the <term>hypatemeson</term>.

<column-break/>

This string joins two tetrachords, just as it joined the <term>hypaton</term> <note>the "low" group</note> and <term>meson</term> <note>the "middle" group</note> tetrachords in the description above. Therefore, <term>sinaphe</term> (which is also called <term>synemmenon</term>) is said to be the conjunction of two tetrachords by a shared middle note—the highest of the former and the lowest of the latter.

### What Disjunction (Diezeuxis) Is. Chapter 25.

<term>Diezeuxis</term> <note>Greek for "separation"</note>, however, is the name for what can be called "disjunction," which occurs whenever two tetrachords are separated by the space of a full tone, as in these two tetrachords:

<margin>Tulis <note>possibly a name or a reference mark</note></margin>
->Lowest of the middle<-
->Next-to-lowest of the middle<-
->Index-string of the middle<-
->Middle<-
---
->Next-to-the-middle<-
->Third of the disjunct<-
->Next-to-last of the disjunct<-
->Last of the disjunct<-

It evidently appears, therefore, that there are two separate tetrachords, since there are eight distinct strings. <term>Diezeuxis</term>, or disjunction, occurs between the <term>mese</term> <note>the "Middle" note</note> and the <term>paramese</term> <note>the note "Next-to-the-Middle"</note>, which differ from each other by a full tone. These will be explained more clearly when a later treatise takes up each one to be explained more studiously. But to one looking more carefully, no more than five tetrachords are found: the <term>hypaton</term> (lowest), <term>meson</term> (middle), <term>synemmenon</term> (conjunct), <term>diezeugmenon</term> (disjunct), and <term>hyperboleon</term> (highest).

### In What Ways Albinus Named the Strings. Chapter 26.

Albinus <note>likely Albinus the Platonic philosopher, who wrote on music and geometry in the 2nd century AD</note>, in his Latin speech regarding their names, mentioned them thus: he called the *hypatae* "principals" (*principales*); the *mesae* "middles" (*medias*); the *synemmenas* "conjunct" (*coniunctas*); the *diezeugmenas* "disjunct" (*disiunctas*); and the *hyperboleas* "excellent" or "highest" (*excellentes*). But to us in a foreign...
