### Which strings are compared to which stars. Chapter [27].
### That much, meanwhile, needs to be added to the previous [discussion] of the twenty-seventh [chapter].

It seems something must be added to the tetrachords <note>the four-note scales that form the basis of the Greek system</note>, because from the *hypate melon* <note>original: *hypate melon*; the principal note of the "middle" tetrachord</note> up to the *nete* <note>original: *nete*; the last or highest-pitched note of the system</note>, there is, as it were, a certain celestial model in its order and distinction. For the *hypate melon* is attributed to Saturn. The *parhypate* <note>the note "next to" the hypate</note>, however, is similar to the circle of Jupiter. They have assigned the *lichanos melon* <note>the "forefinger" note of the middle tetrachord</note> to Mars. The Sun occupies the *mese* <note>the "middle" note, often considered the most important</note>. Venus holds the *trite synemmenon* <note>the third note of the "conjunct" tetrachord</note>. Mercury rules the *paramese synemmenon* <note>the note next to the middle in the conjunct system</note>. Moreover, the *nete* holds the model of the lunar circle. 

But Marcus <margin>Cicero</margin> Tullius Cicero <note>original: *M. Tullius*; the Roman statesman and philosopher</note> makes the order contrary. For in the sixth book of *The Republic* <note>specifically from the "Dream of Scipio," a famous text on the harmony of the spheres</note>, he says thus: "And nature brings it about that the extremes sound low on one side and high on the other. Wherefore that highest starry course of heaven, whose revolution is more rapid, is moved by a high and excited sound; while this lunar and lowest one [is moved] by the deepest." For the Earth, the ninth [sphere], remaining immobile, always clings to one seat. 

Here, therefore, Tully [Cicero] places the Earth as if it were a silence, but immobile. After this, to that which is nearest to silence, he gives the deepest sound to the Moon, so that the Moon is the *proslambanomenos* <note>original: *proslambanomenos*; the "added note" at the very bottom of the Greek scale</note>; Mercury is the *hypate hypaton*; Venus the *parhypate hypaton*; the Sun the *lichanos hypaton*; Mars the *hypate melon*; Jupiter the *parhypate melon*; Saturn the *lichanos meson*; and the highest heaven is the *mese*. 

Which of these strings are truly immobile, which are entirely mobile, or which consist of both immobile and mobile parts, will be much more clearly explained when you treat the regular division of the <term>monochord</term> <note>a single-stringed instrument used to demonstrate musical ratios and intervals</note>.

### What the nature of consonance is. Chapter 28.

<image-desc>Large blue ornamental initial 'C' with red pen-flourishing extending into the margin and an internal yellow circular detail.</image-desc> Consonance, although the sense of the ear judges it, nonetheless reason weighs it. For when two strings are stretched with one tension, and being struck together, they return a somewhat mixed and sweet sound, and the two voices

<column-break/>

coalesce as if mixed into one, then that which is called <term>consonance</term> <note>from the Latin *con-sonantia*, meaning "sounding together"</note> occurs. But when, being struck together, each [sound] desires to go its own way, nor do they blend into a sweet and single sound composed of the two, then it is that which is called <term>dissonance</term>. Where consonance is found

### How it may be aided by these comparisons. Chapter 29.

and by aid it is necessary that these dissonances <note>likely a scribal error or specific technical usage, as the context describes the math of consonances</note> be found which are commensurate with one another—that is, which can have a common measure. Just as in multiples, the "double" [ratio of 2:1] is that part which is measured by the difference between two terms; for instance, between 2 and 4, the number two [the binary] measures both. Between 2 and 6, which is a "triple" ratio [3:1], the number two measures both. Between 8 and 8, the same unit is what measures both. 

Again, in <term>superparticulars</term> <note>ratios where the larger number contains the smaller plus one part of it, like 3:2 or 4:3</note>, if there is a <term>sesquialtera</term> proportion <note>original: *sesquialtera*; a 3:2 ratio, which creates a musical fifth</note>, such as 4 to 6, it is the number two that measures both, which is clearly the difference of both. But if it is a <term>sesquitertia</term> proportion <note>original: *sesquitertia*; a 4:3 ratio, which creates a musical fourth</note>, as if 8 were compared to 6, the same number two measures both. 

This, however, does not happen in the other kinds of inequalities which we rejected above, such as in the <term>superpartient</term> <note>ratios where the larger number contains the smaller plus more than one part of it, like 5:3</note>. For if we compare 4 to 3, the number two, which is their difference, measures neither. For compared once to 3, it is smaller; but doubled [to 4], it exceeds it. Likewise, compared twice to 5, it is smaller; but the third time, it surpasses it. And therefore this first kind of inequality is separated from the nature of consonance. 

Furthermore, in those things that form consonances, many things are similar; in the others, however, not at all. This is proven in this way: for the "double" is nothing other than twice the simple; the "triple" is nothing other than thrice the simple; the "quadruple" is the same as four times the simple. But the *sesquialtera* is twice a half; the *sesquitertia* is a third part, which is certainly not the case in other inequalities...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical theory, transitioning from the celestial associations of the strings to the physical and mathematical nature of harmony, specifically contrasting the theories of Plato and Nicomachus.</meta>

### How Plato says consonances are made. Chapter [130].

Plato says that a consonance <note>original: *consonantia*; a sounding together of two different notes that is pleasing to the ear</note> is created in the ear in this way: It is necessary, he says, that a sharper sound be faster. Thus, when it strikes a low sound, it enters the ear quickly. Having struck the farthest part of that body, it is returned, as if by a second pulse, in a repeated motion. But it is then slower and not as fast as when it was first sent out by the initial impact. For this reason, it is also lower. Therefore, when it returns—now being "younger" <note>lower in frequency due to losing energy</note>—it meets the incoming low sound for the first time; they mingle together and, as he says, produce one consonance.

### What Nicomachus thinks against Plato’s view. Chapter [131].

But Nicomachus <note>Nicomachus of Gerasa, a mathematician whose work heavily influenced this text</note> does not think this was truly stated. For he argues that a consonance is not made of similar things, but rather of dissimilar things coming into one and the same concord. If a low sound were mixed with another low sound, no consonance would occur, since similarity does not produce this musical concord, but rather dissimilarity. While they are distinct as individual voices, they are joined together when mixed. 

But Nicomachus thinks a consonance is created in this way: He says there is not just one single pulse that emits a simple mode of sound. Instead, a single strike of one string hits the air many times and creates many voices. But because the speed of this occurrence is so great, one sound strikes against another, as it were. The gap between them is not perceived, and it comes to the ears as if it were a single voice. If, therefore, the occurrences of lower sounds are commensurable <note>mathematically proportional</note> with the occurrences of sharper sounds—according to the ratios we mentioned above—there is no doubt that this very commensuration mixes them together and creates a single voice, which is a consonance.

### Which consonance excels another by merit. Chapter [132].

Among all the consonances we have mentioned, a judgment must be made, both by the ear and by reason, 

<column-break/>

as to which of them should be considered better. For the ear is affected by sounds—or the eye by sight—in the same way that the mind judges numbers or continuous quantity. If a line is set before us, nothing is easier for the eye or the mind than to perceive its double. Likewise, after the judgment of the double, the triple follows. In the same way, the parts of the triple are divided into the quadruple. Because the description of the double is easier, Nicomachus thinks the *diapason* <note>term: diapason; the interval of an octave, based on the 2:1 ratio</note> is the best consonance. After this comes the *diapente* <note>term: diapente; the interval of a perfect fifth, based on the 3:2 ratio</note>, which holds the middle, and then the *diapente* and *diapason* <note>an octave plus a fifth, or a compound fifth, based on the 3:1 ratio</note>, which is a triple ratio. He judges the rest according to this same mode and form. However, Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the famous astronomer and music theorist</note> does not view this in the same way; I will explain his entire opinion later.

### How the things spoken should be understood. Chapter [133].

Nevertheless, we are attempting to treat everything that must be carefully explained hereafter in a summary and brief fashion. This is so that, in the meantime, the reader's mind may become accustomed to these ideas through a certain anticipation, before descending into a more vigorous and detailed treatment later. 

For it was the custom among the Pythagoreans that when anything was said by their master Pythagoras, they did not dare to ask for a reason; for them, the authority of the teacher was his word. This was done so that, for a long time, the student's mind—strengthened by solid doctrine—might eventually find the reason for those same things for himself, even with no one teaching him. So also now, let us entrust to the reader's faith the things we have proposed: that he should believe the *diapason* exists in a double ratio, the *diapente* in a *sesquialtera* <note>the ratio 3:2</note>, the *diatesseron* <note>term: diatesseron; the interval of a perfect fourth, based on the 4:3 ratio</note> in a *sesquitertia* <note>the ratio 4:3</note>, the *diapente* and *diapason* in a triple ratio, and the *bis diapason* <note>two octaves</note> in a quadruple ratio. Later, this reasoning will be explained more diligently, as well as the ways in which musical consonances are gathered by the judgment of the mind, and all other things mentioned above will be built up in a more ample tradition.

<summary>This page compares Plato's physical theory of sound reflection in the ear with Nicomachus's theory of mathematical pulses, while establishing the hierarchy of musical intervals (octave, fifth, fourth) based on their ratios.</summary>
<keywords>Plato, Nicomachus, Pythagoras, Ptolemy, consonance, octave, fifth, fourth, musical ratios, acoustics, physics of sound</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the first book of Boethius's fundamental work on music theory. It transitions from mathematical ratios of intervals to a philosophical definition of what truly constitutes a "musician," contrasting the intellectual theorist with the physical performer.</meta>

And the whole sesquioctave <term>sesquioctave: a ratio of 9:8, which represents a whole musical tone</term>, when broken into proportions, cannot be divided into two equal parts; just as no portion of its kind—that is, the superparticulars <note>original: *superparticulares*; a ratio where the numerator is the denominator plus one (e.g., 3/2, 4/3, 9/8). These cannot be divided into two equal rational ratios</note>—can be. The *Diatesseron* <term>Diatesseron: the interval of a fourth</term> also consists of two tones and a semitone. Furthermore, there are two semitones, a minor and one even smaller. The *Diapente* <term>Diapente: the interval of a fifth</term> is filled by three tones and two minor semitones. It does not reach six whole tones in any way. All these things I will explain later through both the reasoning of proportions and the judgment of the ears. And so much for this.

### What a Musician is. Chapter 34.

Now we must consider that every art and every discipline naturally possesses a more honorable character through reason than through the craftsmanship that is exercised by hand and manual labor. For it is much greater and nobler to know what one does than to merely perform what one knows. Indeed, physical craftsmanship serves like a slave, while reason rules like a mistress. And unless the hand immediately carries out what reason permits, the effort would be in vain. Therefore, the science of music is as much more excellent in the knowledge of reason than in the work of performing and acting, as the mind surpasses the body. For that which lacks reason lives in servitude, while reason commands and leads toward what is right. Unless its command is obeyed, the work—lacking reason—will stumble. Hence it follows that the contemplation of reason does not need the act of working, but the works of the hands are nothing unless guided by reason. Indeed, how great the glory and merit of reason is can be understood from this: that other physical craftsmen, so to speak, do not take their names from their discipline, but rather from their instruments. For the *citharedus* <note>original: *citharedus*; a lyre-player</note> is named from the *cithara*, the *tibicen* <note>original: *tibicen*; a pipe or flute player</note> from the *tibia*, and others are named from the titles of their instruments.

<column-break/>

He, however, is a musician who assumes the science of singing through the power of reason, not by the servitude of work, but by the command of contemplation. We see this same principle in the works of buildings and wars, specifically in the naming of terms. For buildings are inscribed with the names, and triumphs are celebrated in the names, of those by whose command and reason they were established, not those by whose labor and completion they were finished. There are, therefore, three classes concerned with music. One class is that which performs with instruments. Another composes songs. The third judges the work of the instrument and the song. But that class which is occupied with instruments and spends all its effort there—such as the lyre-players and those who prove their skill on the organ and other musical instruments—is separated from the understanding of musical science, because they serve, as was said, and bring nothing of reason, being devoid of all contemplation. The second class of those dealing with music is that of the Poets, which is led to song not so much by contemplation and reason as by a certain natural instinct. And for this reason, this class is also to be separated from the true science of music. The third class is that which possesses the skill of judging, so that it can evaluate rhythms, melodies, and the whole song. Since this is entirely placed in reason and contemplation, this shall be properly assigned to Music. He is a musician who possesses the faculty of judging modes and rhythms, the kinds of melodies and their mixtures, and everything which must be explained later, as well as the songs of the poets, according to the contemplation or reason proposed and suitable for music...

->Here ends the book on Music, that is, on<-
->the harmonic instruction, the first book of Anicius<-
->Manlius Severinus Boethius, former consul.<-

# Introduction. 1.

The previous volume organized all the things which I have now proposed to demonstrate diligently. Therefore, before I come to offering the proofs by their proper reasons, let me set down a few things by which the mind of the Christian listener may be prepared to grasp the things that are to be said.

### What Pythagoras established philosophy to be. 2.

Pythagoras was the first of all to name the study of wisdom "philosophy" <note>original: *phiam*, an abbreviation for *philosophiam*</note>. He defined it as the knowledge and discipline of those things which are properly and truly said "to be." He believed that those things "are" which neither increase by extension nor decrease by diminution, nor are changed by any accidents <note>accidents: in philosophy, these are non-essential qualities that can change without changing the nature of the object</note>. These things are forms, magnitudes, qualities, relations, and the rest which, when studied by themselves, are immutable; but when they are joined to bodies, they are altered and transformed into the many-shaped variations of changing things.

### On the differences of quantity and which branch of philosophy it is assigned to. Chapter 3.

According to Pythagoras, all quantity is either continuous or discrete. That which is continuous is called "magnitude," while that which is discrete is called "multitude."

<summary>This page contains the table of contents for Book II of Boethius’s music treatise and begins the text with Pythagoras’s definition of philosophy as the study of unchanging truths, specifically magnitudes and multitudes.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, philosophy, discrete quantity, continuous quantity, magnitude, multitude, Book II, Boethius, musical ratios, medieties</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the Second Book of Boethius’s "Fundamentals of Music." Having listed the chapters, the author now defines the four mathematical disciplines (the Quadrivium) based on the nature of quantity—whether it is discrete (numbers) or continuous (magnitude), and whether it is absolute or relative.</meta>

Of these <note>original: "Quorum", referring to the types of quantity</note>, there is a distinct and almost perfect property. For multitude <note>the concept of discrete numbers, like 1, 2, 3</note>, starting from a finite amount, increases and proceeds toward infinity, so that no end to its growth is ever found. It is bounded at its minimum, but is limitless at its maximum. Its beginning is unity <note>the number 1</note>, than which nothing is smaller. It grows through numbers and proceeds toward infinity, and no force can create a limit to prevent it from growing. But magnitude <note>the concept of continuous size or space, like a line</note>, on the other hand, admits a finite quantity in its measure, yet it may become infinite. For if there is a line one foot long, or of any other kind, it can be divided into two equal parts; and its half can be cut into another half; and that half again into another half, so that there is never any limit to the cutting of the magnitude. Thus, magnitude is limited at its largest scale, but becomes infinite when it begins to be divided. Conversely, number is finite at its smallest scale, but begins to be infinite as it increases. Since these things are thus infinite, philosophy treats them as if they were finite things. Within them, it finds some limit about which the mind can rightly apply its own specialized observation. For of magnitude, some things are immobile, like the earth; others are mobile, like the sphere of the heavens and whatever revolves within it with swift rotation. Of discrete quantities, some are absolute <note>original: "per se", meaning they exist on their own, like the number 4</note>, while others are relative <note>original: "ad aliquid", meaning they exist in relation to another number, like "double"</note>, such as those born from mathematical operations. Geometry holds the study of immobile magnitude, while astronomy pursues the science of mobile magnitude. Arithmetic is the authority on absolute discrete quantity, while Music is proven to possess the skill related to relative quantity.

### On the division of relative quantity. Chapter 4.

**W**<insert>e</insert> have already spoken sufficiently in the *Arithmetic* regarding that discrete quantity which is absolute. Regarding relative quantity, there are three simple types.

<column-break/>

One is the multiple; another is the superparticular; and the third is the superpartient. When the multiple is mixed with the superparticular and the superpartient, two others are formed from these: namely, the multiple superparticular and the multiple superpartient. The rule for all of these is as follows: If you wish to compare unity <note>the number 1</note> to all numbers in their natural order, the multiple order is woven by the ratio. For two to one is double. Three to the same is triple. Four is quadruple, and in others in the same way, as the description placed below teaches.

| 1 | 1 | 1 | 1 | 1 | 1 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2 | 3 | 4 | 5 | 6 | 7 |

**I**f, however, you wish to find the superparticular proportion, compare the natural numbers to one another with a difference of one <note>literally "with unity removed"</note>: for example, 3 to 2 is the one-and-a-half <note>original: "sesquialter"</note> ratio; 4 to 3 is the one-and-a-third <note>original: "sesquitertius"</note> ratio; 5 to 4 is the one-and-a-fourth <note>original: "sesquiquartus"</note> ratio; and in others in the same way, as the following description demonstrates:

| 2 | 3 | 4 | 5 | 6 | 7 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| | One-and-a-half | One-and-a-third | One-and-a-fourth | One-and-a-fifth | One-and-a-sixth |

**Y**ou will find the superpartients in this way. Arrange the natural numbers starting from three. If you skip one step, you will note a superpartient <note>specifically the "two-parts-over" ratio</note>. If you skip two, a "three-parts-over" ratio; if three, a "four-parts-over" ratio; and so on for the others.

| 3 | 4 | 5 | 6 | 7 | 8 | 9 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

<image-desc>A semicircular diagram positioned below a row of Roman numerals (3 through 9). Three curved arcs connect specific numbers: the first arc connects 3 to 5 and is labeled "Two-parts-over" <note>original: "Superbiptiens"; a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller once plus two-thirds of the smaller</note>; the second arc connects 4 to 7 and is labeled "Three-parts-over" <note>original: "Supertripartiens"; a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller once plus three-quarters of the smaller</note>; the third arc connects 5 to 9 and is labeled "Four-parts-over" <note>original: "Superquadrupartiens"; a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller once plus four-fifths of the smaller</note>. The labels are written along the curves of the arcs.</image-desc>

The diligent reader will further observe the order and the proportions composed of multiples and superparticulars, or multiples and superpartients. But all these things have been explained more quickly in the *Arithmetic*.

<meta>This page continues Boethius’s exploration of mathematical proportions. ### Why multiplicity excels the others. 105.

<margin>118</margin>
**B**ut in these matters, one must consider that the "multiple" genus of inequality <note>original: "multiplicis inequalitatis genus"; this refers to ratios where the larger number is a clean multiple of the smaller, like 2:1 or 3:1</note> seems far older and more fundamental than the other two types. For the natural arrangement of numbers is perfected in multiples when compared to unity (which is first). In contrast, the "superpartient" ratio <note>a ratio like 5:3, where the larger number contains the smaller plus several parts of it</note> is not formed by a simple sequence, but is created through an unlimited scattering of those smaller numbers arranged after unity, such as three and the rest, in a certain way. The superpartient ratio actually occupies a much more "backward" or secondary position. It is not formed by continuous numbers, but by skipped ones; it must necessarily be created by skips: here by skipping one number, there by two, here by three, here by four, and so it grows into infinity. Furthermore, multiplicity begins from one; "superparticularity" <note>ratios like 3:2 or 4:3, where the larger number contains the smaller plus one part of it</note> begins from two; but the superpartient genus takes its start only from three. But enough of these things for now. Now it will be necessary to present certain things that the Greeks call *axioms* <note>original: "axiomata"; fundamental truths that require no proof</note>. We will understand what they are pointing toward only when we deal with the demonstration of each individual thing.

### 106.
### What square numbers are, and an observation about them

**A** square number is one produced by a double dimension into equal parts, such as twice two, three times three, four times four, five times five, or six times six. Here is a description of them:

| 2 | 3 | 4 | 5 | 6 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 4 | 9 | 16 | 25 | 36 |
| 7 | 9 | 11 | 13 | 15 |
| 49 | 64 | 81 | 100 | |

**T**hus, the natural number arranged in the top row is the side <term>side: what we would today call the "square root"</term> of the squares described below them. Continuous natural squares are those that follow one another in order, such as 4, 9, 16, and so on. If I subtract a smaller

<column-break/>

continuous square from a larger continuous square, the remainder will be exactly equal to the sum of the sides of both squares. For example, if I take four from nine, five remains; this five is the sum of two and three, which are the sides of those two squares. Likewise, if nine is taken from the number marked 16, seven remains; this is the sum of three and four, which are the sides of the aforementioned squares. The same pattern holds for the rest. But if the squares are not continuous <note>i.e., they are not next to each other in the sequence</note> but one square is skipped between them, then half of the remainder is equal to the sum of both sides. For instance, if I take the square 4 from the square 16, 12 remains; the half of 12 is 6, which is the sum of the two sides (2 and 4). The same method applies to the others. If indeed two squares are skipped, then the sum of the sides will be the third part of the remainder. For example, if I take 4 from 25 (skipping the squares 9 and 16), the remainder is 21. Their sides are 2 and 5, which make 7; and 7 is the third part of the remainder, 21. This is the rule: if three are skipped, the sum of the sides is the fourth part of the remainder left by subtracting the smaller from the larger. If four are skipped, it is the fifth part. The name of the fractional part is always one number higher than the number of skipped squares.

### That all inequality proceeds from equality, and its demonstration. 107.

**J**ust as unity <note>the number 1</note> is the principle of the plurality of numbers, so equality <note>the ratio 1:1</note> is the principle of proportions. For by three rules, as has been said in *Arithmetic*, we may produce a multiple <gloss>proportion</gloss> from equality. From the inversion of multiples, we have all the superparticulars ready for us. Likewise, from the inversion of the superparti—

<summary>Boethius explains why multiple ratios are mathematically primary and describes the geometric properties of square numbers, specifically how the difference between squares relates to the sum of their roots. He concludes by asserting that all complex ratios (inequality) are ultimately derived from the ratio of equality (1:1).</summary>
<keywords>multiplicity, square numbers, sides, roots, axioms, superpartient, superparticular, equality, ratio theory, Boethius</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration of how different types of ratios are "born" from one another. Having established that equality is the parent of all inequality, Boethius now provides the specific procedural steps for generating multiple, superparticular, and superpartient ratios using sets of three numbers.</meta>

...we produce superpartient comparisons from superparticular ones. For let three units, or three twos, or three threes, or any equal terms be set down. Let the first number in the new sequence be equal to the first in the old. But the second number is the sum of the first and the second; the third is the sum of the first, two of the second, and the third. In this way, a progression of multiplicity is made. This is the first description of "doubleness" <note>original: "duplex"; the 2:1 ratio</note>.

| I | I | I |
|---|---|---|
| I | II | IIII |

For the unit placed in the second row is equal to the first unit arranged in the upper place. Likewise, the two is equal to the first and second units. Likewise, the four is equal to the first unit, two of the second units, and the third unit. Thus 1, 2, 4 is a "double" proportion. If you do the same with these, a triple comparison will be created; and from triple, quadruple; from quadruple, quintuple, and so on. Such is the nature of these proportions. Now, let us convert this and arrange the larger term first: 4, 2, 1. Let the first be equal to the first (4); the second be the sum of the first and second (6); the third be the sum of the first, two of the second, and the third (9). When these are arranged, a *sesquialtera* <term>sesquialtera: a ratio of 3:2, where the larger number contains the smaller plus its half</term> proportion is noted.

| IIII | II | I |
|---|---|---|
| IIII | VI | VIIII |

**A**nd if this is done from triples, a *sesquitertia* <note>a 4:3 ratio</note> is produced; if from quadruples, a *sesquiquarta* <note>a 5:4 ratio</note>. Any proportions you like on either side will be born from multiplicity. From a reversed superparticularity, however, a superpartient <note>a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller plus several fractional parts, like 5:3</note> relationship is drawn. For let the *sesquialtera* proportion be arranged again: 9, 6, 4. Let the first be equal to the first (9); the second be the sum of the first and second (15); the third be the sum of the first, two of the second, and the third (25). Let them be arranged in order in this way:

| VIIII | VI | IIII |
|---|---|---|
| VIIII | XV | XXV |

A superpartient relationship has been produced from the reversed *sesquialteras*. If any diligent searcher approaches this specu-

<column-break/>

-lation, they will find that superpartients are produced from reversed *sesquitertias*. By extending similar terms, all species of superpartients will likewise be changed from superparticularity. If, however, the superparticulars are not reversed, but remain as they were created from the multiple, then "multiple-superparticulars" <note>ratios like 2½:1 or 5:2</note> will necessarily be produced. And from superpartients remaining as they came from superparticulars, nothing other than "multiple-superpartients" <note>ratios like 2⅔:1 or 8:3</note> will be produced. But enough of these things for now; for this comparison is disputed more diligently in the books on Arithmetic. Here are the rules for finding continuous superparticular proportions in any quantity.

**A**ttend to this specific point: how a musical theorist <note>original: "Musica disputator"; likely referring to a calculator of harmonic intervals</note> marks out equal proportions of superparticulars, such as 18 in 1,450. But lest any error of difficulty hinder those doing this by chance or madness, we shall derive any equal proportions from multiplicity by this rule: Every multiple, calculated from unity, produces as many superparticular relationships as it is steps away from unity, following the continuous part of the denomination. For instance, "double" precedes *sesquialteras*, "triple" precedes *sesquitertias*, "quadruple" precedes *sesquiquartas*, and so on in this manner. Therefore, let the following description of "double" terms be set down.

| I | II | IIII | VIII | XVI |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| | III | VI | XII | XXIIII |
| | | IX | XVIII | XXXVI |
| | | | XXVII | LIIII |
| | | | | LXXXI |

| 1 | 4 | 8 | 27 | 81 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2 | 6 | 12 | 36 | 108 |
| 3 | 9 | 18 | 54 | 162 |

In the description of the superior genus <note>referring to the previous category of multiples</note>, we see the *sesquitertian* <note>4:3</note> proportions born in such a way that the first number precedes one *sesquitertian*; the second, two; the third, three; and a part of the number three always closes the last natural number with a certain limit. But if you set up quadruples, you will find *sesquiquartas* <note>5:4 ratios</note> in the same way; if you set up quintuples, you find *sesquiquintas* <note>6:5 ratios</note>. And thereafter, the individual multiples, by their very name, procreate as many *superparticulars* <term>superparticular: a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller plus one "part" or unit, such as 3:2, 4:3, or 5:4</term> as they are steps removed from unity. We shall now set down one arrangement of quadruples, so that in these certain matters the diligent reader may exercise an examination of the mind.

| 1 | 4 | 16 | 64 | 256 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **row 2** 5 | 20 | 80 | 320 | |
| **row 3** 25 | 100 | 400 | | |
| **row 4** 125 | 500 | 625 | | |

This operation is seen to be invented so that whenever one wishes to investigate four or five or any number of *sesquialters* or *sesquitertians*—that is, *sesquioctaves* <note>original: "sesquioctanos"; the 9:8 ratio, the basis of the whole tone in music</note> or any other proportions—he may not fall into error. For such proportions are not easily fitted to the first number unless that number is able to have as many terms before and after it as are proposed. But if one arranges the appropriate multiples, he may see how many *superparticulars* he requires and look to that multiple which is as many steps removed from unity as described in the above

<column-break/>

descriptions. For instance, if he perhaps seeks three *sesquialters* <note>3:2 ratios</note>, he should not begin the investigation from the number four. For since four is the second "double" <note>in the sequence 1, 2, 4</note>, it only precedes two such ratios; he will not be able to find a third <note>The sequence 4:6:9 stops because 9 is odd and cannot be halved to create the next 3:2 ratio</note>. But if he begins from eight, a middle term will be applied properly. Since eight is the third term <note>in the sequence 2, 4, 8</note>, it will produce the three *sesquialter* proportions he seeks <note>The sequence 8:12:18:27</note>. And in other cases, it works in the same way.

There is also another way of increasing proportions in this manner. One compares the "roots" of proportions to the same proportions in their smallest terms. For all the natural numbers from unity are arranged: 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7. The smallest proportions are such as 2 to 3 in the *sesquialter*; 3 to 4 in the *sesquitertian*; 5 to 4 in the *sesquioctave* <note>Note: the text here says 5 to 4, which is technically a sesquiquarta, but the author is discussing the general principle</note>, and so on to infinity. And since the proportions always exceed unity, the proposition is that a continuous comparison may produce two *sesquialter* proportions. I take the *sesquialter* root and arrange it as 2 and 3. I therefore multiply 2 by 2, which makes 4. Likewise, I multiply 3 by 2, which makes 6. Then again, we lead the 3 into itself, and it makes 9. These are arranged in this way:

| Numbers | 2 | 3 | |
|---|---|---|---|
| | 4 | 6 | 9 |

Thus, the two proposed *sesquialter* proportions are 6 to 4 and 9 to 6. Now let the goal be to find three. I arrange the same numbers which I had proposed above for the two *sesquialter* relationships, and the *sesquialter* proportions themselves. I will multiply the 4 by two, making 8. Again, the 6 by two, making 12. Again, the 9 by two, making 18. Finally, the 9 by three, making 27. Let them be arranged in this way:

| 2 | 3 | | |
|---|---|---|---|
| 4 | 6 | 9 | |
| 8 | 12 | 18 | 27 |

### Chapter IX.
## On the smaller ratio that flows from others

If the difference between two numbers has measured them both exactly, then those numbers are in the same ratio as the numbers according to which their difference measured them. For instance, let there be the numbers **55** and **50**. These are compared to each other in a **sesquidecima** <note>The 11:10 ratio</note> relationship, and their difference is **five**, which is indeed the tenth part of **50**. Therefore, this five measures the number **50** ten times, and **55** eleven times. Thus, the proper difference of **55** and **50** is five, and it measures them according to **11** and **10**, set in a sesquidecima comparison. Therefore, numbers that are measured exactly by their own difference are in the same ratio as the numbers that define that measurement.

But if the difference of the smaller numbers measures the numbers it belongs to in such a way that a remainder precedes the measurement in the smaller number, and there is an equal excess in both—so that the measure of the difference is less than the plurality of the numbers—then these numbers will obtain a **greater ratio** to each other once that remainder is subtracted. For example, take the numbers **53** and **58**. Let **five**, which is their difference, measure them. Five measures **53** ten times 

<column-break/>

up to **50**, leaving a remainder of **three**. Again, let it measure the number **58** eleven times up to **55**, and in that, it again leaves a remainder of **three**. Let the three be subtracted from both, making **50** and **55**, which are arranged in this way:

| In this, it is therefore manifest | 53 | 58 |
|---|---|---|
| that the ratio between 50 and 55 is greater than between 53 and 58. | 50 | 55 |

For in **smaller numbers, a larger ratio is always found**, which we shall demonstrate shortly. 

Conversely, if that measured difference exceeds the numbers by the same amount in both cases, the ratios of the numbers measured above will be **smaller** with the addition of that sum, compared to what they were before their own difference measured them. For example, let the numbers be **48** and **53**. Their difference is **five**. Five measures **48** ten times, which makes **50**; thus **50** exceeds the number **48** by **two**. Likewise, let it measure **53** eleven times, which makes **55**, which again exceeds the number **53** by **two**. Let two be added to both, and arranged in this way:

| The ratio of 50 to 55 | 48 | 53 |
|---|---|---|
| is therefore smaller | 50 | 55 |

when compared with the addition of the two by which the measuring difference exceeded them, than the ratio of the numbers **48** and **53**, which that same difference of five measured. 

**Greater and smaller ratios** are understood in this way: A **half** part is greater than a **third**, and a **third** is greater than a **fourth**. A **fourth** part is greater than a **fifth**, and so on in the same way. From this it follows that a **sesquitertia** ratio <note>4:3</note> is greater than a **sesquiquinta** <note>6:5</note>, and the sesquitertia surpasses the sesquiquinta, and so in the others. Hence it happens that a **greater ratio** is always seen in **smaller numbers** among the smaller **superparticulars** <term>superparticular: a ratio of the form (n+1):n</term>, which appears in the natural number sequence. With the natural numbers arranged: **1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6**; two compared to one is **double**; three to two is **sesquialter** <note>3:2</note>; four to three is **sesquitertius** <note>4:3</note>. Indeed, the larger...

ratio, 4:3 ratio, 6:5 ratio, a group of five, a group of two, a group of three, 11:10 ratio, number, difference, 3:2 ratio

<summary>This page explains how to determine and compare ratios by looking at the difference between two numbers. It establishes the mathematical principle that as numbers in a superparticular ratio (like 3:2 or 4:3) increase, the ratio itself becomes "smaller" or closer to equality.</summary>
<keywords>arithmetic ratios, superparticular, sesquitertian, sesquiquinta, sesquidecima, mathematical measurement, Boethian proportions</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on superparticular ratios, specifically addressing how they behave when numbers are increased and how they combine to form multiple ratios. It transitions into a foundational explanation of the three types of mathematical means.</meta>

...are the numbers three and four. In turn, three and two and one. In larger numbers, a smaller **proportion** <note>In this context, proportion refers to the ratio or the "size" of the relationship between two numbers.</note> is contained, and in smaller numbers, a larger proportion. From this, it appears that if an equal quantity is added to any numbers maintaining a **superparticular** <note>A ratio where the larger number contains the smaller number plus one part of it, like 3:2 or 4:3.</note> proportion: the proportion was smaller before the increase of that quantity than after the quantity was added to them.

### Chapter X.
### Whether multiples are produced from multiples and superparticulars.

It also seems necessary to state beforehand what will be demonstrated later. If a **multiple** <note>A ratio where the larger number is exactly divisible by the smaller, like 2:1 or 3:1.</note> interval is multiplied by two, that which is born from that multiplication is also a multiple. But if that which is created from such a multiplication is not a multiple, then the original interval that was multiplied by two was not a multiple. Likewise, if a superparticular proportion is multiplied by two: the result is neither superparticular nor multiple. If that which is born from such a multiplication is neither multiple nor superparticular: then that which was multiplied by two is either superparticular or of another kind, but certainly not a multiple.

### Chapter XI.
### Which superparticulars produce which multiples.

To these things, it must be added: the first two superparticulars produce the first multiple proportion. For example, if a **sesquialter** <note>The 3:2 ratio.</note> and a **sesquitertian** <note>The 4:3 ratio.</note> are joined, they create a **double** proportion <note>A 2:1 ratio. Mathematically: (3/2) × (4/3) = 12/6 = 2/1.</note>. For the numbers are 2, 3, and 4 (three compared to two is sesquialter; four to three is sesquitertian; four to two is double). Again, the first multiple added to the first superparticular creates the second multiple. For the numbers are 2, 4, and 6. For four compared to two is double, which is the first multiple. But six compared to four is sesquialter, which is the first superparticular; six to two is

<column-break/>

**triple** <note>The 3:1 ratio.</note>, which is the second multiple. But if you add a triple ratio to a sesquitertian, a **quadruple** <note>The 4:1 ratio.</note> is produced. If a quadruple to a sesquiquartan <note>The 5:4 ratio.</note>: a **quintuple** <note>The 5:1 ratio.</note>; and in this way, by joining proportions of multiples and superparticulars, infinite multiples are procreated.

### Chapter XII.
### On Arithmetic, Geometric, and Harmonic means.

Now that we have discussed the portions that were to be treated, we must speak here of **means** <note>Latin: *medietatibus*. A "mean" is a middle value or a way of balancing numbers between two extremes.</note>. For a proportion is a certain comparison of two terms to each other. We take numbers as the terms. A **proportionality** <note>Latin: *proportionalitas*. This refers to a series of ratios, like 1:2:3 or 1:2:4.</note> is a collection of equal proportions. A proportionality consists of a minimum of three terms. For when the first term maintains the same proportion to the second as the second does to the third, this is a proportionality. And between three terms, there is a middle term, which is the second. Therefore, the ratio of the three-fold arrangement <margin>the same proportion</margin> joining these proportions by a middle term is as follows: 

Either the **difference** <note>The amount by which one number exceeds another (subtraction).</note> of the smaller term to the middle is equal to the difference of the middle to the largest, but the proportion is not equal, as in these numbers: **1, 2, 3**. Between one and two, and between two and three, only a single unit holds the difference. However, the proportion is not equal: for two compared to one is double, while three to two is sesquialter. <note>This describes the **Arithmetic Mean**.</note>

Or the proportion is equal in both, but it is not established with equal differences, as in these numbers: **1, 2, 4**. For two to one is double, just as four is to two; but between four and two, the difference is two, while <margin><image-desc>hand pointing left</image-desc> difference</margin> between two and the unit, the difference is one. <note>This describes the **Geometric Mean**.</note>

There is indeed a third kind of mean which consists of neither the same proportions nor the same differences. Instead, as the largest term is to the smallest, so is the larger difference of the terms to the smaller difference of the terms, as in these numbers: **2, 3, 6**. For six to two is triple. Between six and...

Therefore, as the greatest term is to the least, so is the difference of the larger terms to the difference of the smaller terms. Therefore, that mean <term>mean: a middle value that connects two other values</term> in which the differences are equal is called **Arithmetic**. That mean in which the proportion <note>ratio</note> is equal is **Geometric**. But the third, which we describe, is the **Harmonic** mean. We provide these examples below:

We are not unaware that there are other means of proportions... but for the present treatise, these alone are necessary. But among these three means, proportionality is in a certain sense properly and especially called "geometric." This is because the whole is woven together by equal proportions <note>meaning equal ratios, like 1:2 and 2:4</note>. However, we shall use the term "proportionality" interchangeably, calling the others by this name as well.

### On continuous and disjoined means.

**B**ut among these, one proportionality is continuous (Chapter 13) and another is disjoined. It is continuous, as arranged above, when one and the same middle number is placed after the larger and before the smaller <note>e.g., in 1, 2, 4, the number 2 is the "middle" that links both ends</note>. Whenever there are not two middle terms, it is called a disjoined proportionality, such as the Geometric mean in this manner: 1, 2, 4, 5. <note>The transcription here reads 1, 2, 4, 5, though the mathematical logic that follows—"as 2 is to 1, so 6 is to 3"—suggests the sequence should be 1, 2, 3, 6.</note> For as two is to one, so six is to three; and this is called a disjoined proportionality. From this, it can be understood that continuous proportionality is found in at least three terms, while disjoined is found in four.

<column-break/>

However, a continuous proportionality can exist in four or more terms, in this manner: 1, 2, 4, 8, 16. But here, the proportions <note>the ratios between the numbers</note> will always be one fewer than the number of terms established.

### Why the means organized above were so named. Chapter 14.

We said that one mean is called **Arithmetic** because there is an equal distance between the terms according to number <note>e.g., 1, 2, 3 all have a distance of exactly 1 between them</note>. It is known as **Geometric** because the quality of the proportion <note>ratio</note> is similar. But it is called **Harmonic** because it is so fitted together that an equality of proportions is considered in the differences and the terms <note>The Harmonic mean was seen as the most "musical" because its proportions reflect the physical properties of vibrating strings</note>. These things have been discussed more diligently in *Arithmetic* <note>The author is likely referring to Boethius's earlier work, *De Arithmetica*</note>; for now, we run through them only as a reminder.

### How the aforementioned means are produced from equality. Chapter 15.

**B**ut we must speak briefly on how these proportionalities are produced from equality. It was said before that what "unity" <note>the number one</note> is worth in the world, "equality" is worth in proportions. And just as unity is the head of numbers, so equality is the principle of proportions. Therefore, the **Arithmetic mean** is born from equality in this way: when three equal terms are placed, there are two ways by which this proportionality is produced. For let the first [new term] be equal to the first [original term]. Let the second be the sum of the first and the second. Let the third be the sum of the first, the second, and the third. This is shown by this example: Let there be three units [1, 1, 1]. Let the first be equal to the first, which is 1. The second is the first and the second [1+1], which is 2. The third is the first, second, and third [1+1+1], which is 3. The arrangement will be thus:

| 1 | 1 | 1 |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | 2 | 3 |

In the case where the dyad holds the equality, two is the difference, and one [number] is always skipped between the terms. There is also another way of preparing an **Arithmetic** proportionality. For let three terms be established as equals: let the first be equal to the first; the second equal to the first and the second; the third equal to the first, the second, and the third. | 4 | 6 | 8 |

[The number two] <note>original: "narius"</note> holds the difference between the terms naturally skipped. For between 4 and 6, the number 5 is naturally skipped. Between 6 and 8, the number 7 is placed. But if a triad is the starting point of equality, three will be the difference, with always one less number <note>i.e., two numbers</note> skipped between them. And the same may be seen in the number four; those things which we pass over for the sake of brevity, the diligent reader will find for himself by these same rules.

Now, how **Geometric** proportionality is found from equality can be shown, just as we showed how all inequality flows from equality. Unless it is tiresome, this also must be briefly repeated. Three equal terms being established: let the first be equal to the first; the second equal to the first and the second; the third equal to the first, two of the second, and the third. Let this be done continuously, and thus geometric proportionality takes its beginning from equality. But we have spoken of these proportions most diligently. If the reader approaches this instructed by those things, he will be disturbed by no error of doubt.

And in this way, if there are three units: let the first be equal to the first and two of the second, which is 3. The second equal to two of the first and two of the second, which is 4. # On the Harmonic mean and a more abundant speculation upon it. 116.

But in the present discussion of harmony, I do not think that those things which can be said more diligently concerning it should be passed over. For let a harmonic proportionality be placed in that description where the differences are arranged in the upper order of terms:

| 1 | 2 |
| :---: | :---: |
| 3 | 4 | 6 |

->Differences<-
->TERMS<-

Do you not see, therefore, how 3 to 4 establishes the <term>diatesseron</term> <note>the interval of a fourth</note> consonance? 6 to 3 establishes the <term>diapason</term> <note>the interval of an octave</note> concordance. 6 to 4, truly, [establishes] the <term>diapente</term> <note>the interval of a fifth; the original Latin text incorrectly says "diapason" here</note> consonance symphony. And the differences of these themselves, in turn, establish the same consonance; for in the <term>dyads</term> compared to the unit, a double is established in the <term>diapason</term> <note>octave</note> consonance. 

But if the extremes multiply each other, and the middle term grows by its own multiplication, they will undoubtedly preserve the proportion in their compared state. For three times 6 makes 18. Four times four becomes 16. But 18 compared to 16 is seen to be greater by an eighth part <note>the ratio 18:16 simplifies to 9:8, known as the "epogdous" or the musical whole tone</note>. Again, if the smallest term multiplies itself, it will make 9. But if the greater term gains strength by its own multiplication, it will make 36. These, compared to each other, are quadruple, which is the <term>bisdiapason</term> <note>a double octave</note> ::

<column-break/>

| 2 | 2 | 2 |
| :---: | :---: | :---: |
| 6 | 8 | 12 |

It appears that the terms differing from themselves by a double <insert>written above</insert> are distant as the following descriptions advise:

| 2 | 2 | 2 |
| :---: | :---: | :---: |
| 4 | 6 | 12 |

And from the second seat, out of the first and two of the second thirds; and from the first, two of the second, and three of the thirds, as the <insert>above-written</insert> description is placed.

--- they preserve the consonance. If we inspect these things diligently, this will be the whole definition of letters, or the musical multiplication of terms into themselves. For the smallest term multiplied by the middle will be 12; and if it is multiplied by the greatest, it will be 18. The middle term, however, if increased by the magnitude of the greatest, will be 24. Again, if the smallest term grows by itself, it will be 8 <note>the text says 8, though 3x3 is 9; 8 may refer to a different starting term like 2x2x2</note>. In the same way, the middle will become 16. But the term which is the greatest, if it multiplies itself, will be 36. Let these, therefore, be arranged in order:

<insert>24</insert> <insert>18</insert>
<insert>18</insert> <insert>12</insert> <insert>16</insert> <insert>8</insert>
<insert>36</insert> <insert>24</insert>

Therefore, 18 to 12 and 16 to 8 are sounding <term>diatesseron</term> <note>fourth</note> consonances. 24 to 18 and 16 to 8 [are likewise]. <term>Diapente</term> <note>fifth</note> truly is preserved in the comparison of 18 to 12, and 24 to 16, and 36 to 24. The triple, which is the <term>diapason and diapente</term> <note>an octave plus a fifth</note>, is 36 to 12. The quadruple, which is the <term>bisdiapason</term> <note>double octave</note>, is 36 to 8. The <term>epogdous</term>, which is the tone, is preserved in the comparison of 18 to 16.

# In what manner the above-mentioned means may be placed in turn between two terms. 117.

Two terms are usually given as a proposition so that between them we may place here an arithmetic mean, there a geometric, and now a harmonic mean; concerning which we have also spoken in the *Arithmetics*. But unless we explain briefly here... If the arithmetic...

<summary>This page explores the properties of the harmonic mean (specifically the 3, 4, 6 proportion) and demonstrates how musical intervals like the octave, fifth, and fourth are derived from the multiplication of these terms. It introduces the method for inserting different types of mathematical means between two given numbers.</summary>
<keywords>harmonic mean, diatesseron, diapason, diapente, bisdiapason, epogdous, musical ratios, mathematical proportions, Boethius</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of mathematical proportions, specifically providing a practical demonstration of how to calculate arithmetic, geometric, and harmonic means between two numbers before transitioning into how these ratios define the fundamental intervals of music theory.</meta>

To find the middle of two terms, the difference between them is to be divided, and that division is to be added to the smaller term. For example, let 10 <note>original: "x"</note> and 40 <note>original: "xl"</note> be established as the outer terms. Since a middle according to <term>Arithmetic proportionality</term> <note>a sequence where the difference between numbers is constant, like 1, 2, 3</note> is sought, I first look at the difference between both, which is 30. I divide this, which makes 15; I add this to the smaller term, that is to say the ten, and it makes 25. If, therefore, this is placed in the middle between 40 and 10, an arithmetic proportion is made in this way: 10 . 25 . 40. Next, let us place a <term>geometric mean</term> <note>a sequence where the ratio between numbers is constant, like 2, 4, 8</note> between those terms. For I multiply the magnitude of the extremes, as in 10 times 40, which makes 400. I take the <term>square root</term> <note>original: "tetragonale latus"; literally the 'four-angled side'</note> of these; it makes 20. For twenty times twenty makes 400. If I place these middle terms between 10 and 40, let it be a geometric mean formed by the following description: 10 . 20 . 40. But if we seek a <term>harmonic mean</term> <note>a complex proportion used in music where the middle term relates to the extremes in a specific ratio of differences</note>, we join the extremes to themselves, such as 10 and 40, which makes 50. Their difference, which is 30, we multiply by the smaller term, namely by the ten, so that they make ten times 30, which is 300. We immediately divide these by the 50; it makes 6. Which, if we add it to the smaller of all terms, results in 16. If we place this as the middle between 10 and 40, the harmonic proportionality is completed: 10 . 16 . 40. But enough concerning these things.

### On the merit or manner of consonances according to Nicomachus. Chapter 18.

Now it seems that this must be added: in what way the followers of Pythagoras <note>the famous Greek mathematician and philosopher</note> claim that musical <term>consonances</term> <note>combinations of notes that sound pleasant together</note> are found in the aforementioned proportions. In which matter, it is evident that <term>Ptolemy</term> <note>an influential astronomer and music theorist</note> does not agree with them, about which we shall speak a little later. For this must be established: that the first and sweetest consonance is that whose property the sense [of hearing] perceives most clearly. For just as each thing is through itself, so it is also grasped by the sense. If, therefore, that consonance which consists in a <term>double</term> ratio <note>the ratio of 2:1</note> is better known to all,

<column-break/>

there is no doubt that the <term>diapason</term> <note>original: "diapason"; the interval of an octave</note> is the first consonance of all, and it rightly excels because it leads in recognition. The remaining [consonances] necessarily hold their order according to the Pythagoreans following this one—an order which is maintained by the increase of <term>multiplicity</term> <note>ratios like 2:1, 3:1</note> and the <term>superparticular</term> <note>ratios where the larger number is one more than the smaller, like 3:2 or 4:3</note> relationship. Indeed, it has been shown that the inequality of multiplicity transcends the superparticular proportions in terms of ancient natural priority. Since our natural numbers are arranged from unity up to the number four: 1 . 2 . 3 . 4. Therefore, two compared to one makes a double proportion and produces the <term>diapason</term> consonance, which is the greatest and best known for its simplicity. But if three is compared to the unit, it will sound the concord of the <term>diapason and diapente</term> <note>an octave plus a fifth, or a 3:1 ratio</note>. Truly, four compared to the unit holds a fourfold ratio, namely it produces the symphony of the <term>bisdiapason</term> <note>a double octave</note>. But if three is compared to two, it completes the <term>diapente</term> <note>the interval of a fifth</note>. If four is compared to three, it fills out the <term>diatessaron</term> <note>the interval of a fourth</note> consonance. This is the order of these, with all compared to one another in turn. For what comparison remains? If we compare four to two, it will fall into the double proportion which the two held when compared to the unit. And so, sounds are most distant in the <term>bisdiapason</term>, since they depart from each other by a fourfold dimension of interval. Sounds seem to be minimally consonant with each other unless the sharper [higher] note transcends the heavier [lower] by a triple proportion. And from then on stands the mode of those to be generated, which can neither be extended beyond the quadruple, nor narrowed within the third part. And according to Nicomachus, this is indeed the order of consonances: so that the first is the <term>diapason</term>, the second the <term>diapason and diapente</term>, the third the <term>bisdiapason</term>, the fourth the <term>diapente</term>, and the fifth the <term>diatessaron</term>.

### On the order of consonances according to the opinion of Eubulides and Hippasus. Chapter 19.

Eubulides and Hippasus <note>early Pythagorean philosophers</note> propose another order of consonances. For [they say] either the increases of multiplicity or the decreases of superparticularity respond in a fixed order. Therefore, a double [ratio] cannot be...

<keywords>arithmetic mean, geometric mean, harmonic mean, music theory, Pythagoras, Nicomachus, diapason, diapente, diatessaron, mathematical proportions, musical consonances</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical exploration of musical intervals, specifically transitioning from general proportional theory to the specific system proposed by the 2nd-century mathematician Nicomachus of Gerasa. It explains how the fundamental intervals—the octave, fifth, and fourth—are derived from the division of ratios.</meta>

...except for the half; nor is the triple proportion formed without the third <margin>part</margin>. Since that which is a double ratio renders the consonance of the **diapason** <note>original: "diapason"; the interval of an octave (2:1 ratio)</note>. Since, indeed, the half of it [the double], by a kind of continuous division, produces the **sesquialter** <note>a ratio of 3:2, where one number contains another plus its half</note>, that is the **diapente** <note>original: "diapente"; the interval of a perfect fifth</note> proportion. Since when both are joined—namely the octave and the fifth—the triple proportion is procreated <note>3/1 = 2/1 x 3/2</note>, both of which are continued through consonance. But again, the triple, by continuous division of its third part, gives birth to the **diatesseron** <note>original: "diatesseron"; the interval of a perfect fourth (4:3 ratio)</note> consonance. Indeed, the triple joined with a certain **sesquitertius** <note>original: "sesq̃uicentūm", likely a scribe's error for "sesquitertium"; a 4:3 ratio</note> produces the quadruple proportion by its operation <note>4/1 = 3/1 x 4/3</note>. Whence it happens that from the octave and the fifth (which is one consonance) and the fourth (another consonance) joined together, the **bisdiapason** <note>original: "bisdiapason"; the double octave (4:1 ratio)</note> receives its name, consisting in the quadruple. According to these, this is the order: **Octave**, **Fifth**, **Octave-and-Fifth**, **Fourth**, **Double Octave**. 

### **Summary of Nicomachus: Which proportions are opposed to which consonances. Chapter 20.**

**B**ut Nicomachus does not believe that the reasoning for their position is the same. Instead, he posits that just as "unity" in arithmetic arguments was the principle of all divisions, so also the octave consonance is the principle of the others. He believes that the others can be established from it in continuous division. This will be easier to understand if it is first previewed in numbers. Therefore, let unity [1] be established, and let two parts flow from it: one of multiplicity <margin>half / 1</margin> and the other of division. And let this be the <margin>diagram.</margin> diagram:

->I<-
->III  IIII  V<-
->sixth part / fourth / fifth<-

And in this way, there is an infinite progression. For the number two is the double of unity. Its continuous part shows the dividing of unity [the half]. Three is the triple, and the third part corresponds to the triple. Four is the quadruple, and the [fourth] part...

<column-break/>

...the eighth [is] the fourth <note>The text here discusses the reciprocal relationship between whole numbers and fractions</note>; and so the matter yields and holds its principle in simple unity. Let us convert this same logic to the consonances. Therefore, the **diapason** [octave], which is double, will be in the place of the highest principle. Those which remain are in continuous division in this way: the **sesquialter** [3:2] to the triple [3:1], and the **sesquitertius** [4:3] to the quadruple [4:1]. This will be proved by the following argument. 

For the first sesquialter is the same as the first triple, but relative to the principal unity. For the number three is the first triple if compared to unity [3:1]; the same number three is the first sesquialter if it is compared to the number two [3:2]. Again, that same three, by the difference it makes when compared to two [a difference of 1], is proved to be both a sesquialter and a triple. Since, therefore, the sesquialter is placed in relation to the triple, the **diapente** [fifth] consonance is reasonably thought to be opposed to the **diapason-plus-diapente** [octave-plus-fifth] consonance. Again, the quadruple ratio contains the sesquitertius in continuous division. For that which is the first quadruple is found to be the first sesquitertius in this way: the number four is the first quadruple if compared to unity [4:1], and the first sesquitertius if compared to the number three [4:3]. Again, by the difference it holds with the number three [a difference of 1], it becomes the quadruple. Whence it happens that the sesquitertia proportion, which is the **diatesseron** [fourth], is divided into the quadruple proportion, which is the **bisdiapason** [double octave]. But the double, since it has no opposite proportion <note>in this specific mathematical sequence</note>, nor is it the "sesqui-" of any other number; nor is there a number that can be joined to the number two (which is the first double) in a <term>superparticular proportion</term> <note>a ratio where the numerator is exactly one greater than the denominator, like 3/2 or 4/3</note>. He hands down such a form of these proportions. And for that reason, according to Nicomachus, the octave consonance holds the principle in this manner:

->**DIAPASON** [Octave]<-
->**DIAPENTE** [Fifth] — **DIAPENTE** [Fifth]<-
->**BISDIAPASON** [Double Octave] — **DIATESSERON** [Fourth]<-

<summary>This page explains how the fundamental musical intervals—the octave, fifth, and fourth—are mathematically derived from one another through ratios, citing Nicomachus's theory that the octave (2:1) is the source of all other musical proportions.</summary>
<keywords>Nicomachus, octave, perfect fifth, perfect fourth, double octave, musical ratios, sesquialter, sesquitertius, music theory, Boethius</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical and harmonic theories of the 6th-century philosopher Boethius, following his discussion of the Greek mathematician Nicomachus. The previous page established the hierarchical order of intervals; this page begins the formal proof that the octave (diapason) belongs to the "multiple" category of ratios, while the fifth and fourth belong to the "superparticular" category.</meta>

But although this is the case, he says: it is better that all multiple proportions precede the consonances, and he allows the superparticulars to follow, as we described a little earlier. Since a **consonance** <note>original: "consonantia"; a pleasing combination of two notes</note> is a fixed mixture of two sounds, and **Sound** <note>original: "Sonus"</note> is a melodic inflection of the voice produced in a single pitch, it is the same as the smallest particle of melody. Indeed, all sound consists in a strike. Every strike comes from motion. Of motions, some are equal, others are unequal. Of the unequal ones, some are greatly unequal, others less so, and others are moderately unequal. From equality, an equality of sounds is born. From inequality, those which are distant according to a middle ground are unequal. It is manifest that the more primary and simple proportions are those which are **multiple** <note>original: "multiplices"; ratios like 2:1, 3:1, where the larger number is an exact multiple of the smaller</note> and **superparticular** <note>original: "superparticulares"; ratios like 3:2 or 4:3, where the larger contains the smaller plus one part of it</note>. The Double, Triple, and Quadruple, and the Sesquialter and Sesquitertius are the consonances. However, those which are in the remaining proportions—being in many forms, or not so closed, or distant by a long way—I consider to be unequal **dissonances**, for they produce no concord of sounds.

### XXI. What must be premised so that the octave may be demonstrated in the multiple genus.

This having been so distinguished, it will be demonstrated that the **diapason** <note>original: "diapason"; the interval of an octave</note> consonance—since it is the best of all—is found in the multiple genus of inequality and in the relationship of "doubleness" <note>the 2:1 ratio</note>. And first indeed, it must be shown how the octave consonance can be recognized in the genus of multiplicity. We must run through a certain brief point, which, once known first, will make the demonstration easier. From any superparticularity, if one takes away a continuous superparticular proportion—that is to say, the smaller one—that which is left over is less than half of the proportion that was taken away. As in the **sesquialter** <note>3:2 ratio; the perfect fifth</note> and **sesquitertius** <note>4:3 ratio; the perfect fourth</note>: since the sesquialter is larger, and the sesquitertius is taken away from the sesquialter, the **sesquioctave** <note>9:8 ratio; the whole tone</note>

<column-break/>

proportion is left over. This [the whole tone], when doubled, does not produce a full sesquiseptima <note>8:7 ratio</note> proportion, but is less than that distance which is found in a **semitone** <note>original: "semitonio"; a half-step</note>. Since if I compare a doubled sesquioctave, it is not a full sesquiseptima. A single sesquioctave is not a full half of a sesquiseptima proportion. But if you take a sesquioctave away from the sesquitertius, that which is left over will not produce half of a sesquioctave. And the same applies to the rest.

### XXII. Demonstration by [way of] impossibility that the octave is in the multiple genus.

Let us return to the consonance of the **diapason** <note>the octave</note>. If it is not in the multiple genus of inequality, it must fall into the superparticular genus of inequality. Let the octave consonance, therefore, be a superparticular proportion. If a continuous consonance is taken from it—that is, the **diapente** <note>original: "diapenthe"; the perfect fifth</note>—the **diatesseron** <note>the perfect fourth</note> is left over. In this case, the fourth is less than one fifth. And the fourth itself does not fill half of the fifth consonance. This is impossible. For it will be shown that two fourths exceed the consonance of a fifth by a tone and a semitone. Therefore, not even the octave can be placed in the superparticular genus of inequality.

### XXIII. Demonstration that the Fifth, the Fourth, and the Tone are in the superparticular [genus].

It remains, therefore, that we must show that the **diapente** <note>fifth</note>, the **diatesseron** <note>fourth</note>, and the **tone** are to be placed in superparticularity. For even if we showed in the first proof that the octave is not to be placed in the superparticular genus, and that also became clear, let us nevertheless treat it individually and more diligently. For if one does not consider that these relationships should be placed in the superparticular genus, he will admit they must be placed in the multiple genus. For if they cannot be composed in the **superpartient** <note>ratios like 5:3, where the larger contains the smaller plus more than one part</note> or other mixed [genera], it has, I believe, been explained that they must be "super." Let it be placed, therefore, if possible, in the multiple genus. And since the fourth consonance is smaller and the fifth is larger...

<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration regarding the classification of musical intervals into specific categories of ratios. Having previously discussed the octave, the author now uses logical proofs to determine the correct mathematical genus for the fourth and the fifth.</meta>

the **fourth** <note>original: "diatesseron"; an interval spanning four notes</note> to the double [2:1]. Let the **fifth** <note>original: "diapenthe"; an interval spanning five notes</note> indeed be adapted to the triple [3:1] proportion of multiplicity. For it is probable that just as the **consonance** <note>original: "consonantia"; a pleasing combination of tones</note> of the fourth is continuous with the resonance of the fifth, so if the fourth is established in the double [2:1], the fifth should be placed in the next continuous double, which is the triple [3:1]. But since the **tone** <note>original: "tonus"; the basic whole-step interval</note> is located after the fourth in musical relations, it should certainly be placed in that proportion which is less than the double [2:1]. However, such a ratio cannot be found in the **genus of multiplicity** <note>original: "multiplicitatis gnē"; ratios like 2:1, 3:1, where the larger number is a whole multiple of the smaller</note>. It remains, therefore, that it falls into the **superparticular** <note>original: "superparticulariabus"; ratios like 3:2 or 4:3, where the larger contains the smaller plus one part of it</note> relations. Let it be, then, the first [ratio], which is the **sesquialter** <note>original: "sesqualtera"; a 3:2 ratio</note> proportion for the tone. For if we take away the triple [3:1] from the double [2:1], what remains is the sesquialter [3:2]. But if the fourth is indeed the leader [2:1] and the fifth is the triple [3:1], once the fourth is subtracted from the fifth, the tone is what remains; there is no way around the fact that the tone must then be established in the sesquialter [3:2] proportion. But two sesquialter proportions exceed the double [2:1], as anyone can gather for themselves by arithmetic principles. Therefore, two tones would exceed the fourth, which is inconsistent. For the fourth consists of two tones and the space of a **semitone** <note>original: "semitonij"</note>. Therefore, it cannot be that the fifth and the fourth are placed in the genus measured by superparticulars. But if someone should establish that the tone is also in the multiple genus—since indeed the tone is smaller than the fourth, and the fourth is smaller than the fifth—let the fifth be placed in the quadruple [4:1], the fourth in the triple [3:1], and the tone in the double [2:1]. But the fifth consists of the fourth and a tone. Therefore, according to this reasoning, the quadruple [4:1] would consist of the triple [3:1] and the double [2:1], which cannot be. Again, let the fourth be established in the triple [3:1] and the fifth in the quadruple [4:1]. If we then take the triple away from the quadruple, the **sesquioctave** <note>original: "sesquioctavus"; a 9:8 ratio</note> remains. Again, if you subtract the fourth from the consonance of the fifth, the remaining part is a tone. The tone, therefore, according to this reasoning, would stand in the sesquioctave [9:8] proportion. But three

<column-break/>

sesquioctaves [9:8] are less than one triple [3:1]. Therefore, three tones would by no means fulfill one fourth, which is entirely false. For two tones and a minor semitone fulfill the resonance of the fourth. From these points, therefore, it is demonstrated that the consonance of the fourth is not of the multiple genus. I say also that the consonance of the fifth cannot be placed in the multiple genus. For if the consonance smaller than it and continuous with it—that is, the fourth—is not so placed, the fifth will not be placed in the smallest multiple, which is the double [2:1], specifically so that the tone might be adapted to the fourth consonance. But the consonance of the fourth is not of the multiple genus. Therefore, neither can the fifth be adapted to a larger multiple relation than the double [2:1], which is the smallest. Let, then, the fifth be in the smallest [multiple], the double [2:1]; the fourth, which is smaller, certainly cannot be adapted to the multiple genus, for there is nothing [in that genus] smaller than the double. Let, then, the fourth be the sesquialter [3:2] and the tone be the **sesquitertia** <note>original: "sesquitertia"; a 4:3 ratio</note>. For it will be located in the continuous proportion. But two measures of the sesquitertia exceed one sesquialter. Therefore, two tones would exceed one consonance of the fourth, which happens by no logic. From these things, then, it is proven that the fifth and the fourth cannot be placed in the multiple genus, since they are not found in that genus of inequality.

### Demonstration that the fifth and the fourth are in the greatest superparticulars. XXIV.

This must also necessarily be added: that if the fifth and the fourth hold superparticular proportions, they are located in the greatest superparticular proportions. These are the sesquialter [3:2] and the sesquitertia [4:3]. This is proven in this manner: for if the consonances of the fifth and the fourth were placed in smaller proportions than the sesquialter or sesquitertia, there is no doubt that there would be any other superparticular proportions

It demonstrates that assigning them multiple ratios leads to mathematical results that contradict the known physical structure of scales.</summary>
