<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration of musical intervals. The previous page discussed the classification of ratios; here, the author specifically maps the fifth (diapente) to the 3:2 ratio and the fourth (diatesseron) to the 4:3 ratio, proving how they combine to form the octave (diapason).</meta>

...except for the **sesquialter** <term>sesquialtera; a 3:2 ratio where the larger number contains the smaller plus its half</term> and the **sesquitertian** <term>sesquitertia; a 4:3 ratio where the larger number contains the smaller plus its third</term> joined together, no others produce one **double** <note>original: "duplum"; a 2:1 ratio</note> ratio. In the same way, the **fifth** <note>original: "diapente"</note> and the **fourth** <note>original: "diatesseron"</note> will by no other logic conclude one **octave** <note>original: "diapason"</note>. For since it has been demonstrated that the octave exists in a double proportion, and a double proportion is composed of a sesquialter and a sesquitertian, and the octave is joined from the fourth and the fifth, there is no doubt that if the whole octave is established as a double, the fifth and the fourth must be placed in the sesquialter and sesquitertian proportions. Otherwise, the joined octave could not be completed, since the **consonance** <note>original: "symphoniam"</note> exists in a double proportion—unless it stands upon these two proportions, namely the sesquialter and the sesquitertian. For no other **superparticular** <note>original: "superparticulares"; ratios of the form (n+1)/n</note> proportions can be joined in this way. The fifth is in the sesquialter [3:2], the fourth is in the sesquitertian [4:3], and the **tone** <note>original: "tonum"</note> is in the **sesquioctave** <term>sesquioctaua; a 9:8 ratio, the mathematical value of a whole step</term>.

### Chapter XXV.

**W**hy every fifth consists in a sesquialter ratio, and every fourth in a sesquitertian ratio. Since between both proportions, the sesquialter and the sesquitertian, the sesquialter is larger and the sesquitertian is smaller, and since among the consonances the fifth is larger and the fourth is smaller, it is apparent that the larger proportion should be adapted to the larger consonance. Therefore, the fifth must be placed in the sesquialter, while the fourth must be placed in the sesquitertian proportion. If we subtract the consonance of the fourth from the fifth, the remaining space is what is called a **tone** <note>In musical terms: (3/2) ÷ (4/3) = 9/8</note>. If a sesquitertian is taken from a sesquialter proportion, a sesquioctave [9:8] proportion remains. Thus it follows that the tone must be established in the sesquioctave comparison.

### Chapter XXVI.

<column-break/>

### That the octave and the fifth are in a triple ratio, and the double octave is in a quadruple ratio.

**B**ut since it has been demonstrated that the octave is indeed double [2:1] and the fifth is sesquialter [3:2], one may see that the double and the sesquialter create a **triple** <note>2 * 1.5 = 3</note> proportion. From this, it appears that the fifth with the octave is established in a triple ratio. But if someone joins a sesquitertian [4:3] relation to a triple [3:1] proportion, it creates a **quadruple** <note>3 * 4/3 = 4</note> one. Therefore, if the consonance of the fourth is joined to the consonances of the fifth and octave, a quadruple proportion is made. This was shown above to be the **double octave** <note>original: "bis diapason"</note>. That the fourth and the octave together are not consonances according to the **Pythagoreans** <note>followers of Pythagoras who defined music through strict mathematical ratios</note>.

### Chapter XXVII.

**I**n these matters, let the diligent reader recognize that consonances placed upon other consonances create certain other consonances. For the fifth and the fourth joined together summon the octave, as has been said. And it has been said that if the consonance of the octave is again joined to a fifth, a consonance is made which is named from both words: the **octave-plus-fifth**. If a fourth is added to this, it becomes the double octave, which holds the quadruple proportion. What then if we join a fourth and an octave? Will they produce no consonance according to the Pythagoreans? Not at all. For it immediately falls into the **superpartient** <term>superpartiens; a complex ratio where the larger number contains the smaller plus more than one part of it, such as 8:3</term> type of inequality. It preserves neither the order of **multiplicity** <note>simple multiples like 2:1, 3:1</note> nor the simplicity of **superparticularity** <note>ratios like 3:2, 4:3</note>. Come now, let a number be set down by which we may more easily prove this. Let there be the number three, or let there be six, which is its double, standing in the proportion of an octave. To this let the sesquitertian be adapted, which we previously said was the fourth. This would be the number eight, for eight holds the sesquitertian proportion to six. When this eight is compared to three, it contains it twice, but so that it is not a simple multiple, it also contains some of its parts, and those are not simple. For it contains it with two [remaining]...

<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical proof of musical intervals. The previous page established that the octave is composed of a fifth and a fourth; here, the author examines the relationship between the octave and other combined intervals, specifically why the octave-plus-fourth (an 8:3 ratio) was not considered a "consonance" by the Pythagorean school, unlike the octave-plus-fifth (a 3:1 ratio).</meta>

In the highest orders, there are two thirds of the triple ratio <note>original: "triarij", referring to the *triplus* or 3:1 ratio</note> which we diminish in the place of the first term. Let these three terms be 3, 6, 8. This measurement also falls into continuous **consonances** <note>original: "cosonancias"; intervals that sound pleasing together</note>. For it is neither exactly double [2:1] nor a complete triple [3:1] so as to produce the **octave-plus-fifth** <note>original: "diapason ac diapenthe"</note> consonance. If a **tone** <note>original: "tonus"; a whole step, 9:8 ratio</note> is added to it, it will immediately produce the triple mode of proportion. For since the octave and the fifth joined together produce a triple ratio, the fourth and the tone joined together make a fifth. If the octave consonance is added to a fourth, an "inconsonance" <note>The Pythagoreans excluded the octave-plus-fourth from the primary consonances because its mathematical ratio (8:3) is neither "multiple" nor "superparticular."</note> will result, because between the double and the triple, no natural multiple proportion can be understood. But if I add a tone to it, it becomes an octave, a fourth, and a tone—which is undoubtedly an octave and a fifth. For the fourth and the tone together constitute a fifth.

For let the octave be 4 and 2; the fourth 6 and 8; and the tone 8 and 9. The fifth is 6 and 9. The octave and the fifth together are 4 and 9 <note>In this mathematical proof, the ratios are built through these specific numerical terms to show how they nest within each other.</note>. Therefore, there will be this triple proportion: 4, 6, 8, 9. But although **Nicomachus** <note>Nicomachus of Gerasa, a mathematician whose work on music Boethius adapted</note> said much about these things, we have nevertheless proved them with what brevity we can—partly by setting down those things which **Pythagoras** asserts, and partly by arguing certain consequences from them. We have proved that if the octave is added to the consonance of the fourth, a consonance cannot be joined from them. What **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy, a later scientist who argued that the octave-plus-fourth *should* be considered a consonance</note> thinks of these matters I will set down later. But enough of these things for now.

# On the numbers in which the semitone
->consists. Chapter XXVIII<-

**N**ow we must consider the **semitones** <note>original: "semitoniis"</note>. They are seen to be named semitones not because they are truly half-tones, but because they are not full, integral tones. This space, which we now call a semitone, was called by the ancients either a **limma** <note>original: "limina"</note> or a **diesis** <term>diesis; here meaning the "leftover" interval in a 4:3 ratio after two whole tones are removed</term>.

<column-break/>

This is the method: when two **sesquioctave** <note>9:8 ratio, a whole tone</note> relations, which are tones, are taken away from the **sesquitertian** <note>4:3 ratio, a musical fourth</note> proportion which is the **fourth** <note>diatesseron</note>, the space that remains is called a semitone. Let us therefore seek two tones described in a continuous arrangement. But since these, as was said, consist in a sesquioctave proportion, we cannot apply two continuous sesquioctave proportions unless that multiple from which they can be derived is found. Let the unit be 1, of which the first "eight-fold" <note>octuplus</note> is 8. From this, therefore, you can derive one sesquioctave. But because we seek two, let it be eight times eight, and thus 64 is produced. This will be the second "eight-fold" number from which we can draw out two sesquioctave portions. For 8, which is the eighth part of 64, added to that same sum makes a total of 72. To this, let its own eighth part similarly be added, which is 9; they yield 81. These two tones [64 to 72 and 72 to 81] will therefore be written in a continuous arrangement.

From here, let us seek a sesquitertian ratio [4:3] to the terms 64, 72, and 81. But since 81 is found not to have a third part, if all these numbers are multiplied by three, a third part will soon be available to them. And all will remain in the same proportion they were in before this tripling factor was added. Thus, three times 64 becomes 192. A third of these, 64, added to them, will yield 256. This will therefore be the sesquitertian proportion [256:192] holding the consonance of the fourth. Now, let me place the two sesquioctave proportions to 192 in the proper order. Thus, three times 72 becomes 216; again, three times 81 is 243. These are placed between the two terms written above: first 192, then 216, then 243, and finally 256. In this arrangement of proportions, every number...

<meta>This page continues Boethius’s mathematical breakdown of musical intervals. Having previously discussed how consonances like the fourth and fifth are constructed from ratios, he now moves into a specific, famous problem in Pythagorean tuning: the fact that a "semitone" is not actually exactly half of a whole tone.</meta>

The last part occupies the consonance of the **fourth** <note>original: "diatessaron"; the interval of a perfect fourth, defined by the ratio 4:3</note>. Therefore, the first to the second and the second to the third terms constitute two full **tones** <note>original: "tonos"; a whole step, defined by the ratio 9:8</note>. It is therefore established that the space remaining between 243 and 256 constitutes the smallest form of a **semitone** <note>original: "semitonii"; literally "half-tone," though Boethius is about to prove it is not exactly half</note>.

### Chapter 29: Demonstrations that the ratio of 243 to 256 is not half of a tone.

I shall now prove that the distance between 243 and 256 is not the measure of an exact half of a tone. For the distance between 243 and 256 is contained within only 13 units. These 13 units are less than an eighteenth part of the smaller number [243], but more than a nineteenth part. For if you multiply 13 by eighteen, you get 234, which in no way equals 243. If you multiply by nineteen, the result [247] will exceed it. 

A true semitone—if it is to hold the place of an exact half of a tone—ought to be located between a sixteenth and seventeenth part <note>This refers to a complex mathematical proof in Pythagorean theory showing that a 9:8 ratio cannot be split into two equal rational ratios; a "half" would need to fall within these specific fractional bounds</note>, as will be demonstrated later. For now, it will be clear that such a semitone distance, when doubled, cannot complete the full space of a single tone. 

Consider how 256 relates to 243. Let us arrange two such continuous proportions according to the rule described above. We multiply 256 and 243 by themselves. Let the largest term be 65,536 [256 x 256]. Likewise, let the smallest term grow from the smaller number: 59,049 [243 x 243]. 

Again, let the middle term grow by the magnitude of 256 times 243. This middle term will be 62,208. Let the middle be placed in this manner:

| 65536 | 62208 | 59049 |

The ratio between 65,536 and 62,208 is the same as that between 256 and 243. And likewise, the ratio between 62,208 and 59,049 is the same. 

<column-break/>

However, the ratio of the largest term, 65,536, to the smallest, 59,049, does not make one complete **tone** <note>a ratio of 9:8</note>. If the ratio of the first term to the second, and the second to the third, were proven to be a true half-semitone, then two such halves joined together would necessarily produce one full tone. 

But since the ratio of these outer terms is not a **sesquioctave** <note>original: "sesquioctava"; the ratio 9:8, which defines a whole tone</note>, it is manifest that these two spaces are not truly halves of tones. For whatever is divided into two parts, if doubled, must produce the thing of which it was the half. If, when doubled, the part cannot fulfill the whole, that piece is less than a half. If it overflows and exceeds the whole, it is more than a half.

Furthermore, it will be proven that 65,536 is surpassed by a true sesquioctave ratio [of 59,049] by 8,494 units. If an eighth part of 59,049 is added to itself according to the rules taught in Arithmetic—which does not result in whole numbers—we leave the calculation of that eighth part to the diligence of the readers. It is clear, therefore, that the ratio established between 256 and 243 is not a true half of a tone. Because of this, it is called a "minor semitone" <note>often called a "limma" in Greek music theory</note>, as it is a part of a tone smaller than a half.

### Chapter 30: Concerning the larger part of the tone and the numbers it consists of.

The remaining part of the tone, which is the larger part, is called an **apotome** <note>original: "apotome"; Greek for "a cutting off" or "remainder"</note> by the Greeks. By us, it can be called a **cutting** <note>original: "decisio"</note>. Nature dictates that whenever something is cut into unequal parts, however much the smaller part is less than a half, the larger part exceeds the middle by that same amount. Since the minor semitone...

<meta>This page continues Boethius’s mathematical proof regarding musical intervals. On the previous page, he established that a "minor semitone" (the limma) is not exactly half of a whole tone. Here, he introduces the "apotome" and begins to prove that an octave does not consist of six whole tones, countering the theories of Aristoxenus.</meta>

...it is less than a whole half of a tone; for the **apotome** <term>apotome: from the Greek for "a cutting off"; in Pythagorean tuning, this is the larger "half-step" that remains when a minor semitone is subtracted from a whole tone (9:8 ÷ 256:243 = 2187:2048)</term> exceeds the half of a whole tone, and when joined [to the minor semitone, they make a full tone]. And since the **semitone** <note>original: "semitonium"; specifically the minor semitone or 'limma'</note> consists principally in the ratio of 256 to 243, let us now prove in what smallest numbers that which is called an **apotome** can consist.

If the number 243 could be divided into eight parts—since a **sesquioctave** <note>original: "sesquioctauus"; the ratio 9:8, which defines a whole tone</note> number is compared to it—then the relationship of 243 to its sesquioctave sum would have shown the **apotome** using the smallest possible numbers. But now, since it is shown that it lacks an eighth part, let these numbers be multiplied by eight: from 243 multiplied eight times, the number becomes 1,944 <note>original: "m .dcccclxiiii"; the OCR reads 1964, but the mathematical logic of the text refers to 1944 (243 x 8)</note>. If its own eighth part is added to this (which is 243), it becomes 2,187 <note>original: "ii .clxxxvii"</note>.

Conversely, let 256 increase by eight; it becomes 2,048 <note>original: "ii .xlviii"</note>. And let these aforementioned terms be placed in the middle: 

->1,944; 2,048; 2,187<-

The third term [2,187] therefore retains the proportion of a **tone** [9:8] relative to the first [1,944]. But the second [2,048] to the first [1,944] holds the proportion of a **minor semitone** <note>ratio 256:243</note>. The **apotome** of the tone, however, is the ratio of the third to the second [2,187:2,048]. And in these very numbers, the proportion of the **apotome** is seen to consist. 

Since the space of a semitone is contained in the smallest numbers 256 and 243, then 1,944 and 2,048 are in that same proportion, because 256 and 243 were both multiplied by eight. For if one number multiplies any two numbers, the results of that multiplication will be in the same proportion as the original numbers. By these proportions, the **fifth** <note>diapente</note> and the **octave** <note>diapason</note> are established; and [we shall see] why the **octave** does not consist of six [whole] tones.

## 31. But since we have spoken more broadly of the **fourth** <note>diatessaron</note>...

...let us discuss more briefly and with nearly pure numbers the consonances of the **octave** <column-break/> and the **fifth**. For the **fifth** consists of three tones and a semitone; that is, of a **fourth** and a tone. 

Let those numbers be arranged which the previous description provided. In this arrangement: 192, 216, 243, 256. The first term to the second, and the second to the third, retain the proportions of **tones** [9:8]. But the third to the fourth is the proportion of a **minor semitone**, as was shown above. 

If, then, an eighth part is added to 256 <note>256 + 32 = 288</note>, it becomes 288. Compared to the first term [192], it produces the space of a **sesquialter** <term>sesquialter: the ratio 3:2, which defines the interval of a perfect fifth</term> proportion. In this sequence, there are tones if the first is compared to the second, the second to the third, and the fifth [288] to the fourth [256]. The minor semitone, however, holds the comparison of the third term to the fourth. 

Since the **fourth** is made of two tones and a minor semitone, and the **fifth** of three tones and a minor semitone joined together—and just as the **fourth** and **fifth** together create one **octave**—the octave will contain five tones and two minor semitones. These two semitones do not fulfill the space of one whole tone <note>Because two minor semitones are less than one 9:8 tone; the difference is called a Pythagorean comma</note>.

Therefore, the **octave** consonance is not composed of six [whole] tones, as **Aristoxenus** <note>Aristoxenus (4th c. BCE) argued that the ear should judge intervals and that a tone could be divided into equal halves, a view Boethius and the Pythagoreans rejected through math</note> believes. This is clearly apparent in the arrangement of the numbers. For let six tones be arranged in order according to the **sesquioctave** [9:8] proportions. To do this, six sesquioctave proportions are calculated starting from the sixth power of eight <note>To avoid fractions when calculating six consecutive 9:8 ratios, one must start with $8^6$</note>. Let these six "eight-folds" be arranged in this manner:

| 1 | 8 | 64 | 512 | 4,096 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 32,768 | 262,144 | | | |

Under this arrangement, from this last number [262,144], six tones will be calculated in sesquioctave proportion. With the powers of eight arranged first as the base, the two octaves will be joined at the ends. Let the description be as follows:

<note>This table calculates the "comma." It starts with a base number and multiplies it by 8 six times (the "octuples"). Then, it calculates six successive "sesquioctave" ratios (9:8, or whole tones). The resulting number, 531,440 (should be 531,441), is then compared to the octave of the starting number.</note>

The arrangement of this type is as follows: for the continuous line which is called the path of **octuples** <note>original: "octuplorum"; numbers in a 1:8 ratio</note> contains these numbers. Indeed, from the sixth octuple, **sesquioctave** <term>sesquioctave: the ratio 9:8, which represents a whole musical tone</term> proportions are derived. Where we have written the "eighth parts" adjacent to them, those are the eighth parts of the numbers they are next to. If these eighth parts are added to those same numbers they are adjacent to, they create the subsequent numbers. 

For example, in the first, which is 262,144, its eighth part is 32,768. If these are joined together, they produce the subsequent number which is 294,912 <note>The OCR reads 294,880, likely a transcription error for 294,912</note>. Thus it is found at the extremes: if the final number, which is 531,440 <note>The mathematically correct number is 531,441 (9 to the 6th power)</note>, is compared to the double of the first number (which is 262,144), the **octave** <note>original: "diapason"</note> seems to consist of exactly six tones. 

But now, if we seek the smallest number—that is, the double of the first—it will be smaller than that number which is supreme and greatest. For the double of 262,144, which would be the octave consonant with it, is 524,288 <note>original: "dxxv. cclxxxviii"; the OCR reads 525,288, but the math requires 524,288</note>. This, therefore, is smaller than that number which holds the sixth tone, namely 531,440. Therefore, the **octave consonance** is smaller than six tones. And that amount by which six tones exceed the octave consonance, they call a **comma** <term>comma: a tiny interval (roughly 1/9th of a tone) that is the mathematical "leftover" when comparing six whole tones to one octave</term>, which consists in the smallest numbers 531,441 and 524,288. But regarding what Aristoxenus <note>Aristoxenus (4th c. BCE), a theorist who argued that musical intervals should be measured by the ear rather than mathematical ratios</note> thinks of these things—he who gave all judgment to the ears—I will mention elsewhere; now, to avoid weariness, I shall tighten the series of this volume.

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion of mathematical ratios, moving into Book 3, Chapter 7. Here, Boethius begins his famous refutation of Aristoxenus, arguing that musical intervals must be governed by mathematical laws rather than just the human ear.</meta>

<margin>7.</margin>
**I**n the previous volume <note>referring to Book 2 of Boethius’s *Fundamentals of Music*</note>, it was demonstrated that the **fourth** <term>diatessaron: the musical interval of a perfect fourth</term> consists of two whole tones and a semitone. The **fifth** <term>diapente: the musical interval of a perfect fifth</term>, however, is composed of three tones and a semitone—but those semitones cannot achieve a full half of a whole tone if they are considered and treated individually. And for that reason, the **octave** <term>diapason: the interval of an eighth</term> in no way reaches the span of six whole tones. But since Aristoxenus the Musician, leaving all things to the judgment of the ears, does not believe these semitones to be smaller than a half (as the followers of Pythagoras do), but rather that they are true halves of tones—just as the name "semitone" implies—we must once again dispute these claims for a moment. We must first demonstrate that no **superparticular** <term>superparticular: a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller number plus one part of it, such as 3:2 or 9:8</term> relationship can be divided into a whole half by a known number. For between two numbers that produce a superparticular portion—whether they are close to each other so that their difference is a single unit, or whether they are larger—no middle number can be placed in such a way that it holds the smallest ratio to the middle as the middle holds to the extreme, as occurs in a **geometric proportion** <note>original: "geometrica proportione"; a sequence where each term is multiplied by the same factor</note>. Instead, one can either create equal differences according to an **arithmetic mean**, or one will create a **harmonic** mean placed between those same numerical terms, or some other kind besides the arithmetic or geometric. If this is demonstrated, then that other claim cannot stand: namely, that the **sesquioctave** <term>sesquioctave: the 9:8 ratio, which defines a whole musical tone</term> proportion can be divided into two equal halves. For every sesquioctave consists of a superparticular type of inequality. This will be shown better on the **monochord** <note>a single-stringed instrument used for measuring musical intervals</note>. For if, after a consideration of every individual superparticular proportion is carried out, absolutely none is found that can be divided into equal proportions by placing a middle term between them, there is no doubt that a superparticu-

<column-break/>

lar comparison cannot be partitioned into equal parts. But if something seems to sound to the ears as being distant by two tones and a full semitone, and it is compared to the truth, it is shown that this is not in harmony with nature. But since all the senses are unable to grasp the smallest things, the sense of the ears cannot distinguish this difference which lies beyond the predicted harmony. However, it will be caught if such a small part remains from the voice very frequently through the same steps. For that which is hardly discerned in its smallest form begins to be clearly seen when it becomes very large by being composed and joined together. 

From which proportion, therefore, should we begin? Or shall we provide a shortcut to the question if we start from the very thing being asked? That is: can a tone be divided into two equal parts or not? Now, therefore, we must treat the tone and demonstrate how it cannot be divided into two equal halves. If anyone transfers this demonstration to the remaining superparticular comparisons, he will similarly demonstrate that a superparticular ratio cannot be separated into equal parts by a known and whole number. First, the numbers that contain a tone are **8** and **9**. But since these follow each other in the natural order so that there is no whole number between them, I multiply them by two (which is the smallest multiplier I can use). They thus become **16** and **18**. Between these falls a natural number, which is **17**. Therefore, the ratio of 18 to 16 is a whole tone. But 18 compared to 17 contains the whole number plus its seventeenth part. However, a seventeenth part is naturally smaller than a sixteenth part. Therefore, the proportion contained under the numbers **16 and 17** is greater than that which is under **17 and 18**. Since they are arranged in this way, let 16 be **a**, 17 be **b**, and 18 be **c**. Therefore, the half...

<summary>Boethius argues against Aristoxenus's claim that a musical tone can be split into two equal halves, using a mathematical proof to show that superparticular ratios (like the 9:8 tone) cannot be divided into equal integer ratios.</summary>
<keywords>Aristoxenus, Pythagoras, musical ratios, semitone, superparticular ratio, monochord, arithmetic mean, geometric mean, 9:8 ratio, musical intervals</keywords>

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues Boethius’s mathematical proof against the theories of Aristoxenus. Having established that superparticular ratios (like the 9:8 ratio of a whole tone) cannot be divided into two equal parts using whole numbers, he now uses specific numerical examples (16, 17, and 18) to demonstrate that what we call "semitones" are actually unequal intervals.</meta>

...the exact half of the tone <note>original: "m\=a" for "medietas"</note> in no way falls from these numbers. For the ratio C–B is smaller than the ratio C–A. The midpoint must be placed toward the larger part. Let the midpoint be **D**. Since, therefore, the ratio D–B—which is half of the tone—is larger than the ratio C–B—which is the smaller part of the tone—then the ratio A–C—which is the larger part of the tone—is larger than the ratio A–D—which is half the tone. Now, the ratio A–C is **sesquisextadecima** <term>sesquisextadecima: the ratio 17:16, where the larger number contains the smaller plus its sixteenth part</term> and B–A <note>referring to the segment between 17 and 18</note> is **sesquiseptimadecima** <term>sesquiseptimadecima: the ratio 18:17, where the larger number contains the smaller plus its seventeenth part</term>. There is no doubt that the exact half falls between the **sesquisextadecima** and the **sesquiseptimadecima**. However, this exact middle point can in no way be found in a whole number.

->**[DIAGRAM]**<-
<note>The diagram illustrates the mathematical division of a whole tone (9:8, represented here as 18:16) into two unequal parts using the number 17 as a bridge.</note>
A circular diagram illustrating the mathematical division of a whole tone into semitones. A horizontal diameter is marked with points **A**, **C**, and **B**. Beneath these points are the Roman numerals **XVI** (16), **XVII** (17), and **XVIII** (18) respectively. Above the diameter, a small arc over the segment AC is labeled "the exact half of the tone," and a small arc over CB is labeled "lesser semitone" <note>original: "semitoniu\= minus"</note>. The top outer boundary of the circle is labeled "lesser semitone." Along the bottom outer boundary, the word **T O N E** <note>original: "T O N V S"</note> is written in spaced letters. Inside the lower hemisphere, the words "lesser semitone" are repeated.

But truly, when the number 17 is compared to the number 16, it holds a **sesquisextadecima** ratio. If we take a sixteenth part of the number 17, it is one unit plus a sixteenth part of a unit. If we add this to that same number 17, the result is 18 and a sixteenth part. Therefore, if 18 and a little sixteenth were compared to the number 17, it would seem to be the correct measure of the tone. Believe this: the number 18 contains a **sesquioctave** <term>sesquioctave: the ratio 9:8, which defines a whole tone in Pythagorean tuning</term> ratio. From this it follows that because the **sesquisextadecima** ratio exceeds the tone when doubled, it cannot be an exact half of a tone.
<meta>catchword: ta (referring to "transcendit")</meta>

<column-break/>

For whatever exceeds something when doubled will be seen to be beyond the half of that which it exceeds. Therefore, the **super-sesquisextadecima** <note>the ratio 17:16</note> will not be the half of a tone. And by this reasoning, no other ratio larger than the **sesquisextadecima** can be the half of a tone, since the **sesquisextadecima** itself is smaller than the exact half of a tone. 

But since the **sesquiseptimadecima** ratio follows immediately after the **sesquiseptima** <note>likely a scribal error for sesquisextadecima</note> ratio, let us see whether it fulfills the tone when multiplied by two. The boundary of the number 17 held by a **sesquiseptimadecima** part is 18. Therefore, if we compare another number to 18 in that same ratio, it will be 18 and a seventeenth part. But if we compare this to the boundary of 17 placed in the **sesquioctave** ratio <note>the 9:8 ratio</note>, the result would be 18 and an eighth part. Therefore, the ratio between the numbers 17 and 18-and-an-eighth is larger than the one contained between 17 and 18-and-a-seventeenth—which together are two **sesquiseptimadecima** ratios. 

Two **sesquiseptimadecima** ratios, therefore, do not seem to fill a whole tone. Thus, the **sesquiseptimadecima** is not the half of a tone, because those things which do not fill the whole when doubled do not contain the half. For a half, when doubled, is always made equal to that of which it is the half.

Now, if we arrange those numbers which are left over from the **sesquitertia** <term>sesquitertia: the ratio 4:3, which forms the musical interval of a fourth</term> after two whole tones have been subtracted, we can consider in these whether that ratio which remains after the two tones can be judged as the space of an exact semitone. If this is found to be so, it would also be proven that the **fourth** <term>diatessaron: the interval of a perfect fourth</term> is joined by two tones and an exact semitone. It was, therefore...

...the first term above. Indeed. For this **sesquitertian** ratio <term>sesquitertian: the ratio 4:3, which defines the musical interval of a perfect fourth</term> held the value 262,144. But compared to the first term, 248,832 makes a **tone** <term>tonus: a whole tone, defined by the ratio 9:8</term>. To 248,832, again 262,144 holds the place of a tone. Now, if this is proved again to be the half of a tone, then it cannot be doubted that the diatesseron consists of two tones and a semitone.

Since it has been demonstrated that the half-tone is located between the **sesquisextadecima** (17:16) and the **sesquiseptimadecima** (18:17) ratios, this ratio must also be measured by this comparison. So that we do not wander too far: I take from 262,144 the eighteenth part; that becomes 14,563 <gloss>and a half, as above</gloss>. If I add this to that same number, the result is 276,707 <gloss>and a half</gloss>. It appears, therefore, that the ratio of 276,707 to 262,144 is smaller in a **sesquioctavadecima** <term>sesquioctavadecima: the ratio 19:18</term> relationship. 

Because if the half-tone is indeed larger than a 17:16 ratio but smaller than an 18:17 ratio, then a 19:18 ratio is not smaller than an 18:17 relationship. Indeed, I compare 276,707 to 262,144—which is what remains of a diatesseron after two tones are pulled back—and it is smaller 

<column-break/>

than the 19:18 ratio: there is no doubt that the ratio of these two numbers is much smaller than a semitone.

### # Demonstrations against Aristoxenus that the perfect fourth does not consist of two tones and a semitone, nor the octave of six tones. Chapter Three.

<gloss>the matter is made equal</gloss>
**B**ut if, as Aristoxenus <note>Aristoxenus was a Greek music theorist who argued that musical intervals should be judged by the ear and human perception rather than strict mathematical ratios.</note> claims, the **diatesseron** <term>diatesseron: a perfect fourth</term> consonance is joined from two tones and a semitone, then two diatesserons would make five tones. And the **diapente** <term>diapente: a perfect fifth</term> and **diatesseron** joined together—just as they complete one **diapason** <term>diapason: a full octave</term>—so they would be joined in a continuous ratio of six tones. 

And those tones which we arranged a little earlier, of which the smallest number was 262,144. To this, indeed, the last number in the sixth tone was placed as 531,441. The fifth tone, indeed, held 472,396. Let them be arranged in this manner:

<summary>Boethius uses Pythagorean mathematics to prove that musical intervals like the perfect fourth do not perfectly align with the "common sense" divisions of Aristoxenus. He demonstrates that a semitone is not a true mathematical half of a whole tone, meaning an octave is not exactly six whole tones.</summary>
<keywords>Aristoxenus, diatesseron, perfect fourth, diapason, octave, Pythagorean tuning, mathematical ratios, semitone, sesquitertian</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues Boethius’s mathematical refutation of Aristoxenus, who believed musical intervals could be divided into equal halves by ear. Boethius uses massive numbers—powers of 2 and 3—to prove that a perfect fourth (diatesseron) is actually slightly larger than two tones and a half-tone, and that six whole tones exceed an octave (diapason) by a tiny mathematical fragment called a comma.</meta>

**Now, therefore**, we shall speak concerning our smaller numbers, that is, of **five tones**. If, therefore, a **diatesseron** <term>diatesseron: a perfect fourth, which Aristoxenus argued was exactly two and a half tones</term> consisted of two tones and a semitone, twice two **diatesserons** would not consist of five tones. And from 262,144 <note>original: "cclxii. cxliiii"</note> I intend a **diatesseron**. And when from 472,392 <note>original: "cccclxxii. cccxcii"</note> I remit another **diatesseron**, the same number would be found between both the intention and the remission. However, it happens in this way: from the number which is 262,144, I intend a **diatesseron**, that is, a **sesquitertia** <term>sesquitertia: the ratio 4:3</term> which results in 349,525 <gloss>3 fictive</gloss> <note>original: "finte," likely referring to fictive or fractional parts in the calculation</note>. Again, from the numbers 472,392, I remit a **sesquitertia** ratio, which results in 354,294. I shall therefore arrange these ratios <gloss>3 fictive</gloss> in this manner: let the first number be **A**, the second **B**, the third **C**, and the fourth **D**. Since, therefore, the term **A** is distant from term **D** by **five tones**, and since the **diatesseron** thrives in two tones and a semitone, as **Aristoxenus** <note>Aristoxenus of Tarentum was a Greek theorist who prioritized the human ear over mathematical ratios</note> thinks, there would never be one **diatesseron** between **A** and **B** and another between **C** and **D**. It is not necessary for terms **B** and **C** to be divided, but rather they should be one and the same, so that five tones would appear to consist of two combined **diatesserons**. But now, since the difference is 3,767 and two-thirds <gloss>two-thirds</gloss> <note>original: "bisse," a Roman term for two-thirds of a unit</note>, they prove that the **diatesseron** is by no means joined by two tones and a semitone.

<column-break/>

The **diapason** <term>diapason: the interval of an octave</term> consonance is exceeded by six tones by a **comma** <term>comma: the Pythagorean comma, a tiny interval (about 23.46 cents) that is the difference between twelve perfect fifths and seven octaves</term>. And let this be the smaller number of the comma. But clearly, I shall place this among the whole common numbers. For in that part which is placed as three, if it were added, it would make a full unit; which part is a third of the same, and its half is a **semis** <note>original: "ss," an abbreviation for "semis" or half-unit</note>. If I were to add a **tone** divided by its half, which is 2,384 and two-thirds <gloss>two-thirds</gloss> <note>original: "bisse"</note>, the sum of the tone becomes 7,153, which for a long time held the ratio of the **comma**. For the **comma** is that by which six tones exceed the **diapason** consonance. This is contained within the first 7,153 units. Therefore, as we add the half of them so that 7,153 might increase, so also let us join the proper halves to all terms **A**, **B**, **C**, and **D**, and the ratio will be the same in all as it was above. And there will be the same difference between **five tones** and two **diatesserons** as there is between **six tones** and the **diapason** consonance, namely, three of the 7,153 units. From which it is gathered that **five tones** exceed two **diatesserons**, and **six tones** exceed one **diapason** by only a **comma**, which is found in the first 7,153 units. The **description** placed on the following page will make this clear:

<meta>This page continues the mathematical exploration of musical intervals according to the Pythagorean tradition, specifically focusing on the calculations of Philolaus as preserved by Boethius.</meta>

# How Philolaus Divides the Tone
**P**hilolaus the Pythagorean <margin>Chapter 5</margin> attempted to define the tone in another way. He took the starting point of the tone from the first number that creates a cube from the first odd number—a number held in the highest honor among the Pythagoreans. Since the number three is the first odd number, if you cube it, 27 necessarily results. This number 27 is distant from the number 24 by the ratio of a tone <note>In Pythagorean music theory, the ratio of a whole tone is 9:8. 27 to 24 reduces to the same 9:8 ratio.</note>, maintaining that same difference of three. Indeed, three is exactly one-eighth of 24. When this is added to the 24, it produces 27, the first cube of three. From these, Philolaus creates two parts: one which is larger than half, which he calls the <term>apotome<note>The "major semitone."</note></term>, and the remaining part which is smaller than half, which he calls the <term>diesis<note>original: "diesim"; here referring specifically to the "minor semitone."</note></term> (which later authors called the minor semitone). The difference between these two is called a <term>comma</term>. First, he considers the diesis to consist of 13 units, because it is the difference between 216 and 243 <note>In the complex math of the time, 216 is 6 cubed and 243 is 3 to the 5th power; their ratio represents the minor semitone.</note>. This number 13 consists of a nine, a three, and a one. He explains that "one" (the unit) holds the place of a point, three represents the first line, and nine represents the first square space. For these reasons, he sets the diesis (which he wishes to call the semitone) at 13 units, while the apotome is established as 14 units. But since the difference between 13 and 14 is one unit, he judges that a single unit should be placed as the comma. He locates the whole tone at 27 units, because the difference between 216 and 243—which are a tone apart—is 27 units.

# That a Tone Consists of Two Semitones and a Comma. Chapter 6.
**I**t is easily apparent from these things that a tone consists of two minor semitones and a comma. 

<column-break/>

For if a whole tone consists of an apotome and a semitone, and the apotome differs from the semitone by a comma, the apotome is nothing other than a minor semitone plus a comma. Therefore, if one takes away the two minor semitones from the tone, the comma is what remains.

---

->Demonstration that a tone consists of two semitones and a comma.<-

The same thing will be proven in this way <gloss>it is permitted</gloss>. For if an octave <note>original: "diapason"</note> contains five tones and two minor semitones, then six tones exceed the consonance of an octave by exactly one comma. There is no doubt that if five tones are subtracted from both sides, the remainder of the octave is two minor semitones, while of the six tones, only one tone remains. Thus, this tone exceeds these two remaining semitones by a comma. If the comma is added back to those two semitones, they will equal the tone. It is therefore established that one tone is equal to two minor semitones and a comma, which is found in the first eight units. <margin>Chapter 8</margin>

# Concerning the Intervals Smaller Than Semitones
**P**hilolaus, therefore, includes these and even smaller spaces in the following definitions. A "diesis," he says, is the interval by which a perfect fourth <note>original: "sesquitertia proportio"</note> is greater than two tones. A "comma" is the interval by which a whole tone <note>original: "sesquioctaua proportio"</note> is greater than two dieses (that is, two minor semitones). A <term>schisma</term> is half of a comma. A <term>diaschisma</term> is half of a diesis (that is, half of a minor semitone). From these, it is concluded that a tone is primarily divided into a minor semitone and an apotome; it is also divided into two semitones and a comma.

<summary>This page details the mathematical division of a musical tone into smaller intervals like the apotome, diesis, and comma, using the cubing of the number three as a foundational proof. It explains the Pythagorean belief that a whole tone is composed of two minor semitones plus a tiny leftover interval called a comma.</summary>
<keywords>Philolaus, Pythagoras, musical ratios, whole tone, minor semitone, apotome, comma, diesis, schisma, diaschisma, Boethius, mathematical music theory</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical breakdown of the musical tone from the previous page, moving from the broad definitions of Philolaus to the specific micro-intervals (schismas and commas) and then demonstrating how to locate these intervals on a physical or theoretical string using the primary harmonies of the fourth and fifth.</meta>

Therefore, it follows that the tone is divided into four <term>diaschismas<note>A diaschisma is a tiny interval, defined here as half of a minor semitone.</note></term> and a <term>comma</term>. Truly, the complete half of a tone—which is the [perfect] semitone—consists of two diaschismas (which make up one minor semitone) and a <term>schisma</term>, which is half of a comma. Since a whole tone is composed of two minor semitones and a comma joined together, if one wished to divide it exactly in half, he would create one minor semitone and half of a comma. But a minor semitone is divided into two diaschismas, and half of a comma is one schisma. Therefore, it is correctly said that a complete half-tone can be divided into two diaschismas and one schisma. From this, it appears that a full [mathematical] semitone differs from a <term>minor semitone<note>Also known as a 'diesis' in the Pythagorean system.</note></term> by one schisma. The <term>apotome<note>The major semitone.</note></term>, however, differs from the minor semitone by two schismatas <note>The original Latin reads "maiore" (major), but mathematically and contextually it refers to the difference between the major and minor semitones.</note>; for it differs by a comma. And two schismas constitute one comma. But enough about these matters.

## Regarding that **COURSE**
It seems we must now discuss how we can extend or shorten musical spaces through the use of <term>consonances<note>In this context, 'consonances' refers to the perfect intervals of the fourth and fifth, which were used as stable anchors to find other notes.</note></term>. So that this may be shown graphically, let the lines we describe be taken in place of musical notes, as the matter itself will now demonstrate. Let it be proposed

<column-break/>

to find the space of a tone through consonance, both in the high [acute] and the low [grave] directions. Let there be a sound B. From this, I extend another sound which is a <term>fifth<note>original: "diapenthe"</note></term> away from B; let this be C. From C, I move back down by the interval of a <term>fourth<note>original: "diatesseron"</note></term> to the point D. And since the difference between a fifth and a fourth creates a tone, the space B-D is a tone.

<insert>has been found</insert>
<image-desc>A circular musical diagram illustrating the derivation of a tone. It shows a horizontal line with three points: B, D, and C. An arc labeled 'fifth extended' (diapenthe intensa) connects B to C. An arc labeled 'fourth released' (diatesseron remissa) connects C back to D. A smaller arc labeled 'tone' (tonus) marks the resulting distance between B and D.</image-desc>

To find a tone in the lower part, we will modulate as follows: From B, I extend a fourth up to F, and from F, I move back down a fifth to G. Therefore, G-B will be a tone. The diligent reader will thus notice that the tone D-B was produced in the higher direction, while G-B was produced in the lower direction.

<image-desc>A circular musical diagram showing a horizontal line with points G, F, and B. An arc labeled 'fourth extended' (diatesseron intensa) connects B up to F. An arc labeled 'fifth released' (diapenthe remissa) connects F down to G. The resulting interval between G and B is labeled 'tone' (tonus).</image-desc>

Let it now be proposed to find the minor part of a tone through consonance, in both the higher and lower directions. For the minor part of a tone is the space by which two tones [differ from] a fourth.

<image-desc>A large, complex circular musical diagram showing the relationship between multiple intervals. A central line contains points A, B, G, E, and C. Arcs above show two whole tones and two fourths (diatesseron) being compared. Arcs below the line are labeled with technical terms including 'minor semitone', 'fourth extended', 'fourth released', 'fifth extended', and 'fifth released', showing how these overlapping large intervals leave behind the small 'minor semitone' as a remainder.</image-desc>

<meta>This page provides a practical, step-by-step geometric and musical guide to constructing specific intervals—the tone, the minor semitone, the apotome, and the comma—by "stacking" and "subtracting" the primary consonances of the fourth and fifth. It continues the previous page's discussion of micro-intervals by showing how they can be physically located on a string.</meta>

...transcends through consonance. For instance, let there be a sound A. I ascend from A by a <term>fourth<note>Original: "diatesseron". In Pythagorean tuning, this is the interval with a ratio of 4:3.</note></term> to B. Again, I ascend from B by a fourth to C, and from C I descend by a <term>fifth<note>Original: "diapente". The interval with a ratio of 3:2.</note></term> to D. Therefore, the interval D-B is a whole tone. Again, from D I ascend by a fourth to E; I descend again from E by a fifth to F. Therefore, D-F is a tone. Since, therefore, there were the tones B-D and D-F, and B-A was a complete fourth, F-A will be the smaller part of a tone, which is called a <term>semitone<note>Original: "semitonium". Specifically, the minor semitone or limma (256:243), which remains when two whole tones are subtracted from a fourth.</note></term>.

Toward the lower part, indeed, it is done in this way: Let there be a sound A. I ascend two tones through consonance to G. From G, I descend a fourth to K. Therefore, K-A will be the minor semitone part, which is what was required to be produced.

<image-desc>A musical diagram drawn in red ink. A rectangular frame contains the letters K, A, and G. Below the frame, two concentric semi-circular arcs represent intervals. The inner arc is labeled "minor semitone" and "Two tones ascended". The outer arc is labeled "descended fifth" <note>The diagram labels this as a "diapente" (fifth), though the accompanying text describes the movement as a "diatesseron" (fourth). Such discrepancies are common in medieval technical manuscripts.</note>.</image-desc>

If we subtract a fourth from three tones, the <term>apotome<note>The major semitone (2187:2048). In the Pythagorean system, a tone is composed of one minor semitone and one apotome.</note></term> is what remains. For there are three tones: A-B, B-C, and C-D. From these, let the fourth A-E be subtracted. Therefore, E-C will be a minor semitone. Thus, E-D is the apotome.

<image-desc>A complex musical diagram in red ink. At the top, the word "TONE" is centered. Below this is a rectangular frame containing the letters A, B, C, and D. Below the frame, a series of interlocking circles and arcs illustrate the relationship between the "fourth" and multiple instances of "TONE".</image-desc>

<column-break/>

If it is convenient, let us [derive] this apotome as follows. And first, indeed, I ascend three tones from A, which are A-B and B-C; from the point that is B to C, I descend by the consonance of a fourth. And so C-A becomes the remaining apotome.

<image-desc>A red ink musical diagram. A rectangular frame contains the letters A, C, and B. Above the frame, two arcs are labeled "Apotome" and "ascended fourth". Below the frame, a single large arc is labeled "Three tones ascended".</image-desc>

But if we wish to produce the same space toward a lower sound, it is done in this way: Let there be a sound A. I ascend a minor semitone, which is A-D. I descend from D a whole tone, which is D-E. Therefore, A-E will be the apotome we require.

<image-desc>A red ink musical diagram. A rectangular frame contains the letters E, A, and D. Arcs above are labeled "Apotome" and "minor semitone ascended". An arc below is labeled "Tone descended".</image-desc>

Let the goal be to find a <term>comma<note>The Pythagorean comma is the tiny mathematical difference between the apotome (major semitone) and the limma (minor semitone).</note></term> in the higher register. Let there be a sound A. I ascend an apotome A-B. I descend a minor semitone B-C. And since the minor semitone is smaller than the apotome by a comma, C-A will be the comma.

Again, toward the lower part in this manner: Between point A and the sound of a <term>minor semitone<note>Original: "semitoniū minus". In the Pythagorean system used here, this is the 'limma' (256:243), the smaller of the two semitones that make up a whole tone.</note></term>, I descend an <term>apotome<note>Original: "apotomen". This is the 'major semitone' (2187:2048). When you add a minor semitone and an apotome together, you get a perfect whole tone.</note></term> from B, which is B–E. I then ascend a <term>comma<note>Original: "comma". The tiny mathematical gap (roughly 1/9th of a tone) that results from the difference between six whole tones and one octave.</note></term> from E to A.

## Rule for Obtaining the Semitone. Chapter 10.

It is necessary that all these sounding intervals be properly known both to the mind and to the ears. For these reasons and sciences are gathered in vain unless they become very well known through use and practice. We should not only be taught what is in the "Instruction of Music" through words, but we should soon perceive with our ears what we already see through reason. Now we will provide the method for finding the space that seems a bit more difficult—namely, the minor semitone—so that it can be found in order in both the high and low parts.

In the <term>fourth<note>Original: "diatesseron".</note></term> A–B, one must draw out the minor semitone toward the

<column-break/>

lower and higher parts. I ascend a fourth from B to C. I descend again a <term>fifth<note>Original: "diapenthe".</note></term> from C to D. The result will be the <term>tone<note>Original: "tonus".</note></term> B–D. For the fourth is exceeded by the fifth by one tone. And the space C–B is crossed by the space D–C and the space B–D. Again, I ascend a fourth D–E, then I descend a fifth E–F. The interval D–F is a tone. But since B–D was also a tone, A–F is therefore a minor semitone, which is what remains of the fourth A–B after the two tones F–B and B–D are subtracted. Again, I descend a fourth A–G and ascend a fifth G–H. Therefore, A–H will be a tone. But since A–F was a semitone, F–H will be an apotome. Again, I descend a fourth H–K and ascend a fifth K–L. Therefore, H–L is a tone. But since H–A was a tone, L–B is a minor semitone. 

But since B–D was a tone, L–D will be an apotome. Again, I ascend a fourth F–M. Therefore, B–M is a semitone. I descend a fourth L–N. Therefore, N–A is a semitone. By this sounding method, two semitones are established above the fourth A–B: B–M toward the high side and N–A toward the low side. The entire distance O–N is less than a fifth. It consists of five semitones and a doubled apotome; that is, from two tones and three minor semitones. And because two semitones cannot fill a whole tone, a comma remains. The entire space M–N differs from the interval of a fifth by one comma. This the diligent reader will find most easily.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical intervals from the previous page, moving from the practical method of finding the semitone to the mathematical proof regarding the "comma" and the nature of musical ratios.</meta>

But since we have briefly touched upon the theory of the <term>comma<note>The smallest interval in the Pythagorean tuning system, approximately 1/9th of a whole tone. It is the mathematical "leftover" when comparing different ways of stacking intervals.</note></term>, we must not avoid showing by what proportion the comma itself is contained. For the comma is the smallest interval that the sense of hearing can perceive. It must be observed how many individual commas the <term>minor semitone<note>Original: "semitoniū minus." The smaller of two semitones in the Pythagorean system.</note></term> and the <term>major semitone<note>Original: "semitoniū maius." Also known as the apotome.</note></term> are seen to consist of, and likewise, how many commas are joined together to form the whole tone itself. And first, let the beginning be taken here from [unclear: the following demonstration].

### Archytas’s Demonstration that a Superparticular Proportion Cannot Be Divided into Equal Parts; and its Refutation. Chapter 11.

A <term>superparticular proportion<note>Original: "superparticularis pportio." A ratio where the larger number contains the smaller number once, plus one aliquot part of it—for example, 3:2 (sesquialtera) or 4:3 (sesquitertia).</note></term> cannot be divided into two equal parts by placing a middle number proportionally between them. This will indeed be demonstrated firmly once it is established. For the demonstration that <term>Archytas<note>A Greek mathematician and Pythagorean philosopher (c. 428–347 BC) who was the first to provide this mathematical proof.</note></term> provides is of this manner. Let there be, he says, a superparticular proportion represented by the terms A and B. I take the smallest numbers in that same proportion, C and E. Since, therefore, C and E are the smallest in that same proportion and are superparticular, the larger term E exceeds the smaller term C by one part of itself. Now, let this difference be

<column-break/>

D; I say that D will not be a [multi-unit] number, but <term>unity<note>Original: "unitas." The number one, considered by Greeks as the indivisible source of all numbers rather than a number itself.</note></term>. For if D is a number, and it is a part of E, then D measures [is a factor of] the larger term E. Wherefore it would also measure the smaller term C. The result would be that the number D measures both C and E. But this is impossible. For those numbers which are the smallest in the same proportion among any others are <term>prime to each other<note>Original: "primi ad se invicem." Numbers that have no common divisor other than 1.</note></term>, and they retain unity as their only difference. Therefore, D is unity. Thus, the larger E exceeds the smaller C by unity. Consequently, no middle number falls between them which can divide that proportion into equal parts. Because of this, it follows that no middle number can be placed between any other terms that hold the same proportion as these, which could divide that proportion equally.

---

<image-desc>A complex red-ink diagram illustrating mathematical proportions. A large outer semicircular arc is labeled "superparticular proportion: six to four." A middle concentric arc is labeled "Smallest terms of the same proportion: three to two." At the base of the diagram is a horizontal structure resembling a lintel resting on five pillars/boxes. These boxes contain Roman numerals: from left to right, IV (4), II (2), I (1), III (3), and VI (6). In the center of the diagram, within the middle arch, is a small circle labeled "Difference of the numbers" containing the Roman numeral I (1). Smaller connecting arches link the boxes IV to II and III to VI, showing how 6:4 is composed of these parts.</image-desc>

---

And indeed, according to Archytas's reasoning, no middle term falls into a superparticular proportion so as to divide the proportion equally, because the smallest numbers in that same proportion differ only by unity. As if indeed the smallest numbers in a <term>multiple proportion<note>Original: "multiplici pportione." A ratio where the larger number is a whole-number multiple of the smaller, such as 2:1 (double) or 3:1 (triple).</note></term> do not also obtain the same difference of unity? For we see many

<column-break/>

multiples among those which are placed in ratios where a middle term, splitting the proportion equally, could be applied. But those who have diligently inspected our arithmetic numbers will understand this more easily. It must be added, however, that this is just as Archytas thinks—that only the superparticular proportion [is indivisible in this way].

<summary>This page presents a famous mathematical proof by the Pythagorean Archytas, demonstrating that musical ratios like the fifth (3:2) or fourth (4:3) cannot be split into two equal whole-number ratios. It establishes the "comma" as the smallest unit of hearing and the foundational limit of musical measurement.</summary>

<keywords>Archytas, superparticular proportion, comma, musical ratios, unity, Boethius, Pythagorean tuning, mathematical proof, interval division</keywords>

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration regarding musical intervals, specifically moving from the general division of ratios to the specific numerical proof of the size of the "comma." It builds on the previous discussion of Archytas’s theories by applying them to the Pythagorean comma.</meta>

...the same proportion is not, however, to be spoken of as universal. Now, let us proceed to the matters that follow: in what proportion of numbers the <term>comma<note>The Pythagorean comma: the tiny mathematical difference between seven octaves and twelve perfect fifths.</note></term> consists. And that in this proportion, it is larger than 75 to 74, but smaller than 78 to 77.

First, therefore, I say that these numbers 112 <note>Original: "hi nīs CXII." This likely refers to a specific set of units or a calculation step in the proof.</note> which contain the comma hold between themselves a greater proportion than 75 to 74, and a smaller one than 78 to 77. Therefore <gloss>also</gloss> it will be demonstrated in this way. And first, it must be remembered that six <term>whole tones<note>A "tonus" or whole tone in Pythagorean tuning is the ratio 9:8.</note></term> exceed the <term>diapason<note>The interval of an octave, defined by the ratio 2:1.</note></term> by a comma. Let **A**, therefore, be 275,148. Let **B**, however, be the octave related to it, containing the <term>consonance<note>Original: "consonantiam." A harmonious sounding-together of two notes.</note></term> in the double ratio, namely 532,288. Let **C**, truly, be distant from number **A** by a sixth tone, and let it be 531,441. All of these values are to be gathered from the arrangement of tones in the second volume. Therefore, the proportion of the comma is contained between this [B] and **C**. I subtract the smaller **B** from the smaller **C**, and what remains is **D**, placed at 7,153 units. Which number **D** is indeed smaller than a seventy-third part [of B], but larger than a seventy-fifth part of the same. For if the same **D**, the smallest,

<column-break/>

which is 7,153, is multiplied seventy-three times, it becomes the number **E**, constituted of 522,168 units. If indeed it is multiplied seventy-four times, it becomes the number **F**, 538,322. Since indeed **E** (which was increased by 73) is smaller than the smallest **B**, and **F** (which was increased by 74) is larger than the smallest **B**, it is therefore correctly said that **D** is less than a seventy-third part, but larger than a seventy-fourth <gloss>part</gloss>. Wherefore, the number **C** also exceeds the smallest **B** by a part of itself which is smaller than a seventy-third, but larger than a seventy-fourth. Therefore, the proportion of that which is **C** to that which is **B** is indeed larger than 75 to 74, and smaller than 78 to 77. For in the former, the unit is the seventy-fifth part of the smaller; in the latter, however, the same unit is the seventy-third part.

<meta>This page continues the mathematical proof regarding the size of the Pythagorean comma. The previous page established the primary numbers involved (A and B), and this page provides the specific arithmetic comparison to the ratio of 75 to 74.</meta>

<margin>
In the second volume of this work, in the ninth chapter, where he treats this reasoning and thus, having sought it, he finds it.
</margin>

The same is to be explained in another way, by first assuming this: that if the difference of the smaller terms is added equally to every proportion, a smaller proportion will be held among those terms produced after the addition than among the previous terms, which were distant from one another by a certain proportion before that addition. For example, consider **6** and **4**: if to both their difference—that is, two—is added, they become **8** and **6**. But between **6** and **4**, a <term>sesquialtera<note>Original: "sesquialtera." A ratio of 3:2, where the larger number contains the smaller plus its half.</note></term> proportion is contained; between **8** and **6**, a <term>sesquitertia<note>Original: "sesquitertia." A ratio of 4:3, where the larger number contains the smaller plus its third.</note></term> proportion is held. Now, the sesquitertia proportion is smaller than the sesquialtera proportion. Therefore, let the higher numbers which contain the proportion of the <term>comma<note>The Pythagorean comma is the minute discrepancy between six whole tones and one octave.</note></term> be arranged according to what was said before: that is, **526,441** and thus let **A** be beyond **B** at **526,478**. <note>These numbers are likely transcriptions of 531,441 (six tones) and 524,288 (the octave), though the OCR reflects specific manuscript variants.</note> Let their difference be **C**, which is **7,153**. Therefore, let **C** measure the larger number, which is **A**, seventy-five times. If, therefore, **C** is multiplied seventy-five times, there will be a number **D** which is **526,475**. Therefore, the number **D** precedes that which is **A** by the measure of **E**, which is **5,034**. Again, let the number **C** measure that which is **B** seventy-four times, and let it be multiplied.

<column-break/>

Thus, the number **F** will be produced, such as **529,472**. Since **F** is larger than that which is **B** by that same number **E**, which is **5,034**. Therefore, the number **D** precedes that which is **A** by the measure of **E**, while the number **B** is exceeded by that which is **F** by the same measure of **E**. If, therefore, we add the same **E** to **A**, it becomes **D**; <note>The text describes a logical balancing: by adding or subtracting the remainder E, the author aligns the comma with a clean multiple of the difference C.</note> if indeed we add the same **E** to the number **B**, it becomes **F**. But the number **D** has been increased seventy-five times by **C**—that is, by multiplying it—while **F** results from **C** multiplied seventy-four times. Therefore, **D** and **F** maintain a proportion between themselves such as **75** has to **74**. But **D** and **F** are **A** and **B** with the addition of one **E** to them. Therefore, a greater proportion is naturally contained between **A** and **B** than between **D** and **F**. For because **A** and **B** were made into **D** and **F** by the addition of one **E**, the proportion between **D** and **F** is smaller; but between **D** and **F** the same proportion exists as between **75** and **74**. Therefore, between **A** and **B** there is a greater proportion than between **75** and **74**; and **A** and **B** contain the comma. Therefore, the proportion of the comma is greater than seventy-five to **74**.

<meta>This page concludes the mathematical proof from the previous page, which sought to define the upper and lower limits of the Pythagorean comma. The text relies on the Euclidean principle that subtracting an equal amount from two terms in a ratio increases the magnitude of the proportion.</meta>

Since we have shown that the proportion of the <gloss>comma<note>Original: "comatis." The Pythagorean comma is the tiny mathematical gap between six whole tones and a perfect octave, discovered through the calculation of intervals.</note></gloss> is greater than the ratio that 75 holds compared to 74, it must now be shown how the numbers containing the interval of the comma contain a smaller proportion between them than 74 compared to 73. 

This will indeed be demonstrated in the following way. First, we must recall what we said in the single volume <note>referring to an earlier section of Boethius’s work, likely Book 2, Chapter 9</note> when we were discussing the measurement of differences. For if from any proportion we subtract the difference of the smaller terms that contain it, the numbers that remain will hold a greater proportion than those numbers which existed before the subtraction. 

For example, let there be **8** and **6**. From these, I subtract their own difference, which is **2**; they become **6** and **4**. But in the former case [8 and 6], a <term>sesquitertia<note>Original: "sesquitertia." A 4:3 ratio, where the larger number contains the smaller plus one-third of it (e.g., 8 is 6 + 1/3 of 6).</note></term> is contained; in the latter [6 and 4], a <term>sesquialtera<note>Original: "sesquialtera." A 3:2 ratio, where the larger number contains the smaller plus one-half of it (e.g., 6 is 4 + 1/2 of 4).</note></term> proportion is held. Now, a sesquialtera proportion is greater than a sesquitertia proportion. 

Therefore, let **A** and **B** be those values described above, whose difference is **C**. I multiply the difference **C** seventy-four times.

<column-break/>

The result is the number **F**, namely **529,322**. When this is compared to number **A**, it is joined by number **G**, which is **2,128**. Again, the same **C** multiplied seventy-three times will produce the number **K**, that is **522,168**. When this is compared to the smaller number **B**, it is exceeded by that same **G**, the value **2,128**. 

Therefore, having subtracted **G** from numbers **A** and **B**, **F** and **K** are produced. Consequently, **A** and **B** will maintain a smaller proportion than **F** and **K**. But **F** and **K** maintain the same proportion that **74** holds to **73**, for these were created by multiplying **C**. 

Therefore, the proportion of **A** and **B**, which contains the comma, is smaller than the ratio of **74** to **73**. But it was shown a little earlier that this same proportion of the comma is greater than **75** to **74**. Thus, it has been demonstrated that the numbers containing the comma have a proportion between them that is greater than **75** to **74**, but smaller than **74** to **73**. This is what was to be shown.

<meta>This page continues the mathematical proofs of Boethius’s music theory. The previous page established that a minor semitone (the limma) is larger than three "commas" but smaller than four. ### That the apotome is larger than four commas but smaller than five; and that the whole tone is larger than eight commas but smaller than nine. This same reasoning also applies to Chapter 15.

That the major semitone—which we previously called the **apotome** <note>original: "apotomen"; in Pythagorean tuning, the major semitone is slightly larger than the minor semitone. The two together make a full whole tone.</note>—contains a certain number of commas can be discovered in this manner. Let **A** be 272,144. Let the point distanced from it by five tones be **B**, which is 472,396. Let the point distanced from **A** by six tones be **D**, which is 531,441. <note>These large numbers are used to express precise musical ratios as whole numbers, avoiding the need for complex fractions.</note> Therefore, between **B** and **D** there is a whole tone. Now, let **B** be distanced from **C** by a minor semitone, and let **C** be 497,674. There remains, therefore, the proportion of an **apotome** between **C** and **D**. For since **B-D** is a whole tone, if you subtract the minor semitone **B-C** from it, the major semitone **C-D** remains, which we previously noted is called the apotome. The difference <term>differentia|The mathematical remainder when subtracting the smaller string length from the larger.</term> between **D** and **C** is 33,767; let this be **E**. But the difference of a comma was 7,152; let this be **F**. If, therefore, you take **F** (which is the comma) five times 

<column-break/>

multiplied, it becomes 35,760; and let this be **G**. If, however, the same **F** is multiplied four times, it becomes 28,608; let this be **K**. Therefore, **G** is larger than the difference **E**, and **K** is smaller. But **G** is the comma increased five times, while **K** is the comma increased four times. And **E** is the difference of the apotome. Therefore, it is proven that the apotome is indeed smaller than five commas, but larger than four. From these results, it is proven that a whole tone is larger than **8** commas, but smaller than **9**. For if a minor semitone is larger than three commas but smaller than four, and the major semitone (the apotome) is smaller than five commas but larger than four, then the whole tone will be larger than **8** commas but smaller than **9**. For the apotome and the minor semitone together create one whole tone. Therefore, a whole tone is larger than **8** commas, but smaller than **9**.

<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration from the previous page, which sought to define the exact size of musical intervals using large integers. It concludes the proof regarding the whole tone and then demonstrates the relationship between the major and minor semitone.</meta>

What was said above has been demonstrated through numbers.
But although it has been demonstrated through this reasoning of Chapter 16 how the tone is composed of commas <note>original: "commatibus"; in ancient music theory, the comma is the smallest measurable interval, representing the minute discrepancy between different mathematical ways of calculating the same note.</note>, we must not rest as if we were weary or lazy; instead, the tone itself should be shown to contain these commas through its own numerical operation. Let **A** be 262,144. Let **B**, which is five tones distant from it, be 472,396. Let **C**—which does not form an octave <note>original: "diapason"</note> with **A**, but nevertheless contains a concord <note>original: "symphoniam"</note>—be 534,288. Let **D**, differing from **A** by six tones, be 532,441. Therefore, **D** is distant from **C** by...

With these things established: although it has been shown that the major semitone differs from the minor semitone by a comma, the same will also be proven by itself and through the following numbers. Let **A** be 497,673. Let the minor semitone <note>often called the "limma" in Greek theory</note> distant from it be **B**, which was already described above as 524,288. Let an **apotome** <note>the major semitone</note> be distant from **A** by the number 536,452, and let this be **C**. Since

<column-break/>

...namely six commas of the tones from the octave <note>original: "diapason"</note> consonance. Let this value be **E**: 7,153. **D**, however, is distant from **B** by a complete whole tone—that is, the difference between six tones and five tones. Let this be **F**: 59,049. If I increase **E** nine times, I get **H**: 64,388. But if I increase it eight times, I get **G**: 57,224. Now, **H** exceeds **F**, but **G** is exceeded by it. Since **F** is the numerical difference of a whole tone, while **H** is a comma multiplied nine times and **G** is eight times, it is demonstrated that a whole tone is indeed smaller than nine commas, but larger than eight of those same commas.

Therefore, if **A-B** is a minor semitone and **A-C** is a major semitone, the difference between **B** and **C** must be sought. That difference is 7,153, which is **D**. But we showed a little while ago that this specific value represents a comma. Therefore, a comma creates the difference between the major semitone and the minor semitone.

<meta>This page concludes Book III of Boethius’s "Fundamentals of Music" and begins the table of contents for Book IV. ### I propose to demonstrate once again: that the whole tone is greater than two minor semitones by exactly one comma.

Let **A** be the number 476,672. From this, let a whole tone <note>original: "tonus"</note> be stretched to 531,441, and let this be **D**. However, from that same **A**, let a minor semitone <note>original: "semitonium minus"; the smaller of the two intervals that make up a whole tone in Pythagorean tuning</note> be stretched, which is 498,672, and let this be **B**. Likewise, from that **B**, let another minor semitone be stretched, which is **C**, being 522,476.

Therefore, while **AD** is a whole tone, **AC** contains two minor semitones. We see that the comma <note>the smallest mathematical interval in this system</note> is established as the difference between numbers **C** and **D**. This difference **E** is 7,153 according to the units. It is therefore demonstrated that a whole tone is greater than two minor semitones by a comma.

**But** since all those things which we previously promised have now been demonstrated by their own proper reasoning, what remains for this musical instruction is the division of the **monochord** <note>a one-stringed instrument used to demonstrate the mathematical ratios of musical intervals</note> by the rule. Because a longer treatise extends this matter, we have decided to transfer it to the discussion of the following book.

**Here ends the Third Book on Music**
