--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the specific calculations of the previous book into a broader theoretical summary and the beginning of Book IV. It connects the physical motion of objects to the mathematical nature of musical intervals.</meta>

Although we have arranged all those things which were to be demonstrated in the treatment of the previous book: it is nevertheless worthwhile to briefly and mindfully collect them again with a certain variety of treatment. This is so that as these concepts return once again to the memory, we may arrive at the correct division toward which our entire intention aims.

If by chance there were a total stillness of all things, no sound would strike the hearing. This would happen because, with everything at rest, things would stir no striking impulse <note>original: "pulsum"</note> among themselves. Therefore, for there to be a voice <note>original: "vox"; in this context, it refers to any musical sound or pitch</note>, there is a need for a strike. But for there to be a strike, movement must precede it. So, for there to be a voice, it is necessary for there to be movement.

Every movement has within it its own speed, and also its own slowness. Therefore, if the movement is slow in its striking, a low <note>original: "gravis"; literally "heavy," used by ancient theorists to mean low-pitched</note> sound is produced. For just as slowness is closest to a standstill, so is low pitch contiguous with silence. Truly, a fast movement provides a high <note>original: "acutam"; literally "sharp," meaning high-pitched</note> voice. Furthermore, that which is low increases toward the middle through <term>intension: the tightening of a string or increasing of effort to raise pitch</term>, while that which is high decreases toward the middle through <term>remission: the slackening of a string or effort to lower pitch</term>.

From this, it happens that every sound seems to be, as it were, composed of certain opposite parts. However, every mixing of parts is joined by a certain proportion. Therefore, the mixing of sounds is joined by proportions. Proportioins are considered principally in numbers. Simple proportions are found either in multiples, in <term>superparticulars: ratios where the larger number contains the smaller number plus one part of it, like 3:2</term>, or in <term>superpartients: ratios where the larger number contains the smaller plus several parts of it, like 5:3</term>.

It is known that through multiple or sub-particular proportions, voices are heard as consonant or dissonant. What is a consonance? It is when sounds, struck at the same time, join together into a sweet and blended sound. A dissonance, however,

<column-break/>

is that which, when struck together, does not return a sweet or blended sound. Therefore, having said these things, let us say a few things about proportions.

### II. Various speculations on intervals.

If the number two <note>original: "binarius"</note> is multiplied within a triple multiple, that which is produced from this multiplication of intervals is also a multiple. Let there be a multiple interval **BC**, and let **B** be a multiple of that which is **C**. Let it be arranged so that as **C** is to **B**, so **B** is to **D**. Since **B** is a multiple of **C**, the term **C** measures the term **B**—either twice, or three times, and so on. And since the ratio of **C** to **B** is the same as **B** to **D**, the term **B** also measures the term **D**. By this logic, the term **C** also measures the term **D**.

Therefore, **D** is a multiple of **C**, and the interval **DC** is the interval resulting from the compound of the interval **BC** being coupled to itself. This is also proven using actual numbers. For let the ratio of **B** to **C** be double <note>original: "duplum"; a 2:1 ratio</note>, like two compared to one. And let it be that as **C** is to **B**, so **B** is to **D**. Therefore, **D** will be the multiple four <note>original: "quaternarius"</note>. And as **B** is to **C** (two to one), so **D** is to **B** (four to two). Thus, **D** (four) is a multiple of **C** (one). For four is the quadruple of one, and it is the result of the interval **BC** being multiplied by two.

->**Multiple Interval**<-

| I | II | III |
| :---: | :---: | :---: |
| **C** | **B** | **D** |

->**Multiple Interval**<-
<note>This diagram represents a geometric progression where the ratio is doubled. If the first interval is 1 to 2, and the second is 2 to 4, the total span from C to D is 1 to 4.</note>

<summary>This page explains the physical origins of sound as a product of movement and speed, defining high and low pitches through mathematical proportions. It introduces the proof that compounding multiple ratios (like doubling a double) results in a further multiple ratio.</summary>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical proofs concerning musical intervals begun in the previous section. It focuses on the properties of "multiple" and "superparticular" ratios, which are fundamental to understanding how pitches relate to one another mathematically.</meta>

<column-break/>

<image-desc>A circular diagram with the heading "Multiple interval" <note>original: "Interuallum multiplex"</note> written along the upper inner curve. Inside, three boxes labeled I, II, and III contain the letters C, B, and D respectively. Below these boxes, the word "multiple" is written twice in arched script following the bottom curve of the circle.</image-desc>

**I**f an interval, when multiplied by two <note>original: "binario multiplicatu[m]"; in the context of Boethius’s music theory, "multiplying by two" refers to doubling the interval itself, which is achieved by squaring the numerical ratio</note>, results in a **multiple** <term>multiple: a ratio where the larger number is a perfect multiple of the smaller one, such as 2:1 (double) or 3:1 (triple)</term> interval, then the interval itself must also be a multiple. Let the interval be **B to C**, and let it be arranged so that **C** is to **B** as **B** is to **D**. And let **D** be a multiple of **C**. I say that **B** is also a multiple of **C**. For since **D** is a multiple of **C**, **C** "measures" **D** <note>In ancient geometry, one number "measures" another if it divides into it exactly without a remainder</note>. It has been shown that if a proportion is in its smallest terms, and the first term measures the last, it will also measure the middle term. Therefore, **C** will measure **B**. Thus, **B** is a multiple of **C**. Let us look at this again using the smallest units: Let **C** be unity [1], and let **D**, which is the doubled proportion of **B** to **C**, be four [4]. This is a multiple of **C**, for it is quadruple [4:1]. Since this quadruple is generated from doubling the proportion **B** to **C**, the proportion **B** to **C** will be the half-ratio of it. Therefore, the proportion **B** to **C** is double [2:1]. Since the double is a multiple, the proportion **B** is also a multiple.

**B**etween the middle terms of a **superparticular** <term>superparticular: a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller number once plus one extra part of it, such as 3:2 (the musical fifth) or 4:3 (the musical fourth)</term> interval, neither one nor many terms will fall proportionally <note>This is a fundamental proof in Pythagorean music theory: you cannot find a "mean" or middle number to split a superparticular ratio into two equal whole-number ratios</note>. For let **B** to **C** be a superparticular proportion, and in that same proportion let the smallest terms be **D**, **F**, and **G**. Since **D**, **F**, and **G** are the smallest, they are the primary terms of that proportion. Because of this, only unity [1] will measure them. Let **G** be subtracted from **D-F**, and let the remainder be **D**. This is the common measure of both. Therefore, this will be unity. Consequently, no number will fall between **D-F**...

<column-break/>

...and **G** that is smaller than **F-D** but larger than **G**, for only unity [1] exists between them. However, as many proportional terms as can be found between the smallest terms of a superparticular proportion, just as many can be found between any other terms of that same proportion. But since no number can fall between the smallest terms **F-D** and **G**, then no number can fall proportionally between **B** and **C**. Let us look at this in its smallest terms: Let there be any superparticular proportion, such as the **sesquitertia** <term>sesquitertia: the 4:3 ratio, which corresponds to the musical interval of a perfect fourth</term>. Let these terms be 10 and 15 <note>The author uses 10 and 15 as an example, though these actually represent a 3:2 ratio (sesquialtera); 12 and 16 would be a 4:3 ratio</note>. In that same proportion, the smallest terms are 2 and 3 <note>original: "II et III"</note>. I subtract two from three; the remainder is unity. This unity measures them both. Therefore, there will be no number between two and three in their smallest form that is larger than two but smaller than three. Otherwise, unity would have to be divided, which is impossible <note>original: "inconueniens"; in mathematical logic, this refers to a result that contradicts the defined rules of the system, such as dividing the indivisible number 1</note>. Therefore, no such term can be found between 10 and 15 that maintains that same proportion.

<image-desc>A complex circular diagram illustrating musical/mathematical ratios. At the top, curved text reads "Sub-superparticular" <note>original: "Sub Sup' particularis"</note>. A central horizontal divider has an arc above it containing the text "Common to both when subtracted: Number G, the measure of unity." Inside are several rectangular boxes; the labels I, II, and IIII appear on a middle row, and III is centered below them. To the left are boxes labeled X and S. Letters F, G, and C are placed near the rightmost boxes. A lower arc contains the text "Number D.F. the smallest superparticular proportion." On the far left margin of the circle, vertical curved text segments read "Super-par-par-tic-u-lar."</image-desc>

**I**f an interval that is not a multiple is multiplied by two [doubled], it is neither a multiple nor a superparticular. For let the interval **B-C** be non-multiple, and let **C** be to **B** as **B** is to **D**. I say that **D** is neither a multiple nor a superparticular of **C**.

<summary>This page contains mathematical proofs by Boethius demonstrating that doubling a multiple ratio results in another multiple, and proving the impossibility of finding a whole-number geometric mean within superparticular ratios like the perfect fourth or fifth.</summary>
<keywords>Boethius, music theory, mathematical ratios, superparticular, multiple intervals, sesquitertia, unity, musical geometry, proportional terms</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical proofs regarding musical ratios, specifically demonstrating that doubling certain ratios does not always result in a simple category (like multiple or superparticular), and showing how the octave (double) is composed of a fifth (sesquialter) and a fourth (sesquitertian).</meta>

For let it be, if possible, that **D** is first a **multiple** <note>original: "multiplex"</note> of **C**. And since it is known that if an interval is doubled <note>original: "binario multiplicatum"; in Pythagorean music theory, "doubling" an interval means compounding the ratio with itself, or squaring the numbers</note> and a **multiple** is created from that doubling, then that which was doubled must also be a **multiple** interval. Therefore, the interval **B to C** would be a **multiple**. But this was not our premise. Therefore, **D** will not be a **multiple** of **C**, nor indeed will it be a **superparticular**. <term>superparticular: a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller number plus one equal part of it, like 3:2 or 4:3</term> For no middle proportional term falls within a **superparticular** proportion. Between the two [points] and **C**, a proportional term has been established, which is **B**. For as **C** is to **B**, so **B** is to **D**. It is therefore impossible for **D** to be either a **multiple** or a **superparticular** of **C**. This is what needed to be shown.

And in numbers, let there be an interval that is not a **multiple**. If we take the number **4** and make it so that as **4** is to **6**, so **6** is to any other number. This [third] number will be **9**, which is neither a **multiple** of **4** nor a **superparticular** to it. <note>9:4 is a "superpartient" ratio (2 and 1/4), falling outside the two "purest" categories used in ancient music</note>

<image-desc>A red and black ink diagram consisting of an outer arch labeled "Neither multiple nor superparticular" containing two smaller inner arches, both labeled "Not multiple". Below the arches are three rectangular compartments: the left containing "9" over "C", the center "6" over "B", and the right "4" over "A".</image-desc>

If an interval is doubled and that which is created from that multiplication is not a **multiple**, then the interval itself will not be a **multiple**. Let the interval be **B to C**, and let it be arranged so that **C** is to **B** as **B** is to **D**, and let **D** not be a **multiple** of **C**. I say that **B** will also not be a **multiple** of **C**. For if **B** were a **multiple**, then **D** would also be a **multiple** of **C**. But it is not. Therefore, **B** is not a **multiple** of **C**.

<column-break/>

<image-desc>A red and black ink diagram similar to the one in the first column. The outer arch is labeled "Non-multiple interval" and the two inner arches are both labeled "Not multiple". The three compartments below contain: "9" over "A" (left), "6" over "B" (center), and "4" over "C" (right).</image-desc>

A **double** <term>double: the 2:1 ratio, which creates the musical interval of an octave</term> interval is joined together from the two greatest **superparticulars**: the **sesquialter** and the **sesquitertian**. <note>The sesquialter (3:2) is the perfect fifth, and the sesquitertian (4:3) is the perfect fourth. This proof shows that Fifth + Fourth = Octave</note> For let **A** be a **sesquialter** of **B**, and let **B** be a **sesquitertian** of **C**. I say that **A** is a **double** of **C**. Since **A** is the **sesquialter** of **B**, then **A** contains within itself the whole of **B** plus its half. Therefore, two of **A** are equal to three of **B**. Again, since **B** is the **sesquitertian** of **C**, **B** contains **C** and its third part. Therefore, three of **B** are equal to four of **C**. Consequently, one **A** is equal to two of **C**. Therefore, **A** will be the **double** of **C**. And in numbers: here **12** is the **sesquialter** to **8**, and **8** is the **sesquitertian** to **6**. Therefore, **12** to **6** is a **double** proportion.

<image-desc>A red and black ink diagram featuring an outer arch with lowercase letters dispersed above it. Two inner arches are labeled "Sesquialter" and "Sesquitertian". Below are three rectangular compartments: "12" over "A", "8" over "B", and "6" over "C".</image-desc>

From a **double** interval and a **sesquialter**, a **triple** <note>original: "triplex"; a 3:1 ratio, which musically is an octave plus a fifth</note> interval is born. For let **A** be the **double** of **B**, and **B** the **sesquialter** of **C**. I say that **A** is the **triple** of **C**. For since **A** is the **double** of **B**, **A** is equal [to two of B...]

<summary>This page provides mathematical proofs showing that doubling a non-multiple ratio results in a complex ratio, and demonstrates that a perfect octave (2:1) is composed of a perfect fifth (3:2) and a perfect fourth (4:3).</summary>
<keywords>double interval, octave, sesquialter, fifth, sesquitertian, fourth, multiple ratio, superparticular ratio, Boethius, mathematical music theory</keywords>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical demonstrations of musical ratios. Having previously discussed that doubling a non-multiple ratio does not necessarily result in a simple ratio, the text now moves to specific combinations of ratios—showing how the "Fifth" (sesquialter) and "Fourth" (sesquitertian) relate to the "Octave" (duplex) and the "Whole Tone" (sesquioctave).</meta>

...to two **B**s. Again, because **B** is **sesquialter** <term>sesquialter: a ratio of 3:2, where the larger number contains the whole of the smaller plus its half part; in music, this is the interval of a Perfect Fifth</term> to **C**, it follows that **B** contains the whole of **C** and its half part. Therefore, two **B**s are equal to three **C**s. But since two **B**s were equal to one **A**, it follows that **A** is also equal to three **C**s. Therefore, **A** is a **triple** <note>original: "triplex"</note> of one **C**. And in numbers: let there be a **double** ratio, which is six to three, and a **sesquialter** ratio, which is three to two. Therefore, six is the **triple** of two.

->**A (VI) — B (III) — C (II)**<-
<image-desc>A mathematical diagram with three boxes. Box A contains 6, Box B contains 3, and Box C contains 2. An arc labeled "Triple" connects 6 to 2. An arc labeled "Double" connects 6 to 3. An arc labeled "Sesquialter" connects 3 to 2.</image-desc>

If a **sesquitertian** <term>sesquitertian: a ratio of 4:3, where the larger number contains the smaller plus one-third; in music, this is the Perfect Fourth</term> interval is removed from a **sesquialter** interval, the remaining interval will be a **sesquioctave** <term>sesquioctave: a ratio of 9:8, where the larger number contains the smaller plus one-eighth; in music, this is a Whole Tone</term>. For let **A** be the **sesquialter** of that which is **B**, and let **C** be the **sesquitertian** of that same **B**. I say that **A** is the **sesquioctave** of **C**. For since **A** is the **sesquialter** of **B**, **A** contains within itself **B** and its half part. Therefore, eight **A**s are equal to twelve **B**s. Again, because **C** is the **sesquitertian** of **B**, **C** contains within itself **B** and its third part. Therefore, nine **C**s are equal to twelve **B**s. However, twelve **B**s were equal to eight **A**s. Therefore, eight **A**s are equal to nine **C**s. Thus **A** is equal to **C** plus its eighth part <note>9 is 8 plus 1/8th of 8</note>. Therefore, **A** is the **sesquioctave** of **C**. And in numbers: let the **sesquialter** interval be nine to six; and the **sesquitertian** interval be eight to six. Therefore, the proportion of eight to nine is a **sesquioctave** proportion.

<column-break/>

<image-desc>A diagram showing the relationship between the numbers 9, 8, and 6. An arc labeled "Sesquioctave" connects 9 (A) and 8 (C). An arc labeled "Sesquialter" connects 9 (A) and 6 (B). The number 8 (C) is the sesquitertian of 6 (B), though not explicitly labeled with an arc here.</image-desc>

Six **sesquioctave** proportions are greater than one **double** <note>original: "duplici"</note> interval. <note>This is a famous proof in Pythagorean tuning: six whole tones (9:8) are slightly larger than one octave (2:1). The difference is known as the Pythagorean Comma.</note> For let there be a certain number **A**, and let **B** be its **sesquioctave**, and **C** be the **sesquioctave** of that, and **D** the **sesquioctave** of that, and **F** the **sesquioctave** of that, and **G** the **sesquioctave** of that, and **K** the **sesquioctave** of that. This is done according to the method described in **Arithmetic** <gloss>the science of numbers</gloss> for **A. B. C. D. F. G. K.**

And let **A** be 262,144. <note>original: "cclxii. cxlviii"; the OCR likely misread 144 as 148, as 262,144 is the standard starting number for this proof</note> The **sesquioctave** of this, which is **B**, is 294,912. <margin>from this</margin> The **sesquioctave** of this, which is **C**, is 331,776. The **sesquioctave** of this, which is **D**, is 373,248. The **sesquioctave** of this, which is **F**, is 419,904. The **sesquioctave** of this, which is **G**, is 472,392. The **sesquioctave** of this, which is **K**, is 531,441. And 531,441 <gloss>meaning K is greater</gloss>, which is **K**, is more than double 262,144, which is **A**. <note>Double 262,144 is 524,288. Since 531,441 is larger, the "six tones" exceed the octave.</note> Therefore, six **sesquioctave** proportions are larger than one **double** interval.

<summary>This page demonstrates through geometry and arithmetic that subtracting a Fourth from a Fifth leaves a Whole Tone, and proves the Pythagorean discovery that six whole tones slightly exceed the span of a single octave.</summary>
<keywords>sesquialter, sesquitertian, sesquioctave, duplex, Pythagorean comma, mathematical music theory, ratios, interval subtraction</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the mathematical proofs of musical ratios to the practical application of notation. It introduces the system of using Greek and Latin letters to represent specific pitches and string divisions, preparing the reader for a visual and technical demonstration of the Lydian mode.</meta>

### Musical notation through Greek and Latin letters. Chapter Three

**I**t remains now, as we are about to divide the string according to the aforementioned **consonances** <term>consonances: intervals that sound pleasant or "stable" together, such as the octave or fifth</term> by means of a measuring rule. Since this division will display the necessary sounds for the three types of melody, we have found it useful to add musical notes. Thus, when we have marked the divided line with these same notes, anyone can most easily recognize each name here.

For the ancient musicians, for the sake of brevity in writing—so that it would not always be necessary to set down the full names—devised certain small marks <note>original: "notulas"</note> by which the names of the strings might be noted. They divided these according to the **musical genres** <term>genera: the three classifications of musical scales in Greek theory: diatonic, chromatic, and enharmonic</term> and **modes** <term>modes: specific arrangements of intervals that created different musical "moods" or scales, such as the Lydian or Dorian</term>. We are also following this practice for the sake of utility: so that if a musician ever wished to write down a melody over a verse held together by rhythmic metrical composition, he might write these little note-symbols of sounds...

<column-break/>

...above the text. In this wonderful way, they found that not only the words of the poem—which were explained by letters—but also the melody itself, marked by these notes, would endure in memory and for future generations.

But from all these modes, let us for now arrange one, the **Lydian** <term>Lydian: a specific Greek mode often associated in this period with higher pitches or a specific emotional character</term>, and its notes through the three genres; we shall defer doing the same for the remaining modes to another time. Indeed, if I should weary the reader with the variety of Greek letters arranged for these notes, let no one be troubled by the novelty. For the Greek letters are used in any position—sometimes reduced, sometimes turned <gloss>that is</gloss> this is how this description of notes has been established.

We, however, shall sing something handed down from the authority of antiquity. Therefore, the first and upper signs will be for the speech or words. But the second and lower signs will be for the **percussion** <note>original: "percussionis"; this refers to the instrumental accompaniment, typically played on a stringed instrument like the lyre</note>. The **Proslambanomenos** <term>Proslambanomenos: the "added note" at the very bottom of the Greek musical system</term>, which can be called "the acquired note," is marked by an incomplete **zeta** <note>the Greek letter Z</note> (z) and a horizontal T. The **Hypate hypaton** <term>Hypate hypaton: literally the "highest of the highest," though it represents the lowest pitch of the lowest tetrachord</term>, which...

<image-desc>A complex red-ink diagram illustrating musical proportions. At the top, eight lettered boxes (A through H) represent notes. Above them, semi-circular arcs labeled with ratios like "**Sesquioctave proportion**" <note>a 9:8 ratio, equivalent to a Whole Tone</note> connect the letters. Below each box is a numerical value in Roman numerals representing string lengths: A (384), B (432), C (486), D (512), E (576), F (648), G (779) <note>likely a scribal error for 729</note>, and H (768). Underneath this is a large circle containing additional numerical values and the label "**Double Interval, that is, the octave**" <note>original: "Duplex Interuallum id est diapason"</note>.</image-desc>

<summary>This page explains the origins and utility of musical notation, describing how ancient musicians used modified Greek letters to represent both vocal melody and instrumental accompaniment. It focuses on the Lydian mode as a primary example and introduces the technical names for specific notes in the Greek system.</summary>
<keywords>musical notation, Greek letters, Lydian mode, Proslambanomenos, Hypate hypaton, consonances, percussion notation, musical genres</keywords>

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of musical notation symbols for the Lydian mode, identifying specific pitches across the various tetrachords (groups of four notes) used in the ancient Greek musical system. It concludes the list and introduces Chapter 4.</meta>

...is the principal of the principals <note>original: "principalis principalium," referring to the Hypate hypaton, the lowest note of the system</note>. An inverted gamma and a straight gamma. **P** Parhypate hypaton, that is, the one above the principal of the principals. An incomplete beta and an upright gamma. <gloss>other</gloss> **L** Principal enharmonic <term>enharmonic: a musical genre using very small intervals called quarter-tones</term>, which is the enharmonic of the principals. **Λ** an upright lambda and an inverted gamma with a small stroke behind it. <gloss>other</gloss> **H** Principal chromatic <term>chromatic: a musical genre using semitones, sounding more "colored" than the standard scale</term>, which is the chromatic of the principals. **Λ** an upright lambda with a line, and a downward gamma having two lines. **Y** Principal diatonic <term>diatonic: the standard musical scale consisting of whole steps and half steps</term>, which is the extended note of the principals. **Φ** a Greek phi and a digamma <note>an archaic Greek letter resembling an F</note>. <gloss>lydia</gloss> **ꓶ** Hypate meson, which is the principal of the middle strings. **C** and **E**. <gloss>lydia</gloss> **ℨ** Parhypate meson, which is the one above the principal of the middle strings. **P** and an upright **C**. <gloss>lydia</gloss> **F** Middle enharmonic: <gloss>phrygian</gloss> **Π** a Greek pi and an inverted **C**. **Π** Middle enharmonic, which is the enharmonic of the middle strings: which is the enharmonic of the middle strings, a Greek **Π** and an inverted **C**. <gloss>lydia</gloss> **H** Middle chromatic, which is the chromatic of the middle strings. <meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical ratios, moving from abstract numerical theory to the physical measurement of the monochord—a single-stringed instrument used by ancient and medieval scholars to demonstrate the mathematical laws of sound.</meta>

### 5. Division of the Regular Monochord in the Diatonic Genus

It is now time to come to the division of the regular <term>monochord</term> <note>A one-stringed scientific instrument used to demonstrate the relationship between the length of a string and the pitch it produces.</note>. Regarding this matter, it must first be said that whether the division is established by the measurement of the string or by numbers and their proportions, a larger space of string and a greater quantity of number produces lower sounds. But if the length of the string is shorter and the numbers are not so numerous, it is necessary that higher voices be emitted. And from this comparison, as much as each string is either longer or marked by more numbers, or shorter or marked by fewer, it is found to be either lower or higher. 

Nor should it trouble the reader that, when tightening the strings <note>To "intend" or "stretch" a string is to raise its pitch.</note>, we often marked the intervals of proportions with a larger number, and when loosening them, with a smaller one—even though tension creates a higher pitch and loosening creates a lower one. For in those cases, we were only marking the intervals of the proportions, not concerning ourselves with the specific property of low or high pitch; and therefore, we used larger numbers for the higher pitch and often smaller for the lower. 

Here, however, where we measure the physical spaces and sounds of the strings, it is necessary to follow the nature of things: to give larger measurements to the length of the strings from which a low pitch <note>original: *gravitas*</note> arises, and shorter measurements to those from which the high pitch <note>original: *acumen*</note> of the voice is born. Let there be a stretched string **AB**, and let there be a ruler of equal length which is divided according to the proposed partitions; and with the ruler placed against the string, let those same divisions be marked on the length of the 

<column-break/>

string which we have marked on the ruler. We, however, now divide it as if we were partitioning the string itself and not the ruler. 

Therefore, let **AB** be divided into four parts by three points which are **C, D,** and **E**. Therefore, the whole **AB** will be double the segment **DB**. Furthermore, the individual segments of **AB**—namely **AC, CD, DE,** and **EB**—are four equal parts of the whole. Therefore, the full length **AB** will produce the lowest note, that is, the **lowest note of the scale** <note>original: *proslambanomenos*</note>; while **DB** will be the **middle note** <note>original: *mese*</note>. This is because it is half of the whole length. And just as **AB** is double **DB** in physical space, so **DB** is double **AB** in pitch. For as was said above, the order of space and pitch is often reversed. For a string is higher in pitch in proportion to how much smaller it is in length. 

Because of this, **EB** will also be the **highest note of the highest group** <note>original: *nete hyperbolion*</note>, since **EB** is half of **DB** in quantity, but double it in pitch. Again, since this same **EB** is a fourth part of **AB** in space, it will be four times greater than it in pitch. Therefore, as has been said, the **highest note** <note>original: *nete hyperboleon*</note> is double the pitch of the **middle note** <note>original: *mese*</note>; while the **middle note** is double the pitch of the **lowest note** <note>original: *proslambanomenos*</note>. Furthermore, the **highest note** is four times the pitch of the **lowest note**.

Therefore, the **lowest note** will harmonize with the **middle note** at the interval of an **octave** <note>original: *diapason*</note>. The **middle note** to the **highest note** is also an **octave**. The **lowest note** <note>The scribe wrote *proslambanomenonenos* in error for *proslambanomenos*</note> to the **highest note** is a **double octave** <note>original: *bisdiapason*</note>. Again, since **AC, CD, DE,** and **EB** are equal parts, **AB** consists of four of those parts, while **CB** consists of three. Therefore, **AB** is in a **four-to-three ratio** <note>original: *sesquitertia*; this ratio creates the interval of a perfect fourth.</note> to that which...

<summary>This page explains the physical mechanics of the monochord, detailing how string length is inversely proportional to pitch. It provides the mathematical division of a string to identify key notes like the Proslambanomenos (lowest), Mese (middle), and Nete Hyperboleon (highest).</summary>
<keywords>monochord, music theory, Boethius, string length, pitch, octave, diapason, proslambanomenos, mese, nete hyperboleon</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration of how to divide a single string (the monochord) to produce specific musical notes and intervals. It explains how to calculate the positions of notes like the Lichanos Hypaton and Hypate Meson using ratios like 3:2 (fifth), 4:3 (fourth), and 9:8 (whole tone).</meta>

...is CG. Again, because CB consists of three equal parts but DB consists of two, CG will therefore be **sesquialtera** <note>A ratio of 3:2, which produces the musical interval of a fifth.</note> to that which is DB. Again, since CB is of three equal parts, just as one is EG, CB is therefore triple that of EG. Therefore, CG will be the note **lichanos hypaton diatonos** <note>One of the steps in the "lower" part of the ancient Greek musical scale.</note>. The note **proslambanomenos** <note>The lowest note of the scale, literally meaning "the added note."</note> will sound with the **lichanos hypaton diatonos** in the consonance of a **diatesseron** <note>The interval of a musical fourth (4:3 ratio).</note>. Likewise, this same **lichanos hypaton diatonos** will sound to the **mese** <note>The central or "middle" note of the scale.</note> with the consonance of a **diapenthe** <note>The interval of a musical fifth (3:2 ratio).</note>. And this same **lichanos diatonos** will sound to the **nete hyperboleon** <note>The highest note of the entire system.</note> with the interval of an **octave and a fifth** <note>original: *diapason & diapenthe*</note>. Again, if I take a ninth part away from the whole AG—that which is AF—the parts FG will be eight. Therefore, FG will be the **hypate hypaton** <note>The "lowest of the low" strings.</note>, to which AB (which is **proslambanomenos**) maintains a **sesquioctave** proportion <note>A 9:8 ratio, which defines a whole tone.</note>, which in Music is called a **tone**. The diagram above contains small signs for each note, where we have placed the notation symbols for the strings, because it would have been long to write out their full names;

<column-break/>

Likewise, if we divide AB into three segments, the third part will be AG. Two of those same parts will be GB. Therefore, AG, which is **hypate meson** <note>original: *hypate melon*. The text uses a common medieval spelling variant for the "lowest of the middle" strings.</note>, will sound the consonance of a **diapenthe** (a fifth) because it is established in the **sesquialtera** (3:2) proportion. Moreover, CB to GB will be a **sesquioctave** (9:8) and will contain a **tone**. And this falls in order. For the **lichanos hypaton diatonos** (which is CB) to that which is **hypate meson** (which is GB) contains a tone. Again, taking AB as **proslambanomenos** to CB **lichanos hypaton diatonos**, it has the consonance of a **diatesseron** (a fourth). But AG as **proslambanomenos** <note>Here referring to the physical length of the string at that point.</note> to GB **hypate meson** has the consonance of a **diapenthe** (a fifth). Likewise, CB to DB (that is, **lichanos hypaton diatonos** to the **mese**) has the consonance of a **diapenthe** (a fifth). Moreover, GB to DB (that is, **hypate meson** to the **mese**) has the consonance of a **diatesseron** (a fourth). But the **lichanos hypaton** (which is CB) compared to the **hypate meson** (which is GB) will be distant by a tone. If, however, I take a fourth part of that which is CG, it will be GR. Therefore, CB to RG will obtain a **sesquitertia** proportion <note>A 4:3 ratio, creating the interval of a musical fourth.</note>.

If, however, I take a third part of line DB, it will be DN. Therefore, NB will be the **nete diezeugmenon** <note>The "highest" note of the *diezeugmenon* (disjunct) tetrachord.</note>. If, moreover, KB is divided into two equal parts, it will be KX. And XB will be the **paranete hyperboleon** <note>The "next-to-highest" note of the "highest" tetrachord.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the mathematical division of the monochord string, moving from the specific notes of the lower tetrachords discussed on the previous page to a comprehensive system that encompasses all three musical genera (diatonic, chromatic, and enharmonic) across a two-octave range.</meta>

### ### Division of the monochord's highest notes through the three genera, 106

Now, therefore, let the description of the **diatonic genus** <term>Diatonic genus: A musical scale based on a sequence of whole tones and semitones, forming the basis of the standard Lydian mode discussed here.</term> be made in that mode which is simpler and primary, which we call the **Lydian**. We need not discuss these modes at this time. However, so that the description might be mixed through the three genera <term>Genera: The three types of ancient Greek scales: the Diatonic (tones and semitones), the Chromatic (using half-steps), and the Enharmonic (using quarter-tones).</term> and the appropriate plurality of measures be applied to all—specifically to preserve the proportions of both **tones** and **dieses** <term>Diesis: A very small musical interval; in the enharmonic genus, it represents a quarter-tone.</term>—a number was devised that could fulfill all these requirements. Let the largest number, assigned to the **proslambanomenos** <term>Proslambanomenos: The "added note," the lowest note of the ancient Greek musical system.</term>, be 9,216. The smallest, indeed, shall be 2,304. The proportions of the remaining sounds will be woven in the middle of these. Truly, we proceed from the lower notes for all the names of the strings, which we demonstrate not only by names but also by assigned letters. But since a division of three genera is to be made, the number of letters also exceeds the [standard] number of letters. Where the letters end, let us double them again in this way: when one has reached Z, we should describe the remaining strings as double A (that is, AA), double B (that is, BB), and double C (that is, CC).

<column-break/>

Therefore, let the first and largest number be 9,216, which is the measure of the whole string from point A to point LL. This A is the **proslambanomenos**, 9,216. I divide it in half at point O, so that the whole length A is double the length at O. Likewise, let O be double the length at LL. Therefore, A will be the **proslambanomenos**, O will be the **mese** <term>Mese: The "middle" note, which serves as the central structural point of the Greek scale.</term>, and LL will be the **nete hyperboleon** <term>Nete hyperboleon: The "highest of the highest," the top note of the two-octave system.</term>. Thus, A will have 9,216, while O will have half of these, which is 4,608, so that the **diapason** <note>original: *diapason*; the musical interval of an octave, defined by a 2:1 ratio.</note> consonance is achieved from the **mese** to the **proslambanomenos**. And the note at LL is half of the **mese**, so that the **proslambanomenos** is quadruple relative to the **nete hyperboleon**, sounding the **bis diapason** <note>original: *bis diapason*; a "double octave" or two-octave interval, defined by a 4:1 ratio.</note> symphony relative to it. And let LL be 2,304.

--- Page 75 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical division of the monochord string, specifically focusing on the Hyperbolaeon (the highest) tetrachord. It details the exact numerical values needed to define the notes across the three musical genera: Diatonic, Chromatic, and Enharmonic.</meta>

If, therefore, from 2,724 <note>original: ".II.DCCXXIIII."</note> I take the eighth part, that is 340 and a half, and add it [to the original], there will be for me 3,064 and a half. And HH will be 3,064 and a half, which becomes the **paranete hyperbolaeon** <term>Paranete hyperbolaeon: The "next-to-last" note of the highest tetrachord. In the diatonic genus, it is a whole tone below the highest note.</term>, maintaining a distance of one tone from the **nete hyperbolaeon** <term>Nete hyperbolaeon: The "last" or highest note of the ancient Greek musical system.</term>.

Again, from that which is HH, that is 3,064 and a half, I take the eighth part, which is 383 and a certain fraction <note>original: "vi. u." probably referring to small fractions like a sextula or unciola</note>, and I join it to the value from which it was the eighth; it will be 3,448. And there will be for me FF, 3,448, the **hyperbolaeon diatonos** in the diatonic genus, distant indeed by a tone from the note which is the **paranete hyperbolaeon**.

Truly, this same FF will be, in the **chromatic genus**, the **trite hyperbolaeon chromatica** <term>Trite: The third note of a tetrachord when counting down from the top.</term>, and in the **enharmonic genus**, it will be the **paranete hyperbolaeon enarmonios**. By which it will be recognized, or it may come about, that since we have begun the first three tetrachords of the three genera from the **nete hyperbolaeon**, we should write down the following values.

Since truly, if I take away two **sesquioctave** <term>Sesquioctave: The ratio 9:8, which defines a standard whole tone in Pythagorean tuning.</term> portions from a **sesquitertia** <term>Sesquitertia: The ratio 4:3, which defines the musical interval of a perfect fourth (diatesseron).</term> portion, they leave me a **minor semitone** <note>Also known as a leimma, the small interval left over when two whole tones are subtracted from a perfect fourth.</note>. I take a third of that which is LL—that is, the **nete hyperbolaeon**, which is [the base unit]—and add these to the same; there will be for me 3,860, which is DD, the **nete diezeugmenon** <term>Nete diezeugmenon: The highest note of the "disjunct" tetrachord, located just below the highest tetrachord.</term>, containing toward the **trite hyperbolaeon** a minor semitone.

Since the **nete diezeugmenon** maintains a fourth-interval <term>Diatesseron: A perfect fourth.</term> consonance to the **nete hyperbolaeon**, and the **trite hyperbolaeon diatonos** is distant by a tone from it, the space that remains between the **nete diezeugmenon** and the **trite hyperbolaeon** is a minor semitone. Since therefore we have completed the **hyperbolaeon tetrachord** of the diatonic genus, now the chromatic and enharmonic tetrachords must be filled in this way:

<column-break/>

For since the **paranete hyperbolaeon** is distant from the **nete hyperbolaeon** in the diatonic genus by one tone, in the chromatic indeed by three semitones <note>A minor third</note>, and in the enharmonic by two tones <note>A major third</note>: if we consider the distance of the **paranete hyperbolaeon** and the **nete hyperbolaeon** of the diatonic genus, and we add to it half of the diatonic **paranete hyperbolaeon**, we shall have a number distant by three semitones from the **hyperbolaeon nete**, and this will be the **paranete hyperbolaeon** in the chromatic genus.

I shall take therefore from 2,724—that is, from the **paranete hyperbolaeon** of the diatonic genus—[and subtract] 2,333—that is, the **nete hyperbolaeon**; there remains for me 391. I halve these: it will be 195 and a half. I add these same to the number, and it will become 3,226. This will be the **paranete hyperbolaeon chromatica**.

Again, since the **trite hyperbolaeon** in the diatonic or chromatic is distant by two tones from the **nete hyperbolaeon**, and in the enharmonic genus the **paranete hyperbolaeon** is distant by two tones from that which is the **nete hyperbolaeon**, the **paranete hyperbolaeon** in the enharmonic genus will be the same as the **trite hyperbolaeon** in the diatonic or chromatic.

Also, since the **trite hyperbolaeon** of the diatonic and chromatic genus makes a minor semitone toward the **nete diezeugmenon**, it is established that the tetrachord of the enharmonic genus consists of two whole tones and a **diesis** and a **diesis**, which are the half-spaces of a minor semitone <term>Diesis: Here used to mean a quarter-tone, the smallest interval in the enharmonic genus.</term>.

From the distance which is between the **nete diezeugmenon** and the **paranete hyperbolaeon**, I take the enharmonic [division]. Also, since the **nete diezeugmenon** is 3,860 and the **paranete hyperbolaeon enarmonios** is 3,976, the distance between these is 116. I take the half part of these, which is 58. I add these to 3,976: it will be 4,034. This will be CC, the **trite hyperbolaeon enarmonios**.

Thus, this is the description according to the three genera of the tetrachord which is the **hyperbolaeon**, the form of which we have added below. <note>The "form" refers to a diagram or table that usually follows such a description in the manuscript.</note>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical and theoretical division of the tetrachord, focusing on how the interval of a perfect fourth is subdivided into smaller intervals (tones and semitones) according to the three classical Greek musical genera: Diatonic, Chromatic, and Enharmonic.</meta>

# The logic of the previously arranged description of the pathway and the tetrachords:

It has been described by us with the following reasoning. For every **tetrachord** <term>Tetrachord: A sequence of four notes spanning the interval of a perfect fourth, the building block of ancient Greek music theory.</term> sounds the concord <note>original: "symphoniam"</note> of a perfect fourth <note>original: "diatesseron"</note>. Therefore, the **nete hyperbolaeon** <term>Nete hyperbolaeon: The highest note of the complete musical system.</term> and the **nete diezeugmenon** <term>Nete diezeugmenon: The highest note of the "disjunct" tetrachord, located a perfect fourth below the highest nete.</term>—whether in the three genera, that is, in the **diatonic**, the **chromatic**, or the **enharmonic**—contain the concord of a perfect fourth.

Moreover, the concord of a perfect fourth consists of two whole tones and a minor semitone. This is divided through the three genera in the aforementioned tetrachords in the following way:

In the **diatonic** genus, the first part—the **paranete hyperbolaeon** (that is, 2,726 <note>original: ".ii. dccxxvi."</note>) to the **nete hyperbolaeon** (that is, 2,304 <note>original: ".ii. ccciiii."</note>)—holds the distance of a whole tone, which we mark with this little sign: **T**. Again, between the **hyperbolaeon diatonos** (which is 2,881 <note>original: ".ii. dccclxxxi."</note>) and the **paranete hyperbolaeon** of the diatonic genus (which is 2,721 <note>original: ".ii. dccxxi."</note>), it again holds the distance of a whole tone, which we mark with a similar sign. But the **nete diezeugmenon** (3,072 <note>original: ".iii. lxxii"</note>) to the **trite hyperbolaeon** (2,876 <note>original: ".ii. dccclxxvi."</note>) relates as a semitone, which we mark with this sign: **S**. And this is the entire space between the [nete] diezeugmenon and the nete hypo-

<column-break/>

-rbolaeon: two tones and a semitone. But these same two tones and a semitone are divided in the **chromatic** genus by this logic. For the second genus, which is the chromatic, is described in this way: The **paranete hyperbolaeon chromatica** (2,881) compared to the **nete hyperbolaeon** (2,304) occupies the whole space of the **paranete hyperbolaeon** of the diatonic genus to the **nete hyperbolaeon**, which is one tone—that is, two semitones, major and minor.

And the space of the **paranete hyperbolaeon diatonia** and the **nete hyperbolaeon** is divided again. For it is done so that it is a half tone, but not exactly, because (as has been demonstrated most fully above) a tone cannot be divided into two equal parts <note>This refers to the Pythagorean mathematical proof that a 9:8 ratio cannot be split into two equal rational ratios.</note>. Therefore, we shall designate this space of three semitones—that is, a whole tone and a semitone <note>A "trihemitone" or minor third.</note>—in this way: **SSS**. Again, the **paranete hyperbolaeon chromatica** reaches to the **trite hyperbolaeon** a part of a tone—that is, a semitone—which remains from the two tones contained between the **trite hyperbolaeon diatonica** and the **nete hyperbolaeon**.

Thus, with four semitones <note>Counting the composite parts of the intervals.</note> subtracted, the remaining space from the whole tetrachord is a semitone, which is contained between the **nete diezeugmenon** and the **trite hyperbolaeon**. Therefore, the tetrachord consists of two tones and a semitone, divided indeed into one space of three [semitones]...

<meta>This page continues the mathematical division of the musical scale on the monochord, specifically completing the Enharmonic division of the highest (hyperbolaeon) tetrachord and beginning the division of the "disjunct" (diezeugmenon) tetrachord using the ratios of the perfect fourth, perfect fifth, and whole tone.</meta>

in two semitones and in two spaces of semitones. Truly, three spaces are contained by four strings <note>Referring to the four strings that make up a tetrachord</note>. In the **enharmonic** genus, however, there is the highest degree—that is, the power of fully knowing the system. From that note which is the **nete hyperbolaeon**, that is 2,304 <note>original: "m. cccxxxii"</note>, the **enharmonic paranete hyperbolaeon**, that is 4,926 <note>original: "iiii. dccccxxvi."</note>, stands two whole tones apart, which we will note in this way: **TT**.

There remains, therefore, from the two tones and the semitone of the whole tetrachord, one minor semitone <note>original: "scitonium"</note> which is contained between the **nete diezeugmenon** and the **enharmonic paranete hyperbolaeon**. This is divided into two **dieses** <term>Diesis: In the enharmonic genus, this is a "quarter-tone," or half of a minor semitone.</term>. Between these, the **enharmonic trite hyperbolaeon** is placed in the middle. In this manner of the spaces of a diesis, the sign **¶** is used. Thus, the **hyperbolaeon tetrachord** has been described by us.

By what path we may now come to the **diezeugmenon tetrachord** <note>The "disjunct" tetrachord, located below the highest one</note>. Nor should we linger on these same reasons in the others, unless from this description an example can also be taken for the others.

### The partition of the Netes of the Monochord through the diezeugmenon through the three genera. Chapter VIII.

The **diezeugmenon**, which is 1,672 <note>original: "m. dclxxii"</note>: if I take half, it will be 2,736 <note>original: "ii. dccxxxvi"</note>. Which, when added to the same, will be 1,708 <note>original: "m. dccviii"</note>, which is the **mese** <term>Mese: The "middle" note, serving as the central pitch and structural anchor of the ancient Greek musical system.</term>, which we designate with the letter **O**. But if from the same **nete diezeugmenon**, that is 1,674, I take away a third part, it will be 1,722 <note>original: "m. dccxxii"</note>. Which, joined to the same, will make 1,896 <note>original: "m. dcccxcvi"</note>, which for us is the **paramese**

<column-break/>

the **mese** noted below with the letter **X**. Therefore, the **nete diezeugmenon**, that is 1,672, to the **mese**, that is 1,708, since it consists in a **sesquialter proportion** <term>Sesquialter: A 3:2 ratio, which produces the musical interval of a perfect fifth.</term>, resonates with the concord of a **perfect fifth** <note>original: "diapenthe"</note>. 

Truly, the same **nete diezeugmenon**, that is 1,672, to the **paramese**, that is 1,896, is placed in a **sesquitertian proportion** <term>Sesquitertian: A 4:3 ratio, which produces the musical interval of a perfect fourth.</term> to it, and retains the concord of a **perfect fourth** <note>original: "diatesseron"</note>.

If, therefore, from that which is the **nete diezeugmenon**, 1,672, I take away an eighth part, that is 373 <note>original: "ccclxxiii"</note>, and add it to them, it becomes 1,476 <note>original: "m. cccclxxvi"</note>. And this will be the **paranete diezeugmenon diatonos**, marked with the letters **CC**. To the **nete diezeugmenon**, it holds the distance of a whole tone.

From this note, truly, if I take an eighth part (that is, from 1,476, which is 432) and add it to them, they will be 1,878 <note>original: "m. dccc lxxviii"</note>. And that will be the **trite diezeugmenon diatonos**, marked **FF**. Since the **nete diezeugmenon** held a sesquitertian proportion to the **paramese**, but the **trite diezeugmenon diatonos** is two whole tones away from the **nete diezeugmenon**, it will contain a **minor semitone** <note>original: "scitonium minus"</note> between the **trite diezeugmenon diatonos** and the **paramese**.

Thus, in the **diatonic genus**, it is also completed in this **tetrachord** and **pentachord** <term>Pentachord: A series of five notes spanning a perfect fifth.</term> in such a way that the tetrachord from the **nete diezeugmenon** to the **paramese** is the concord of a **perfect fourth**. But its pentachord, from the **nete diezeugmenon** to the **mese**, is the concord of a **perfect fifth**.

<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical division of the monochord, moving from the highest tetrachord (hyperbolaeon) into the disjunct tetrachord (diezeugmenon). It specifically details how to calculate the string lengths for the Chromatic and Enharmonic genera, building upon the Diatonic foundation previously established.</meta>

We will construct the **Enharmonic** and **Chromatic** genera in this manner. I take the distance between the **nete** and the **diatonic paranete diezeugmenon**, which are 3,772 and 3,456 <note>original: "iii. dcclxxii et iii. cccclvi"</note>. Their difference is 388 <note>original: "ccclxxxviii"</note>. Divide this, and it will be 194. I will add this sum to the **diatonic paranete diezeugmenon**, which is 3,456, and it becomes 3,670 <note>original: "iii. dclxx"</note>. This will be the **chromatic paranete diezeugmenon**, marked with the double letters **BB** <term>BB: A notation used in this manuscript to identify specific pitches in the Chromatic genus.</term>. It is distant from the **nete diezeugmenon** by a tone and a semitone—that is, by three semitones. 

Divide for the **trite diezeugmenon**, which was diatonic but is now chromatic, that is 3,888 <note>original: "iii. dccclxxxviii"</note>, a semitone. The remainder from that tone, which is the division between the **diatonic paranete diezeugmenon** and the other remainder from the tetrachord, is a semitone between the **chromatic trite diezeugmenon** and the **paramese**. This leaves the resonance of a **fourth** <term>Diatesse-ron: The interval of a perfect fourth, which defines the outer limits of a tetrachord.</term> between the **nete diezeugmenon** and the **paramese**, after subtracting the two tones which the chromatic paranete and chromatic trite contained.

<column-break/>

That which in the diatonic genus is the **diatonic trite diezeugmenon**, in the chromatic is the **chromatic trite diezeugmenon**. In the **enharmonic**, it is called the **enharmonic paranete diezeugmenon**. For it stands two whole tones distant from the **nete diezeugmenon** and is noted as **AA**. Between the **nete diezeugmenon** and the **enharmonic paranete**, no other string intervenes <note>In the enharmonic genus, the first interval is a large leap of two whole tones, skipping the positions used by other genera.</note>, and thus it is called by the name "paranete" <note>Literally "next to the nete."</note>. 

We determine the semitone which is between the **enharmonic paranete diezeugmenon** and the **paramese**—that is, between **AA** and **X**—in this way, so that two **dieses** <term>Diesis: In ancient music theory, this is a "quarter-tone," the smallest interval in the enharmonic scale.</term> are made. I take the difference between the **enharmonic paranete diezeugmenon** and the **paramese**, which are 3,888 and 4,096 <note>original: "iii. dccclxxxviii et iiii. xcvi"</note>. The difference is 208. I divide this: it will be 108 <note>The math here (208 / 2) should be 104, but the scribe records "cviii" (108).</note>. I add these to 3,888: they will become 3,993 <note>original: "iii. dccccxciii"</note>. This will be the **enharmonic trite diezeugmenon**, marked with the letter **Z**. 

From here, I have added the **hyperbolaeon tetrachord** <note>The "highest" of the four-string groupings.</note> above the proper arrangement of the tetrachord described before, so that it might be as one description, and the form of the whole arrangement might grow together little by little.

<meta>This page continues the mathematical instructions for dividing the monochord string. The previous page concluded the division of the "disjunct" (diezeugmenon) tetrachord. ### **The division of the conjunct [synemmenon] tetrachord through the three genera. Chapter VIII.**

The previous description showed how the tetrachords of the monochord are joined to one another, and how the "disjunct" <term>diezeugmenon: A tetrachord separated from the central note (mese) by a whole tone.</term> is separated from the **mese** <term>mese: The "middle" note and central anchor of the ancient Greek musical system.</term>. Now we must come to that tetrachord which is called the **synemmenon** <term>synemmenon: Meaning "conjunct," this tetrachord begins on the same note that the previous one ends on, rather than being separated by a gap.</term>, which is joined to the note called the **mese**. Since we have previously stated that the **nete diezeugmenon** and the **mese** resonate at the interval of a **fifth** <note>original: "diapethe" [diapente]</note>, and a **fifth** is a consonance composed of three whole tones and a semitone, there are indeed three tones in this pentachord. One tone is from the **diatonic nete diezeugmenon** to the **diatonic paramese diezeugmenon**; another is from the **diatonic paranete diezeugmenon** to the **diatonic trite diezeugmenon**. From the **paramese** to the **mese** is the remaining semitone <note>original: "scitonium" [semitonium]</note>, as is the interval from the **diatonic trite diezeugmenon** to the **paramese**. Because the **nete diezeugmenon** and the **paramese** tetrachord are separated from the **mese** by that tone which lies between the **paramese** and the **mese**, if we subtract one tone from that pentachord—specifically the one between the **nete diezeugmenon** and the **diatonic paranete diezeugmenon**—we can most suitably join another tetrachord to the **mese** to create the **synemmenon** (that is, the "conjunct" tetrachord) in this manner:

Since the **diatonic paranete diezeugmenon**, which is marked with the letters **CC**, is 3,456, three parts added to these will make the **mese** <note>The author is calculating a 4:3 ratio (a perfect fourth). 3456 divided by 3 is 1152; 3456 + 1152 = 4608, which is the value for the Mese.</note>. Therefore, this number, which is marked with the letters **CC** in the disjunct tetrachord, was distant by a tone from the **nete diezeugmenon** in the diatonic genus and is called the **diatonic paranete diezeugmenon**. In the **synemmenon** (conjunct) tetrachord, let the **nete synemmenon** be established across the three genera. Where it is pre-marked, an eighth part of it should be subtracted—which is 432—and when this is added to it, it becomes 3,888, which is the **paranete synemmenon**, marked with the letter **T**. Of this, an eighth part is taken,

<column-break/>

which is 486. Let this be added to the same value from which the eighth was taken; it becomes 4,374, which is the **diatonic trite synemmenon**, marked with the letter **Q**. But since the **nete synemmenon** (3,456) to the **mese** (4,608) maintains the perfect **sesquiterce** ratio <term>sesquiterce: A 4:3 ratio, which creates the interval of a perfect fourth (diatesseron).</term>, and the **trite synemmenon** to the **nete synemmenon** (that is, 4,374 to 3,456) leaves the ratio of two whole tones, the ratio of the **diatonic trite synemmenon** to the **mese** is a semitone. Thus, this tetrachord is "conjunct" with the **mese**, and for that reason, the **synemmenon** is called "continuous" or "joined." 

This is how the division of the **Diatonic** genus is made. The division of the **Chromatic** is as follows: I take the difference between the **nete synemmenon** and the **diatonic paranete synemmenon** (that is, 3,456 and 3,888). The difference is 432. I halve this so that a semitone is created: it is 216. I add this to 3,888 so that three semitones [a minor third] are created. It will be 4,104, which is the **chromatic paranete synemmenon**, marked above with the letter **S**. And from this—the **chromatic paranete synemmenon**—to the **trite synemmenon** (which was previously diatonic but is now chromatic), there is a semitone. From there to the **mese**, another semitone is found. 

But since from the **nete synemmenon** to the **diatonic or chromatic trite synemmenon** there are two whole tones in both the Diatonic and Chromatic genera, the **diatonic or chromatic trite synemmenon** is the same as the **enharmonic paranete synemmenon** in the Enharmonic genus, having the sum of 4,374, marked with the letter **R**. From this note to the **mese** is a semitone. This interval is divided into two **dieses** <term>diesis: A quarter-tone, the smallest interval used in ancient Greek music, specifically in the Enharmonic genus.</term> in this manner: I take the difference of the **enharmonic paranete synemmenon** and the **mese** (that is, 4,374 and 4,608). That is 234. I halve this, which is 117. I add this to the **enharmonic paranete synemmenon**, which is...

<note>original vocabulary: monochord, tetrachord, nete (highest note), mese (middle note), diezeugmenon (disjunct), synemmenon (conjunct), diatonic, chromatic, enharmonic, Boethius</note>

**Note Labels and Positions:**
* **Highest of the highest** <term>nete hyperbolon: The highest note of the entire system, located at the top of the 'highest' tetrachord.</term>
* **Next-to-highest of the highest** <term>paranete hyperbolon: The second highest note of the 'highest' tetrachord.</term>
* **Third of the highest** <term>trite hyperbolon: In the enharmonic genus, this note is tuned very close to the one below it to create a quarter-tone.</term>
* **Highest of the disjunct** <term>nete diezeugmenon: The highest note of the 'disjunct' tetrachord, which is separated from the middle of the scale by a whole tone.</term>
* **Next-to-highest of the disjunct** <term>paranete diezeugmenon: The second note from the top of the disjunct tetrachord.</term>
* **Third of the disjunct** <term>trite diezeugmenon: The third note of the disjunct tetrachord.</term>
* **Next-to-middle** <term>paramese: The note sitting immediately above the 'mese' (middle note); it serves as the bridge to the higher tetrachords.</term>
* **Middle** <term>mese: The central anchor of the Greek scale, acting as a reference point for all other intervals.</term>
* **Index-finger of the middles** <term>lichanos meson: The third note from the bottom of the 'middle' tetrachord, traditionally played with the index finger.</term>
* **Next-to-lowest of the middles** <term>parhypate meson: The second note from the bottom of the 'middle' tetrachord.</term>
* **Lowest of the middles** <term>hypate meson: The fundamental or bottom note of the 'middle' tetrachord.</term>
* **Index-finger of the lowest** <term>lichanos hypaton: The third note from the bottom of the 'lowest' tetrachord.</term>
* **Next-to-lowest of the lowest** <term>parhypate hypaton: The second note from the bottom of the 'lowest' tetrachord.</term>
* **Lowest of the lowest** <term>hypate hypaton: The very lowest note in this system's arrangement.</term>

<margin>
**THE ENHARMONIC GENUS**
</margin>

...4384 will become 4382. Let this point be marked with the letter **P** and the name **third note of the conjunct tetrachord** <term>trite synemmenon: The 'third' note of the conjunct tetrachord, used here in its enharmonic tuning.</term> enharmonic. And there will be a semitone <note>In the enharmonic genus, the traditional semitone is divided into two much smaller intervals called "dieses" or quarter-tones. The text here describes the collective interval between the central note and the upper notes of the tetrachord.</note> which is maintained between the **next-to-highest conjunct note** <term>paranete synemmenon: The 'next-to-highest' note of the conjunct tetrachord.</term> enharmonic and the **middle note** <term>mese: The 'middle' note, which serves as the fundamental anchor for the entire Greek musical system.</term>; the same relationship exists between the numbers 4384 and 4632. By lowering the **next-to-highest conjunct note** enharmonic—specifically the one situated at the value 4382—the calculation of the rest of this tetrachord is now fully explained. Now, however, we must construct a diagram joined with all the other parts—that is, the **highest** <term>hyperboleon: The highest tetrachord of the Greater Perfect System.</term> and the **disjunct** <term>diezeugmenon: The 'disjunct' tetrachord, separated from the central note by a whole tone.</term>—so that the ordered arrangement of the notes may proceed step by step.

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for the mathematical division of the monochord, a single-stringed instrument used to demonstrate musical ratios. # Partition of the Monochord’s Lowest Tetrachord by Genera
# And the writer's complete arrangement.

**N**ow, therefore, the <term>hypaton<gloss>The lowest grouping of four notes in the Ancient Greek musical system.</gloss></term> tetrachord is divided through the three <term>genera<gloss>The three types of scales: Diatonic (whole tones and semitones), Chromatic (using semitones), and Enharmonic (using quarter-tones).</gloss></term>. I take the *hypate meson* <note>original: *hypatē meson*; the "principal middle" note, which serves as the anchor for these calculations</note>, that is 6,249. I take half of this part, which is 3,124. If I add this to the same number, it becomes 9,372, which is the <term>proslambanomenos<gloss>The "added note," the lowest pitch in the entire musical system.</gloss></term>, preserving a <term>diapente<gloss>A perfect fifth; an interval with the ratio of 3:2.</gloss></term> consonance in relation to the *hypate meson*. Furthermore, if I take a third part of the same *hypate meson* (6,249), which is 2,083, and add it to the same, the result is 8,336; this is the *hypate hypaton*. The *hypate meson* stands at a <term>diapente<gloss>Perfect fifth</gloss></term> consonance to the *proslambanomenos*, and a <term>diatesseron<gloss>A perfect fourth; an interval with the ratio of 4:3.</gloss></term> from the *hypate hypaton*. Now, let an eighth part of the *hypate meson* (6,249) be taken away <note>The Latin here describes the ratio for a whole tone (9:8).</note>; there will be 882. If one joins this to the same, it becomes 7,337, which is the <term>lycanos hypaton diatonos<gloss>The "index-finger" note of the lowest tetrachord in the Diatonic genus.</gloss></term>, holding the proportion of a whole tone to the *hypate meson*. Again, from 6,912, let an eighth part be taken away, which is 882. If this is coupled with the same, it becomes 7,794, which is the *parhypate hypaton diatonos*, preserving the distance of a whole tone to the *lycanos hypaton diatonos*. Therefore, a <term>semitonium<gloss>A semitone or half-step.</gloss></term> is left between the *parhypate hypaton diatonos* and the *hypate hypaton*, that is, between 7,794 and 8,336. Such is the tetrachord of the *hypaton* in the Diatonic genus.

We divide the Chromatic genus by the following reasoning. I take the difference between the *hypate meson* and that which is the *lycanos hypaton diatonos*, namely 6,249 and 6,912; that is 768. I divide this in half so that I may create two <term>schitoma<gloss>Likely a variant of 'schisma,' a very small interval used to split tones in precise tuning.</gloss></term>; they will be 384. By adding this to 6,912 so that three *schitoma* are made, the result will be 7,297. This will be the *lycanos hypaton* of the Chromatic genus, distant from the *hypate meson* by three *schitoma*. Therefore, two *schitoma* remain: one

<column-break/>

indeed between the *lycanos hypaton* of the Chromatic genus and the *parhypate hypaton* of the Chromatic genus (that is, between 7,297 and 7,768), and another between the *parhypate hypaton* of the Chromatic genus and the *hypate hypaton* (that is, between 7,768 and 8,336).

There remains the Enharmonic genus, whose division—following the previous model—is as follows. Since the *parhypate hypaton diatonos* (or the *hypate hypaton* of the Chromatic genus), which is marked with 7,768 units, stands two whole tones away from the *hypate meson*, it will be the same in the Enharmonic genus: the *lycanos hypaton enarmonios*, which differs from the *hypate meson* by two full whole tones. There remains, therefore, from the *diatesseron* consonance, a semitone which lies between the *lycanos hypaton enarmonios* and the *hypate hypaton* (that is, between 7,768 and 8,336). This we divide into two <term>diesis<gloss>A quarter-tone; the smallest interval in the Enharmonic genus.</gloss></term> in this way: First, the difference between the *lycanos hypaton enarmonios* and the *hypate hypaton* (7,768 and 8,336) is 466. I take half of this, which is 233. By adding this to 7,768, the result is 7,881, which becomes the *parhypate hypaton enarmonios*. Under this, therefore, are two *diesis*: One which is between the *lycanos hypaton enarmonios* and the *parhypate hypaton enarmonios* (between 7,768 and 7,881). The other is that which is between the *parhypate hypaton enarmonios* and the *hypate hypaton* (between 7,881 and 8,336). The final whole tone is contained between the *proslambanomenos* and the *hypate hypaton*, that is, between 9,372 and 8,336.

Thus, the *hypaton* tetrachord is divided according to the three genera: Diatonic, Chromatic, and Enharmonic. If the higher tetrachords—the *hyperboleon*, *diezeugmenon*, *synemmenon*, and *meson*—are joined to these, there is a complete and perfect description of the whole regular monochord...

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for dividing the monochord. Having previously established the calculations for the lowest tetrachord (hypaton), Boethius now applies the same logic to the middle tetrachord (meson), demonstrating how to find the specific mathematical points for the three different musical genera: Diatonic, Chromatic, and Enharmonic.</meta>

# Division of the Middle Tetrachord of the Monochord by the Three Genera

<margin>·</margin>
**F**rom what has been said before in section 110, I do not think I should labor much longer. For according to the same model, the other <term>tetrachords<gloss>A grouping of four notes, the building block of the Ancient Greek musical scale.</gloss></term>, the <term>meson<gloss>The "middle" tetrachord.</gloss></term> and the <term>hypaton<gloss>The "lowest" tetrachord.</gloss></term>, must also be constructed. And first, indeed, we shall describe the <term>diatonic<gloss>The most common scale type, consisting of two whole tones and one semitone.</gloss></term> meson tetrachord in this order: I take the <term>mese<gloss>The central "middle" note of the entire scale.</gloss></term>, which is 4,608; I take a third part of it, which is 1,536. I join this to the same number, and they become 6,144. Let that be **H**, the <term>hypate meson<gloss>The "principal of the middle" notes.</gloss></term>, containing a <term>diatesseron<gloss>A perfect fourth interval, based on the ratio 4:3.</gloss></term> consonance in relation to the *mese*. This is divided by two tones and a semitone in this way: I take an eighth part of the *mese* (4,608), which is 576. I add this to the same; they become 5,184. That is the <term>lichanos meson diatonos<gloss>The diatonic "index-finger" note of the middle tetrachord.</gloss></term> <note>original: *lyranos*; a scribal error for *lichanos*.</note>, which is also **O**. Of this, let an eighth part again be taken, which is 648. I add this to the same, and they become 5,832. Let that be **I**, the <term>parhypate meson diatonos<gloss>The "next-to-the-principal" note of the middle tetrachord.</gloss></term>, holding the interval of a tone in relation to the *lichanos meson diatonos*, and being two tones distant from the *mese*. The remaining semitone is established between the *hypate meson diatonos* and the *parhypate meson diatonos*, that is, at 6,144.

**W**e divide this same tetrachord—between the 5,832 <note>This refers to the *parhypate* calculated above.</note> of the *mese* and the *hypate meson*—into the <term>chromatic genus<gloss>A scale that uses smaller intervals, including semitones and minor thirds.</gloss></term> by the following reasoning. I take the difference of the *mese* (4,608) to the *lichanos meson diatonos* (5,184), which is 576. I divide this into two equal halves; they become 288. I add this to the larger measure <note>meaning the larger of the two numbers in the interval ratio</note>, which is 5,184; they become 5,472, which shall be **N**, the *lichanos meson chromatice*. There remain, therefore, two semitones: one between the *lichanos meson chromatice* and the *parhypate meson chromatice* (that is, between 5,472 and 5,832), and another between the *parhypate meson chromatice* and the *hypate meson* (that is, between 5,832 and 6,144).

**W**e divide the <term>enharmonic genus<gloss>The most complex scale, using "quarter-tones" known as dieses.</gloss></term> in this manner. Since that which was

<column-break/>

the *parhypate meson diatonos* (or that which was the *parhypate meson chromatice*) was distant from the *mese* by two tones, holding the number 5,832, that note in the enharmonic genus will be the *lichanos meson enarmonios*, marked with the letter **L**. It holds the distance of two tones from the *mese* nonetheless. The remaining semitone, therefore, which is between the *lichanos meson enarmonios* and the *hypate meson* (that is, between 5,832 and 6,144), we divide into two <term>diesis<gloss>Small intervals, often called quarter-tones, characteristic of the enharmonic genus.</gloss></term> in this way: I take the difference between 5,832 and 6,144, which is 312. I divide this into two equal halves; they become 156. I join this to 5,832, and it becomes 5,988. And let this be **K**, the *parhypate meson enarmonios*. Thus, there are two *diesis* between the *lichanos meson enarmonios* and the *parhypate meson enarmonios* (that is, between 5,832 and 5,988) and between the *parhypate meson enarmonios* and the *hypate meson* (that is, between 5,988 and 6,144).

**T**hus the *meson* tetrachord has been divided. Let it be placed in the diagram so that it is joined with the tetrachords described above.

1. **The Five Tetrachords:** tetrachord of the lowest notes, tetrachord of the middle notes, tetrachord of the conjunct (joined) notes, tetrachord of the disjunct (separated) notes, and the tetrachord of the highest notes.

4. **Harmonic Ratios:** Central to the diagram are the numbers 6, 8, 9, and 12, illustrating the mathematical proportions of musical harmony (e.g., 6:12 is an octave, 6:8 is a fourth).

# THE CHROMATIC GENUS
<note>original: "CHROMATICVM". The Chromatic genus is one of the three "colors" or classifications of musical scales in ancient Greek theory, characterized by a sequence of a minor third and two semitones.</note>

The labels identify different groups of four notes (tetrachords): the lowest, the middle, the disjunct (separated), the highest, and the conjunct (joined). Individual strings or notes of the Greater Perfect System are identified by their position. Key Roman numerals (16, 15, 12, 9, 8, 6) represent mathematical proportions, while larger numbers (e.g., 288, 256) denote monochord string lengths or specific ratios required to produce these chromatic pitches. On the far right, a vertical grid lists Greek-style musical notation symbols alongside Latin letters. The diagram is drawn in red and black ink to distinguish different harmonic relationships.
