<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the Greek musical system, specifically outlining the relationships between notes in the Greater Perfect System and the classification of pitches as fixed or variable.</meta>

## XII. The Logic of the Diagram Arranged Above

In the diagram above, it is shown that the **diapason** <term>diapason: an octave interval</term> spans from the **proslambanomenos** <term>proslambanomenos: the lowest note of the system, literally 'the acquired one'</term> to the **mese** <term>mese: the middle note</term>, and from the **mese** to the **nete hyperboleon** <term>nete hyperboleon: the highest note of the system</term>. A double **diapason** <term>bis diapason: an interval of two octaves</term> spans from the **proslambanomenos** to the **nete hyperboleon**.

A **diatessaron** <term>diatessaron: an interval of a perfect fourth</term> consonance is formed between:
*   **hypate hypaton** and **hypate meson**
*   **hypate meson** and **mese**
*   **mese** and **nete synemmenon**
*   **paramese** and **nete diezeugmenon**
*   **nete diezeugmenon** and **nete hyperboleon**

Within these consonances, we find four **tetrachords** <term>tetrachord: a group of four notes spanning a fourth</term>. So that the entire order of the strings <note>Latin: *nervorum*</note> may be more clearly seen in this arrangement, there are only five groups called tetrachords according to their types.
1. The first and lowest is the **hypaton** [tetrachord of the lows], of which the leader is **hypate hypaton** and the last note is **hypate meson**.
2. The second is the **meson** [tetrachord of the middles], whose leader is **hypate meson** and the furthest note is the **mese**.
3. The third is the **synemmenon** [the conjunct or connected tetrachord], whose leader is the **mese** and whose final note is the **nete synemmenon**.
4. The fourth is the **diezeugmenon** [the disjunct or separated tetrachord], whose first note is the **paramesos** and the **nete diezeugmenon** is the furthest.
5. The fifth is the **hyperboleon** [the highest tetrachord], of which the leader is **nete diezeugmenon** and the **nete hyperboleon** serves as the last note.

## XIII. Concerning Standing and Movable Voices

Of all these voices <note>In this context, 'voices' refers to specific musical pitches or notes on the scale</note>, some are entirely **immobile** [fixed], some are entirely **mobile** [variable], and some sound neither entirely immobile nor entirely mobile.

The entirely **immobile** notes are: **proslambanomenos**, **hypate hypaton**, **hypate meson**, **mese**, **nete synemmenon**, **paramese**, **nete diezeugmenon**, and **nete hyperboleon**. They are fixed because they remain the same across all **genera** <term>genera: the three types of scales—diatonic, chromatic, and enharmonic</term>, changing neither their names nor their positions, whether they are contained in **pentachords** or **tetrachords**. An example of a pentachord is from **proslambanomenos** to **hypate meson**...

<column-break/>

...and from **mese** to **nete diezeugmenon**. Examples of tetrachords include **hypate hypaton** to **hypate meson** and **hypate meson** to **mese**.

The **mobile** notes are those that change according to the individual genera, such as the **paranete** and **lycanos** in the **diatonic** and **chromatic** scales, and the **trite** and **parhypate** in the **enharmonic**. The **paranete hyperboleon diatonos** [the diatonic version] is one thing, the **paranete hyperboleon chromatica** [the chromatic version] is another, and the **trite enarmonios** [the enharmonic version] is yet another.

The **paranete diezeugmenon diatonos** and **chromatica** are also different from one another, and the **trite diezeugmenon enarmonios** is not the same note as found in the other genera. Likewise, the **paranete synemmenon diatonos** and **chromatica** and the **trite synemmenon enarmonios** are not the same as the **trite** in the remaining genera. The **lycanos meson diatonos** and **lycanos meson chromatica** also differ. Furthermore, the **parhypate meson enarmonios** is found to be similar to the **parhypate** of no other genera. Neither do the **lycanos hypaton diatonos** and the **lycanos hypaton chromatica** sound at the same positions or in the same manner. Indeed, the **parhypate hypaton enarmonios** is found to be dissimilar to the other **parhypates**.

Notes are neither entirely immobile nor mobile when they remain the same in two genera—namely, the **chromatic** and the **diatonic**—but are changed in the **enharmonic**. This may be considered as follows: the **trite hyperboleon diatonos** and the **trite hyperboleon chromatica** are the same, and in the diagram described above, this is the note [numbered] 8,826. But in the **enharmonic** genus, we find another note for the first **trite**, namely 9,826. Since this voice is common to all, it is changed within the tone.

The same occurs in the **diezeugmenon** diatonic tetrachord. For the **trite diezeugmenon diatonos** and the **trite diezeugmenon chromatica** are the same and harmonize with each other, but the **trite diezeugmenon enarmonios** differs from those above. In the **synemmenon** tetrachord, it is the same: the **trite synemmenon diatonos** and **trite synemmenon chromatica** are the same, but the **trite synemmenon diatonos** is divided. Likewise, the **parhypate**...

<summary>This page explains the structure of the Greek Greater Perfect System, identifying the five tetrachords and classifying pitches as fixed (standing) or variable (movable) across the three musical genera.</summary>
<keywords>tetrachord, diapason, diatessaron, musical genera, diatonic, chromatic, enharmonic, standing voices, mobile voices, Boethius, music theory</keywords>

--- Page 89 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical classification of notes within the Greek musical system, focusing on how note names change between the three genera (diatonic, chromatic, and enharmonic) and introducing the "species" of the primary consonances: the fourth, fifth, and octave.</meta>

The **meson diatonos** <term>meson diatonos: the 'diatonic of the middles,' the third note of the middle tetrachord</term> and the **parhypate meson** <term>parhypate meson: 'next to the highest of the middles'</term> in the chromatic genus are noted with the same symbols. But in the enharmonic genus, just as described above, this **parhypate** is found next to the **hypate meson**. It is found there so that in the sharpness of the sound <note>Latin: *soni acumine*</note> it is divided from the remaining **parhypates** of the diatonic and chromatic genera. Again, the **parhypate hypaton** <term>parhypate hypaton: the second note of the lowest tetrachord</term> in the chromatic is the same [as the diatonic]; but it is not the same in the enharmonic in terms of its quantity. 

However, so that the incomplete mutability of these notes may be understood more clearly, let us return to the **hyperboleon** tetrachord <term>tetrachord hyperboleon: the highest of the four-note groupings in the system</term>. In this genus, because there is a **trite hyperboleon** <term>trite hyperboleon: 'the third note of the highest tetrachord'</term> in the diatonic and chromatic genera, the same note is changed in the enharmonic and becomes the **paranete** <term>paranete: 'next to the lowest' of a tetrachord</term>. Likewise, what was called the **trite diezeugmenon** <term>trite diezeugmenon: the third note of the 'disjunct' tetrachord</term> in the diatonic and chromatic genera is called the **paranete** in the enharmonic. What was seen as the **trite synemmenon** <term>trite synemmenon: the third note of the 'conjunct' tetrachord</term> in the chromatic or diatonic will be found as the same **paranete** in the enharmonic. Furthermore, what appeared as the **parhypate meson** in the chromatic or diatonic will be found as the same **lycanos meson** <term>lycanos meson: the 'indicator' note of the middle tetrachord, played by the index finger</term> in the enharmonic. And what was called the **parhypate hypaton** in the diatonic or chromatic is now named the **lycanos hypaton** in the enharmonic.

There are certain notes that are **immobile** <note>Fixed pitches that do not change regardless of the genus (tuning) being used.</note>: the **proslambanomenos**, **hypate hypaton**, **hypate meson**, **mese**, **nete synemmenon**, **paranete** <note>The transcription lists *paranete* as immobile here, though it is usually mobile; it likely refers to its fixed position as a boundary in this specific context</note>, **nete diezeugmenon**, and **nete hyperboleon**. The **mobile** notes, however, are those we call **lycanas**, or **paranetes**, or the **diatonics**, **chromatics**, and **enharmonics**. These are not entirely mobile or immobile, because we call them **parhypates** or **tritas** in the diatonic or chromatic, but we call them **lycanas** or **paranetes** in the enharmonic genus.

### XIV. On the Species of Consonances

Now we must treat the species of the primary consonances. The primary consonances are: the **diapason** [octave], the **diapente** [fifth], and the **diatesseron** [fourth]. A "species" is a certain proper position having its own form according to each genus, established within the terms

<column-break/>

of the proportions creating the consonance. For example, in the diatonic genus: if we place the **diezeugmenon** tetrachord with the **hyperboleon** tetrachord and the **mese**, there will be fifteen strings. But if from these the **proslambanomenos** is removed, there will be fourteen. Let these <margin>A</margin> be arranged in this manner:
Let **A** be the **hypate hypaton**, <margin>B</margin> **B** the **parhypate hypaton**, <margin>C</margin> **C** the **hypaton lycanos**, <margin>D</margin> **D** the **hypate meson**, <margin>E</margin> **E** the **parhypate meson**, <margin>F</margin> **F** the **lycanos meson**, <margin>G</margin> **G** the **mese**, <margin>H</margin> **H** the **paramese**, <margin>K</margin> **K** the **trite diezeugmenon**, <margin>L</margin> **L** the **paranete diezeugmenon**, <margin>M</margin> **M** the **nete diezeugmenon**, <margin>N</margin> **N** the **trite hyperboleon**, <margin>X</margin> <gloss>Paranete</gloss> **X** the **paranete hyperboleon**, <margin>O</margin> and **O** the **nete hyperboleon**.

From the **hypate** [A] to the **paramese** [H] is a **diapason** [octave] consonance. From the same **mese** [G] to the **lycanos hypaton** [C] <gloss>First <unclear>way</uncase></gloss> is a **diapente** [fifth]. From the **mese** [G] to the **hypate meson** [D] is a **diatesseron** [fourth]. Therefore, the **diapason** will consist of eight strings, the **diatesseron** of four, and the **diapente** of five. Because of this, the **diatesseron** will have three species, the **diapente** four species, and the **diapason** seven species. There will always be one fewer species than there are voices <note>The number of possible "species" or patterns of intervals is always the number of notes in the consonance minus one.</note>.

To begin with the **mese** [G]: the species of the **diatesseron** consonance are three, in this manner:
1. One species will be from **G** to **D**.
2. The second will be from **F** to **C**.
3. The third will be from **E** to **B**.

And the species of the **diatesseron** extend this far. This is because up to this point, the species contain two [internal] strings: for the **diatesseron** **GD**, these are **E** and **F**; for **FC**, they are **E** and **D**; and for **EB**, they are **C** and **D**. If you add to these the **diatesseron** **DA**, it will be different from the one that is **GD**. It will not contain only the string of the **GD** consonance, that is **O** alone <note>The text here seems to be referencing specific positions in a diagram or calculation of intervals</note>. Therefore, **GD** excels the consonance, and thus it is declared that the **diatesseron** has three species. It is the same in the other consonances. The **diapente**...

<summary>This page explains the shifting nomenclature of notes across the three musical genera and defines the mathematical "species" of the fourth, fifth, and octave, illustrating them using a labeled system of fourteen strings.</summary>
<keywords>fixed strings, movable strings, species of consonance, diatesseron, diapente, diapason, tetrachord, musical genera, Boethius, Greek music theory</keywords>

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical and musical classification of intervals within the Greek Great Perfect System. Having previously discussed how notes change across the three genera, the author now defines the "species" (the specific internal patterns of steps) for the primary musical consonances: the Fourth, the Fifth, and the Octave.</meta>

But there will be four species <note>The text here begins describing the species of the Fifth, known as the Diapente, which has four variations</note> in this way: one indeed from **H** to **D**; another from **G** to **C**; another from **F** to **B**; and another from **E** to **A**. There will also be seven species of the consonance of the octave <term>Diapason: the interval of an octave</term> in this manner: the first from **O** to **G**; the second from **X** to **F**; the third from **N** to **E**; the fourth from **I** to **D**; the fifth from **L** to **C**; the sixth from **K** to **B**; and the seventh from **H** to **A**. It is clear, therefore, from what has been said, that the consonance of the fourth <term>Diatesseron: the interval of a fourth</term> is contained only once among the immobile and fixed notes <note>Latin: *immobilibus ac statutis vocibus*. These are the notes that do not change pitch regardless of the musical genus</note>. For if it begins from the **hypate hypaton** <term>hypate hypaton: the lowest note of the lowest tetrachord</term>, such as **AD**—that is, from the **hypate hypaton** to the **hypate meson**—it is the first in this order. For the others are not bounded by fixed notes, such as **BC**, **EF**, and **NA**; and the **parhypate hypaton**, **parhypate meson**, **lichanos hypaton**, and **lichanos meson** have been shown to be "mobile" notes <note>Mobile notes change their tuning between the Diatonic, Chromatic, and Enharmonic systems</note>. But if we again begin a consonance of the fourth from the **hypate meson**, there will be a species of the fourth bounded by fixed notes at **GD**; this is the first species, from the **hypate meson** to the **mese** <term>mese: the 'middle' note, the central anchor of the Greek scale</term>. The remaining intervals are by no means composed of fixed notes, such as **EA** and **FR**. For the **parhypate meson**, **lichanos meson**, and **trite diezeugmenon** are not proven to be immobile. Again, if the **paramese** <term>paramese: the note 'next to the middle' in the higher register</term> takes up the beginning of that same fourth, the species of the fourth which is constrained by fixed sounds will be **HO**; that is, from the **paramese** to the **nete diezeugmenon**, which is the first. For the others, which are **KN** and **LX**, are bounded by mobile sounds. For we have said before that the **trite diezeugmenon**, **paranete diezeugmenon**, **trite hyperboleon**, and **paranete hyperboleon** are mobile notes. Likewise, the consonance of the octave <note>diapason</note> holds only two species that are included between fixed notes. For example, if we begin from the **hypate meson**, one

<column-break/>

is **OH**, which is from the **hypate meson** to the **paramese**, and that is the first. The other is **GO**, which is from the **mese** to the **nete diezeugmenon**, and this is the fourth species. The others, namely **EK** and **FL**, are by no means enclosed by fixed notes; for the **parhypate**, **lichanos**, **trite**, and **paranete** are called "unstable." There will be a reason for this, however, if these species of consonance are considered from the **nete diezeugmenon** toward the lower part—that is, from the **mese**. For they will be contained by the same immobile notes which were mentioned above. Whether we lead the consonances toward the lower part from the **hypate meson**, or from the **mese**, or from the **paramese**, or even from the **nete hyperboleon**, there cannot be a distinction between the two that are constrained by fixed notes. But the order of the octave <note>The Latin text here reads *diapason dissonantie* ("dissonance of the octave"), likely a scribal error for *consonantie* (consonance) given the context</note>, whether the sequence is taken from the **hypate hypaton** to the **paramese**, or from the **nete hyperboleon** to the **mese**, will obtain only three species <note>This refers to species formed between specific fixed points in the Great Perfect System</note>. For among those beginning from the **hypate hypaton**, one is **AH**, which is the first, from the **hypate hypaton** to the **paramese**. Another is **ON**, which is the fourth, from the **hypate meson** to the **nete diezeugmenon**. Then there is **GO**, which is the seventh, from the **mese** to the **nete hyperboleon**. The outer notes of the remaining species are in no way fixed. For the **parhypate**, **lichanos**, **trite**, and **paranete**, as was also said above, are not immobile. Similarly, and through those same notes, if we begin from the **nete hyperboleon**, the order of the species is woven together. The following description <note>referring to a diagram usually found in the manuscript</note> makes the understanding of all these things more clear.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical intervals from page 43, moving from the definition of the "species" of the fourth, fifth, and octave to how these species form the foundation of the musical modes. The author, likely Boethius, is explaining the transition from mathematical ratios to the practical "modes" or "keys" used in ancient music.</meta>

### On the beginnings of the modes, in which the arrangement of notes through each mode and voice is given. Chapter XV.

From the species of the consonance of the octave <term>diapason: the interval of an eight-note scale</term> arise what are called modes <note>original: *modi*</note>, which some also call "tones" or "tropes." These tropes are systems <note>original: *constitutiones*</note> in the entire range of notes, differing from one another in being either higher or lower in pitch. A "system" is, as it were, a complete body of melody consisting of a combination of consonances, such as the octave, the octave-plus-fifth <term>diapason and diapente</term>, or the double-octave <term>bisdiapason</term>. For instance, an octave system is formed from the lowest added note <term>proslambanomenon: the base note of the Greek scale</term> to the middle note <term>mese</term>, with the other intermediate notes included in the mix. Or, again, from the note next to the middle <term>paramese</term> to the highest note of all <term>nete hyperbolon</term>, with the intermediate notes inserted; or from the lowest note of the middle tetrachord <term>hypate meson</term> to the highest note of the separated tetrachord <term>nete diezeugmenon</term>, including those notes which these outer boundaries enclose in the middle. Indeed, an octave-plus-fourth <term>diapason and diatesseron</term> system is that which consists of the range from the lowest added note to the highest note of the conjunct tetrachord <term>nete synemmenon</term>, with the middle notes inserted. The double-octave system, however, is considered to span from the lowest added note to the highest note of all <term>nete hyperbolon</term>, including every note placed in between. These systems, therefore,

<column-break/>

if someone makes them all higher in pitch, or lowers them all toward the bass, according to the aforementioned species of the octave consonance, he will produce seven modes. The names of these are as follows: **Hypodorian**, **Hypophrygian**, **Hypolydian**, **Dorian**, **Phrygian**, **Lydian**, and **Mixolydian**. Their order may be understood in this way: Let an order of notes be arranged in the diatonic genus <term>diatonic: a scale that moves primarily by whole steps</term> from the lowest added note to the highest note of all, and let this be the **Hypodorian** mode. If someone, then, raises the pitch of the lowest added note <note>original: *proslambanomenon*</note> by a whole tone, and likewise raises the lowest of the low <note>original: *hypate hypaton*</note> by that same tone, making them higher, the entire arrangement would be pitched higher than it was before it received the upward tension of that tone. Thus, the entire system, having been made higher, will result in the **Hypophrygian** mode. But if, in this Hypophrygian mode, the notes again receive the tension of a whole tone, the melody of the **Hypolydian** will be born. And if someone raises the Hypodorian <note>This is likely a scribal error for "Hypolydian," as the Dorian is traditionally a semitone above the Hypolydian in this construction</note> by a semitone <term>semitone: a half-step</term>, he will create the **Dorian**. In the others, there is a similar process of raising the tension toward the higher pitches. So that the logic of these things might not only be grasped by the intellect, but also that their form might be recognized by the eyes, the visual description of music handed down by the ancients must be set below. But since, in each mode, every single note was marked with different symbols by the ancient musicians, a diagram of lines should first be placed here, so that once these symbols are understood on their own, it will be easier to survey the full description of the modes.

### A description containing the order and difference of the modes. Chapter XVI

<columns>2</columns>

The description above <note>original: *Superior descriptio*</note> contains the names of the strings <term>chordarum: the strings of the lyre, representing specific pitches</term>. The written marks <note>original: *Notulas*</note> placed beside them signify—through the addition of the names Lydian, Phrygian, or Dorian—which note belongs to which mode. But since we have said that these modes are found in the species of the octave <term>diapason: the interval of an eight-note scale</term> consonance,

<column-break/>

come, let us describe these same modes in the diatonic genus <term>diatonic: a scale moving primarily by whole tones and half tones</term> alone, so that their order, when placed before the eyes, may be understood without confusion.

## The Reasoning for the Arrangement of the Modes Described Above. Chapter 17.

**W**e have indeed said that there are seven modes, but let it not seem troublesome that an eighth has been attached above them. We will explain the reason for this addition a little later. For now, this must be considered: that among these small cells <note>original: *paginule*</note> which the arrangement of intersecting lines distinguishes, some indeed contain musical notations, while others have none at all. For example, in the mode labeled "Hypermixolydian," the first cell is marked with the letter omega (Ꞷ) and the third with phi (ϕ), but the second cell is empty of notation. In this gap, therefore, it is demonstrated that a whole tone <term>tonus: a musical interval consisting of two semitones</term> exists between the notations.

<column-break/>

However, regarding the fact that a note, rather than an empty cell, divides the third cell [containing] phi (ϕ) and the fifth cell [containing] psi (ψ), the established practice derived from the proper order declares that they differ by a semitone <term>semitonium: a half-step or half-tone</term>. This is proven in the following way: For if omega (Ꞷ) is the proslambanomenos <term>proslambanomenos: the lowest "added" note of the Greek scale</term>, phi (ϕ) is the hypate hypaton <term>hypate hypaton: the lowest note of the first group of four</term>, and psi (ψ) is the parhypate hypaton <term>parhypate hypaton: the note placed next to the lowest</term>, it is necessary that a distance of a full tone exists between the proslambanomenos (Ꞷ) and the hypate hypaton (ϕ). Between the hypate hypaton (ϕ) and the parhypate hypaton (ψ), however, the difference of a semitone is contained. Therefore, this must be considered a rule in all instances: that if the notations... <note>The text ends mid-sentence as it transitions to the next page.</note>

<summary>This page explains the spatial logic of a musical diagram, showing how the presence or absence of gaps between Greek notation symbols represents the intervals of tones and semitones within the modes.</summary>
<keywords>musical notation, Greek modes, Hypermixolydian, tonus, semitonium, proslambanomenos, hypate hypaton, parhypate hypaton, music theory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical demonstration of how the visual grid of musical notation represents specific intervals. distinguishes an entire cell. But if a note <note>original: *notulas*</note> rather than a cell <note>original: *pagina*</note> separates the pitches, let us not be unaware that the distance is a semitone.

Having established these premises, if two scales <note>original: *ordines*</note> arranged in a **double-octave consonance** <term>bisdiapason: an interval spanning two octaves</term> are compared to one another, so that it may be recognized which scale is lower <note>original: *gravior*</note>, then if the proslambanomenos <term>proslambanomenos: the lowest note of the Greek scale</term> is lower, or any other note of that same position is marked as a lower voice in that same genus, it is certain that the entire natural scale is lower. 

However, this is better measured at the middle note, which is the **mese** <term>mese: the central pitch and functional heart of the Greek scale</term>. For of two scales in double-octave consonance, the one whose **mese** is lower will have its entire scale be lower as well. For when the other individual notes are compared one by one, they will nonetheless be found to be lower. 

Thus, if one **mese** appears either higher <note>original: *acutior*</note> or lower than another by a whole tone, all the strings, if they are in the same genus and compared one by one, will appear lower by a tone. Or, if we look at four middle notes, if the first maintains the distance of a **fourth** <term>diatesseron: a musical interval of four steps</term> from the fourth note, while the first differs from the second by a tone, and the second likewise differs from the third by a tone, it follows that the third will make a difference of a semitone from the fourth. In this way, let there be four middle notes, **A, B, C,** and **D**; and when **A** is compared with **D**, let it maintain the **4:3 ratio** <term>sesquitertia: the mathematical ratio 4:3, which produces the interval of a fourth</term>, which is a **fourth**. Likewise, **A** is a tone away from **B**, and **B** is a tone away from **C**; it remains that **C** maintains a distance of a semitone from **D**.

->4:3 Ratio<-
->A FOURTH<-

[A circular diagram representing the intervals of a musical tetrachord. In the upper center, four boxes contain the letters A, B, C, and D, with symbols above each (resembling λ, T, C, and Δ). The bottom half of the circle is divided into three segments indicating the interval spacing: "TONE", "TONE", and "SEMITONE".]

<column-break/>

And if there are five middle notes in the same way: for if the first differs from the fifth by a **3:2 ratio** <gloss>ratio</gloss> <term>sesquialtera: the ratio 3:2, producing the interval of a fifth</term>, and the first from the second, the second from the third, and the fourth from the fifth differ by single tones, then the third will make a difference of a semitone from the fourth. 

Likewise, whatever middle notes are added to the proslambanomenos notes of the other modes, these produce the lower modes. Whichever are added to the **netes** <term>netis: the highest notes of the scale</term> will make them higher. Therefore, in the modes described on the previous page, those which occupy the first proslambanomenos on the reader’s left hand are the lowest. Those on the reader’s right are closed by the last **netes**. Indeed, the mode inscribed as **Hypermixolydian** will be the highest of all, while the **Hypodorian** is the lowest of all.

We, however, starting from the lowest, that is the **Hypodorian**, will designate the others which have a difference between them. For in the **Hypodorian** mode, the **mese** is a tone away from the same **mese** in the **Hypophrygian**. This is easily perceived here: if one compares the **mese** of the Hypophrygian to the symbol $\omega$ of that same Hypophrygian, which is the **mese** of the Hypodorian but the **index note** <term>lycanos meson: the "indicator" or index-finger string in the middle tetrachord</term> in the Hypophrygian. For $\phi$ and $\omega$ differ by a tone, as looking at the cell <note>original: *pagina*</note> demonstrates. 

Likewise, the **mese** of the Hypolydian makes a difference of a tone from the **mese** of the Hypophrygian. For **G**, which is the **mese** of the Hypolydian, is a tone away from $\phi$, which is the **index note** in the Hypolydian, but the **mese** in the Hypophrygian. 

Likewise, the **mese** of the Hypolydian, which is **C**, is a distance of a semitone from the **mese** of the **Dorian**; this can be recognized from the fact that when the scales proceed again, its **mese** (of the Hypolydian) and the **mese** of the **Dorian** are distinguished by a single cell. From this it happens that the **mese** of the **Hypodorian** is distant from the same **mese** of the **Dorian** by a complete **fourth** consonance. And this is proved in this way: For that which is...

It also begins a comparative ranking of the musical modes—from the lowest (Hypodorian) to the highest (Hypermixolydian)—based on the pitch of their central note, the mese.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical demonstration of how the visual grid of musical notation represents specific intervals. The middle note <term>mese: the central pitch and functional heart of the Greek scale</term> in the Hypodorian mode is the same pitch as the lowest note of the middle tetrachord <term>hypate meson: literally "the highest of the middle," though it refers to the lowest pitch of the central four-note grouping</term> in the Dorian mode. From that note which is the middle note in any mode or genus, it forms a **fourth** <term>diatesseron: a musical interval of four steps, based on the 4:3 ratio</term> consonance.

Likewise, the Dorian middle note, which is marked as **M**, differs by a whole tone from the Phrygian middle note, which is [also] **M** <note>The text here describes how the same notation symbol represents different functional positions depending on the mode.</note>. For that which is the middle note in the Dorian is the "index" note of the middle tetrachord <term>licanos meson: the third note from the bottom of the middle tetrachord, often called the "indicator"</term> in the Phrygian mode.

Again, the Phrygian middle note (which is **M**) differs by a whole tone from the Lydian middle note, which is **I**. For that which is the middle note in the Phrygian is the index note of the middle tetrachord in the Lydian.

Again, the middle note of the Lydian mode differs from the Mixolydian middle note (which is **N**) by a semitone. For this arrangement, which correctly contains the middle note, when compared to the arrangement that correctly contains the Mixolydian middle note, is separated on the page but joined together in the sequence.

That same middle note of the Mixolydian, which is **N**, creates a difference of a whole tone from the Hypomixolydian middle note, which is **Γ**. This is because the **N**, which is the middle note in the Mixolydian, is the index note of the middle tetrachord in the Hypomixolydian.

<column-break/>

Whence it happens that the Dorian middle note is separated from the Mixolydian middle note by the consonance of a **fourth** <note>original: *diatesseron*</note>. This is proven in this manner: for the middle note of the Dorian, which is **π**, is the same as the lowest note of the middle tetrachord in the Mixolydian, which is **π**, which maintains a consonance of a fourth in the middle of each individual mode.

Likewise, the Dorian middle note, which is **π**, creates a **fifth** <term>diapente: a musical interval of five steps, based on the 3:2 ratio</term> consonance with the Hypomixolydian middle note, which is **Γ**. For that middle note of the Dorian, which is **π**, is the index note of the lowest tetrachord <term>licanos hypaton: the third note of the lowest four-note scale grouping</term> in the Hypomixolydian arrangement.

And the index note of the lowest tetrachord, compared to the middle note in the diatonic genus <note>The 'diatonic' is the most common scale structure, using two whole tones and a semitone.</note> of any mode, is separated by the consonance of a **fifth**.

Why the eighth mode, which is the Hypomixolydian, was added is clear from this. Let this be the **double-octave** <term>bisdiapason: a scale spanning two full octaves, the standard range of the Greek Greater Perfect System</term> consonance:

<meta>This page concludes Book IV of Boethius’s treatise, moving from the theoretical arrangement of musical modes to a practical demonstration using a monochord. It then introduces the table of contents for Book V, which focuses on the competing theories of Ptolemy, Aristoxenus, and the Pythagoreans.</meta>

Therefore, the **octave** <term>diapason: the interval where a frequency is doubled, sounding like the same note at a higher pitch</term> consonance is formed from **A** to **H**, for it consists of eight names.

We have said, therefore, that the first species of octave is **AH**, the second is **BI**, the third is **CK**, the fourth is **DL**, the fifth is **EM**, the sixth is **FN**, and the seventh is **GO**. There remained, therefore, the extra interval **HP**, which was added so that the entire order might be completed. And this is the eighth mode <note>The Hypermixolydian mode, which Ptolemy added to complete the system of seven modes into an eight-mode scale.</note> which Ptolemy added above.

**How musical consonances can be judged by the ear without doubt.**

# XVIII.

To understand how the movements of consonances are calculated, it can be done with a very short and simple instrument. Let there be a ruler carefully stretched out, **AD**, upon which two "half-spheres"—which the Greeks call *magades* <term>magadas: movable bridges used on a monochord to change the vibrating length of the string</term>—are placed. These should be placed so that a line drawn from the curve at **E** to the point **B** forms right angles. Likewise, let a line drawn from the curve at **F** to the point **C** return right angles. Let these bridges be equally polished on all sides, and let others be prepared in the same way for the same purposes. Above these, let a string be stretched that is equal in thickness throughout its length, which is **AE FD**.

If you wish to discover the nature of the **fourth** <term>diatesseron: a musical interval of four steps</term> consonance, I shall do it thus: From point **E**, where the string touches the bridge, to point **F**, where the string rests on the bridge from the other side, I divide the space **EF** into [seven] parts <note>original: "tribus partibus" (three parts). To achieve the 4:3 ratio of a fourth, one usually divides the whole into seven units to create segments of four and three.</note>. And at the fourth part I place a point, which is **EK**. Therefore, the ratio of **EK** to **KF** is a **sesquitertia** <term>sesquitertia: a 4:3 ratio, where one value contains the other plus one-third of it</term>. If I then place a bridge at **K** equal to the others, and the segments **EK** and **KF** are plucked with a plectrum by alternating hands, they will sound a fourth <gloss>distat: it is distant</gloss> apart.

<column-break/>

But if you strike both at the same time, you will recognize the consonance of a fourth.

If we wish to create a **fifth** <term>diapente: a musical interval of five steps</term>, I divide the whole into five parts; I give three parts to one portion and two to the other <note>This creates the 3:2 ratio required for a perfect fifth.</note>. By placing the bridge according to the method described above, I can weigh the consonances and dissonances.

Likewise, if I wish to test the **octave** consonance, I cut the whole into three parts, distributing them into one and two [parts]. By plucking them together or alternately, I can certainly recognize what sounds harmonious and what sounds discordant.

The **triple** [octave plus fifth] <term>tripla: a 3:1 ratio</term> which is born from mixed consonances is produced thus: if we divide the whole ruler into four divisions, and the entire length of the string is divided into three parts and one part. Thus, with the bridge placed at the three-part mark, the triple proportion returns its dissonance and consonance.

**Here ends the fourth book of Boethius on Music.**
**The Fifth begins.**

# Prologue, Chapter 1.

¶ **On the power of harmony, what its judgments and instruments are, and how far the senses should be trusted. Chapter II.**

...in the number of eight proportions.
¶ IX. A demonstration according to **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), an influential mathematician and astronomer whose musical theories Boethius is now introducing.</note> that the **octave** <term>diapason</term> and **fourth** <term>diatesseron</term> are consonances.
¶ X. What the property of the **octave** <term>diapason</term> consonance is.
¶ XI. In what ways **Ptolemy** establishes the consonances. <gloss>Book V</gloss>
¶ Which notes are consonant, unison <term>equisone: sounds that are different but seem the same to the ear, like an octave</term>, and melodic <term>emmelicę: notes that are not consonant themselves but fit into a melody</term>.
¶ XIII. How **Aristoxenus** <note>A student of Aristotle who argued that musical intervals should be judged by the ear rather than mathematical ratios.</note> considers intervals.
¶ XIV. A description of the eight-stringed instrument <term>octochord</term> through which it is shown that the **octave** <term>diapason</term> consonance is less than six **whole tones**.
¶ XV. How the **fourth** <term>diatesseron</term> consonance is contained within a **tetrachord** <term>a scale of four notes</term>.
¶ XVI. How **Aristoxenus** divides either the **whole tone** or its types, and the arrangement of that division.
¶ XVII. How **Archytas** <note>A Pythagorean philosopher and mathematician.</note> divides the **tetrachords**.
¶ XVIII. How **Ptolemy** criticizes the divisions of the **tetrachords** made by **Aristoxenus** and **Archytas**.
¶ XIX. How **Ptolemy** says the divisions of the **tetrachords** should be made.
¶ XX. How the magnitude of proportions arises from equality.
¶ How **Ptolemy** divides the **fourth** <term>diatesseron</term> into two parts.

After the division of the standard **monochord** <term>a one-stringed instrument used to measure musical ratios</term>, I think those things should be added in which the ancient teachers of Music disagree with a diversity of opinion. A subtle judgment must be held concerning all of them, and whatever is lacking in the work I have proposed must be supplied with a modest arrangement of teaching. For there can also be another division in which not just one string is used to be divided into proportions, but rather eight. Let a **cithara** <note>original: "cithara" — a lyre-like instrument</note> of this kind be made, so that the entire system of proportions may be seen together across several strings, as they are necessary.

<column-break/>

## On the Power of Harmonics, the 
## Instruments of Judgment, and to What 
## Extent the Senses Should Be Trusted.
## Chapter II.

But we shall speak of these things a little later. Now we must describe the power of **Harmonics** <term>Armonica: the science of musical sounds and intervals</term>. In setting out to treat this subject, we have already filled four books. However, we have delayed expressing its true power until this fifth volume. **Harmonics** is the faculty of weighing the differences between high and low sounds through the senses and reason. For the senses and reason are, as it were, the instruments of the harmonic faculty. The sense perceives something confused and approximately like the thing it feels; reason, however, judges the integrity and seeks out the specific differences. Thus, while the sense finds things that are confused and merely close to the truth, reason receives the integrity itself. The sense achieves no perfection, but only comes close; reason is what truly judges. 

It is just as if someone were to draw a circle by hand; perhaps the eye, coming to its aid, would think it is a perfect circle, but reason understands in no way that it is what it pretends to be. This is because the sense is involved with physical matter, and it perceives forms in the same way as that matter—which is fluid, imperfect, and not precisely polished <note>original: "ad unguem" — a Latin idiom meaning "to the fingernail," referring to something finished with extreme precision.</note>. Therefore, confusion follows the sense. But mind and reason are not delayed by matter; they behold the forms they perceive beyond the nature of the physical object. Thus, integrity and truth accompany reason, whereas the sense either errs, or falls short, or must be corrected and completed. Perhaps...

<summary>This page contains the table of contents for Book V of the treatise and introduces a philosophical debate on "Harmonics," arguing that while the ear hears sound, only the mind can truly judge the mathematical truth of music.</summary>
<keywords>Ptolemy, Aristoxenus, Archytas, Harmonics, sense vs. reason, musical ratios, tetrachord, diapason, diatesseron</keywords>

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues Boethius’s argument from Book V regarding the limitations of the human ear compared to mathematical reason. He explains why we cannot trust our senses alone to judge musical intervals, as small errors accumulate into large discrepancies.</meta>

...however, that which the sense recognizes—not completely but confusedly and diminished from the truth, acting as a sort of **clumsy judge** <note>original: "incalidus estimator"—referring to an unskillful or careless evaluator.</note>—is the small degree of error it possesses in individual instances. When these errors are collected, however, they are multiplied into a total sum and therefore create a very great difference.

For if the sense should judge two small notes to be a **whole tone** <term>tonus</term> apart, and they are not; and then it thinks a third note is a **whole tone** from one of them, yet that is not a complete and true distance of a **whole tone**; and likewise if it removes the difference of a third and fourth **whole tone** and errs in that as well; and if it should also think a fifth note is a **semitone** <term>semitonium</term> away from this fourth one, yet does not estimate it truly and completely—in each individual case, the error might perhaps seem small.

But that which the sense leaves behind in the first **whole tone**, added to the error made in the second, third, and fourth **semitone**, will, when gathered together and collected, result in the first voice and the fifth voice containing a **fifth** <term>diapente</term> <note>A musical interval of a perfect fifth.</note> that is entirely different from what it ought to be if the sense had judged the three **whole tones** and the **semitone** perfectly. If the error seemed small in the individual **whole tones**, once collected, it becomes evidently apparent in the **consonance**.

And so, let us consider it this way: so that it may be seen that the sense indeed gathers confused things, but in no way ascends to the integrity of **reason** <term>ratio</term>. For if a line is given, it is not at all difficult for the sense to find another part that is larger or smaller. But once a specific measure is proposed—so that it might find a part exactly so much larger or so much smaller—the first perception of the sense will not achieve this; rather, it requires the skillful invention of **reason**. Or, if it is again proposed to either double a given line or cut it in half, that is perhaps a little more difficult than simply finding a "larger" or "smaller" part,

<column-break/>

yet the sense can still be guided by **reason**. But if it were commanded that a **triple** length of a proposed line be set down, or a **third part** be drawn from it; or a **quadruple** be established, or a **fourth part** be marked off—would this not be impossible for the sense unless the integrity of **reason** is added? This is because the path of **reason** remains fixed, while the sense falters.

For if someone is expected to take away an **eighth part** of a proposed line, or is asked to provide **eight times** the same, he is compelled to take a half of a **whole**, then a half of that half so that it is a **fourth**, and a half of the **fourth** so that it is an **eighth**. Again, one takes a double, then a double of the double so it is **quadruple**, and a double of the **quadruple** so it is **octuple** <note>eight-fold</note>. Thus, in such easy flexibility, the sense achieves nothing; its entire judgment is sudden and remains on the surface, failing to explain integrity and perfection.

Indeed, all judgment must not be granted to the sense of hearing; rather, **reason** must also be presented to rule and temper the wandering sense. By this, the hidden and failing sense may lean as if upon a **staff** <note>original: "baculo"—Boethius uses the metaphor of reason acting as a walking stick for the "blind" or weak senses.</note>. For just as individual arts have certain instruments—some to shape something that is confused and formless, like an **ax**; others to grasp what is exact and whole, like a **compass** <note>original: "circinum"</note>—so too the mental power of the soul has two parts of judgment. One is of the kind through which it perceives the differences of underlying voices by **sense**. The other is that through which the mind considers the complete mode and measure of those same differences.

### What the Harmonic Rule is, or what goal the Pythagoreans, Aristoxenus, or Ptolemy said Harmony has. Chapter 4.

<image-desc>A large ornamental initial letter H, illuminated in blue ink, begins the following paragraph.</image-desc>

**H**aving established this, an instrument of this kind—in which the differences of sounds are sought out by applying the method of **reason**—is called the **harmonic rule** <term>armonica regula: often referring to the monochord, a one-stringed instrument used to measure mathematical intervals</term>.

<summary>Boethius argues that human hearing is an unreliable judge of music because small sensory errors accumulate into large mathematical discrepancies; therefore, reason and instruments like the monochord must guide the ear.</summary>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the relationship between sense perception and mathematical reason in music, transitioning from the limitations of the human ear to the specific philosophical debates between the Pythagorean, Aristoxenian, and Ptolemaic schools regarding the nature and classification of sound.</meta>

In this matter, the opinions of the learned are many and discordant. For some, who placed the greatest trust in **Pythagorean** <note>Followers of Pythagoras, who believed the universe and music were governed by whole-number ratios.</note> disciplines, said that the aim of **harmony** <term>armonia</term> <note>original: "armonici"</note> was this: that all things should follow consistent **reason**. For they believed the **senses** <note>The physical act of hearing.</note> provide, in a way, certain seeds of knowledge, but that **reason** truly perfects it. **Aristoxenus** <note>A Greek music theorist and pupil of Aristotle who argued that the human ear, not just math, should be the judge of music.</note>, however, said to the contrary that reason followed as a companion, but that all things were determined by the judgment of the senses, and one must strive toward their modulation and consensus. By **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy, a scientist who sought a middle ground between the abstract math of the Pythagoreans and the sensory focus of Aristoxenus.</note>, however, the aim of harmony is defined in another way: specifically, so that nothing can be contrary to either the ears or to reason. For according to Ptolemy, harmony seems to intend that what the sense indicates, reason should also weigh. And thus, reason finds the proportions so that the sense does not protest. For the aim of all harmony is mixed from the concord of these two. And in this, he differs most from Aristoxenus and the Pythagoreans: for Aristoxenus trusts nothing to reason but only to the senses, while the Pythagoreans trust the senses very little, but rely most heavily on the proportions of reason. In which Aristoxenus...

<margin>Whether Pythagoras or Ptolemy established that quantity and sharpness consist of the same thing.</margin>

### What sound truly is. Chapter 4.

While all agree that **acuteness** <note>high pitch</note> is a quality of tension, they placed the difference between **gravity** <note>low pitch</note> and acuteness on different grounds. Aristoxenus <unclear>as if</unclear> <unclear>con-</unclear> and the Pythagoreans: Aristoxenus indeed thinks that the differences of sounds according to gravity and acuteness consist in **quality** <note>The "character" or "feeling" of the sound.</note>. The Pythagoreans, however, placed it in **quantity** <note>Physical measurements, such as the length of a string or the frequency of a vibration.</note>. Ptolemy, however, seems closer to the Pythagoreans, because he himself also does not think that gravity and acuteness consist in quality, but in quantity. For indeed, denser and 

<column-break/>

finer bodies produce acuteness; whereas thin and larger bodies produce gravity—to say nothing now of the manner of tension and relaxation. Although when something is relaxed, it is as if it were thinner and thicker; but when it is stretched, it is made denser and stretched thinner.

### Ptolemy's opinion on the differences of sounds. Chapter 5.

With these things thus explained, Ptolemy divides the differences of sounds in this way. Of **voices**, some are **unison** <note>Sounding the same pitch.</note>, others are not. Unisons are those whose sound is one, whether in gravity or in acuteness. Non-unisons, however, are those where one is lower and the other higher. Some of these are such that their boundaries are joined in a common end. For it is not distinct, but is led from low to high in such a way that it appears continuous. Others, however, are non-unison sounds whose difference is distinguished by an intervening silence. When voices are joined at a common boundary, it happens in this way: just as a **rainbow** <note>original: "arcus in nubibus" - an arch in the clouds.</note> is seen, where the colors are so close to one another that there is no certain boundary where one is separated from another. But the charm of the red recedes toward the pale in such a way that, through a continuous change, it turns into the following color with no ~~middle~~ certain middle ground intervening to distinguish the two. This is also what usually happens in voices: as if someone were to strike a **string** <note>original: "neruum" - a gut string of a musical instrument.</note>, and while he strikes it, he turns [the tuning peg]; it happens that at the beginning the pulse is lower, but while it is being turned, that voice is thinned out, and the sound of the low voice becomes continuous with the high.

### Which voices are fit for harmony. Chapter 6.

Since, therefore, among the sounds that are not in unison, some are continuous and others discrete...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion on the classification of sounds and the mathematical ratios of musical intervals. It specifically addresses the debate between the Pythagoreans and Ptolemy over whether the combination of an octave and a fourth (the eleventh) should be considered a "consonance" or pleasant-sounding agreement.</meta>

...gate, and continue in such a way that they are joined together with the same difference at a common boundary. Nor does a high or low voice have a designated place that they hold. Discrete sounds, however, have their own proper places, just like unmixed colors, where the difference of each stands in its own place. Indeed, continuous voices are not separated from the harmonic faculty by being in unison <note>original: "equisone"</note>. Rather, they are unlike themselves and do not sound together as any single thing. Discrete voices, however, are subject to the harmonic art. For the difference between distant and unlike voices can be grasped. Those which, when joined together, can produce a melody are called **musical** <term>emmelic</term> <note>original: "emmelic"; from the Greek "emmelēs," meaning suitable for melody or in-tune.</note>. And those from which a melody cannot be made are called **unmusical** <note>original: "Emmelic" in the transcription, likely a scribal error for "Ecmelic" or "Ammelic," referring to sounds unsuitable for music.</note>.

### How many proportions the Pythagoreans establish.

Those are called **consonant** because, when joined together, they produce sweet and mixed sounds. **Dissonant** ones, however, do so the least. And this indeed is Ptolemy's judgment on the difference of sounds. Now, however, it seems we must say why he disagreed with the others regarding the placement of consonances. For Pythagoras considers the consonances of the **fifth** <term>diapente</term> <note>original: "diapenthe"; the interval of a perfect fifth.</note> and the **fourth** <term>diatesseron</term> <note>the interval of a perfect fourth.</note> to be simple; and from these they join the **octave** <term>diapason</term> <note>the interval of an octave.</note> consonance. For they say that the former sound is triple and this one quadruple <note>The text here refers to the compound intervals: the octave-plus-fifth (3:1) and the double octave (4:1).</note>. But we do not consider the consonance of an octave and a fourth combined to be a true consonance. This is because it does not fall into a **superparticular** <note>A ratio where the numerator is the denominator plus one, such as 3:2 or 4:3.</note> or **multiple** <note>A ratio where one number is a clean multiple of the other, like 2:1 or 3:1.</note> comparison, but into a **multiple superpartient** ratio. For this proportion of voices is as 8 to 3. If someone places the number 4 in the middle of these, he will create these terms: 8, 4, 3. Between 8 and 4, they create the octave consonance; between 4 and 3, they create the fourth. But 8 to

<column-break/>

3 consists in a multiple superpartient ratio. What a "multiple superpartient" is, indeed, must be learned from the books of Arithmetic and from those things which we arranged in the second book of this instruction. The Pythagoreans, therefore, place consonances in multiple and superparticular ratios, as was stated in the same second and fourth books. But they exclude consonances from superpartient and multiple superpartient ratios. In what ways Pythagoras joins the octave as a double ratio (2:1), the fourth as **sesquitertial** (4:3), and the fifth as **sesquialter** (3:2) must be sought from the second and fourth books of this instruction on Music.

### Why Ptolemy criticizes the Pythagoreans regarding the number of proportions.

**Ptolemy** criticizes the Pythagoreans and that entire demonstration which we set forth in the previous books in many ways. In this, he questions why they join the fourth and the fifth as sesquitertial and sesquialter, yet among the remaining superparticular ratios—even though they are of the same mathematical family—they recognize no other consonances at all.

### Demonstration according to Ptolemy regarding the Octave and Fourth consonances.

**He proves**, however, that a certain **symphony** <note>original: "symphonā"; a sounding together or consonance.</note> is made from the octave and the fourth in this way. Since the octave consonance produces such a sound that the whole seems to be like one and the same string—and in this the Pythagoreans also agree—therefore, if any consonance is added to it, that addition is preserved whole and inviolate. For thus a sound is added to the octave consonance as if to a single string. Let there be, therefore, an octave consonance which is contained between the **hypate meson** <note>The lowest note of the middle tetrachord in the Greek musical system.</note> and the **nete diezeugmenon** <note>The highest note of the "disjunct" tetrachord in the Greek musical system.</note>. And as...

<summary>This page discusses the classification of sounds into continuous and discrete, and explores the mathematical disagreement between the Pythagoreans and Ptolemy over whether the octave-plus-fourth interval (an 8:3 ratio) constitutes a true musical consonance.</summary>
<keywords>Boethius, Pythagoras, Ptolemy, consonance, dissonance, diapason, diapente, diatesseron, emmelic, musical ratios, Greek music theory</keywords>

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion regarding the mathematical properties of musical intervals, specifically focusing on the unique nature of the octave and how it interacts with other intervals like the fourth. It builds upon the debate between the Pythagorean school and the astronomer-theorist Ptolemy regarding which intervals should be classified as consonances.</meta>

...it so agrees with itself and is joined together in sound that it strikes the ear as one voice, as if from a single string, and not as if mixed from two. Because of this, when I join a consonance to the **octave** <term>diapason</term> <note>original: "diapason"; the interval of an octave.</note>, it preserves its integrity because it is joined as if to a single vocal sound or string. If, therefore, the **lowest note of the middle tetrachord** <term>hypate</term> <note>original: "hypate"; literally "the highest" in Greek, but referring to the physically highest string which produced the lowest pitch.</note> and the **highest note of the disjunct tetrachord** <term>nete diezeugmenon</term> <note>original: "nete diezeugmen"; the highest note of the disjunct tetrachord in the Greek musical system.</note> are joined at a high pitch, they will be a **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term> <note>original: "diatesseron"; the interval of a perfect fourth.</note>. If we join the *nete diezeugmenon* to the **highest note of the highest tetrachord** <term>nete hyperboleon</term> <note>original: "nete hypbolon"</note>, and the *hypate meson* to the **middle note** <term>mese</term>, each will sound consonant with the other: the *mese* to the *nete diezeugmenon*, and the same *mese* to the *hypate meson*. Likewise, the *nete hyperboleon* to the *nete diezeugmenon* and to the *hypate meson*. Also, if these are lowered toward the deeper part of the sound by a **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term> consonance, there will be a *hypate hypaton* to the *mese*, retaining the consonance of a fourth, and a **next-to-middle note** <term>paramese</term> to the *nete diezeugmenon*. And there is a consonance of the *hypate hypaton* to the *hypate meson*, and the *nete diezeugmenon* to the *paramese*, and the *nete diezeugmenon* to the *hypate meson*. But it is common that the lower note retains the consonance of its own proper fourth, which makes a **fourth plus an octave** <note>original: "diatesseron ac diapason"; the interval of an eleventh.</note>, such as the *hypate hypaton* to the *hypate meson* (a fourth) and to the *nete diezeugmenon* (a fourth plus an octave). Likewise, the *nete hyperboleon*, which is higher, has a consonance of a fourth with its own nearest *nete diezeugmenon*, and a consonance of an **octave plus a fourth** with the *hypate meson*.

### X. What the property of the octave consonance is.

**H**e shows that this happens precisely because the **octave** <term>diapason</term> is almost a single voice; such is the consonance that it creates, in a certain way, a single sound. And just as

<column-break/>

with the number ten: whatever number is added to it, it is preserved whole and inviolate within that ten, whereas in other numbers this does not happen at all. So it is also in this consonance. For if you add two to three, you immediately produce five, and the original species of the numbers is changed. But if you add those same two to ten, you make twelve, and the "two" is preserved in the middle of the "ten." Likewise, the "three" is preserved from the number three in the same way. Therefore, the **symphony of the octave** <term>symphonia diapason</term> <note>original: "symphonia diapason"; a sounding-together of the octave.</note> sustains any other consonance added to it and does not change it. Nor does it turn a consonant sound into a dissonant one. For just as the symphony of the **fifth** <term>diapente</term> <note>original: "diapenthe"</note> joined to the consonance of the octave—namely in a triple proportion—preserves the consonance of the **octave-plus-fifth** <note>original: "diapason ac diapenthe"; a twelfth.</note>, so also the **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term>, since it is a consonance, produces another consonance when joined to the octave. And according to **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), a mathematician and astronomer whose musical theories Boethius is contrasting with those of Pythagoras.</note>, this is the addition of another consonance, which is the **octave-plus-fourth** <note>original: "diapason ac diatesseron"</note>, established in a **multiple superpartient** ratio. This ratio is **eight to three** <note>original: "VIII ad III"</note>, which is a "double super-bipartient" proportion. For eight contains three twice, plus its two "dual" parts, that is, two units.

### XI. In what ways Ptolemy establishes the consonances.

**P**tolemy judges the opinion of the **Pythagoreans** <note>Followers of Pythagoras, who believed that only ratios involving the numbers 1, 2, 3, and 4 (the Tetraktys) could be consonant.</note> in this way. We must now begin to explain in what ways he himself investigates the proportions and measures of consonances. Voices, he says, are either **unisonant** <note>original: "unifone"; having the same pitch.</note> or **not unisonant**. Of the non-unisonant voices, some are **equisonant** <note>original: "equisone"; sounding equal, like octaves.</note>, others are **musical** <term>emmelic</term> <note>original: "emmelis"; suitable for melody.</note>, others are **dissonant**, and others are **unmusical** <term>ecmelic</term> <note>original: "ekmelis"; unsuitable for melody.</note>. **Unisonant** sounds are those which, when struck individually, produce one and the same sound. **Equisonant** sounds are those which, when struck together, produce one sound out of

<summary>This page explains the unique "transparent" quality of the octave consonance, comparing it to the number ten, which allows other intervals (like the fourth) to remain consonant when added to it. It also begins Ptolemy’s classification of sounds into categories like unisonant, equisonant, and melodic.</summary>

<keywords>octave, diapason, fourth, diatessaron, fifth, diapente, Ptolemy, Pythagorean, music theory, mathematical ratios, musical intervals, emmelic, equisonant</keywords>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues the classification of musical intervals based on their mathematical ratios and how they affect the ear, moving from the definition of the octave as a "unison-like" sound to the specific ratios of the fourth, fifth, and their compounds, concluding with a reference to the monochord and an introduction to the theories of Aristoxenus.</meta>

...from two [voices] and produce a somehow simple and single sound, as is the **octave** <term>diapason</term> <note>original: "diapason"</note>, and that same interval doubled, the **double octave** <term>bisdiapason</term>. **Consonances** <term>consone</term> <note>original: "consone"; sounds that blend harmoniously.</note>, however, are those which produce a sweet sound despite being mixed from different pitches, such as the **fifth** <term>diapenthe</term> and the **fourth** <term>diatesseron</term>. **Melodic intervals** <term>emmelis</term> <note>original: "emmelis"; from the Greek for "in tune" or "suitable for melody." These are intervals like the whole tone that are not consonances themselves but are used to build scales.</note> are those which are not indeed consonances, but can nevertheless be properly adapted to melody, as they are the ones that join consonances together. **Dissonances** <term>dissone</term>, on the other hand, are those which do not blend their sounds and strike the sense unpleasantly. **Unmelodic sounds** <term>ekmelis</term> <note>original: "ekmelis"; sounds that cannot be used in a musical system.</note> are those which are not accepted in the joining of consonances. We shall speak about these a little later in the division of the **four-note scales** <term>tetrachordorum</term>. Since, therefore, **unison-like sounds** <term>equisone</term> <note>original: "equisone"; literally "equal-sounding," referring to the octave's unique property of sounding like the same note at a different register.</note> are closest to the ratios of equality, it is necessary that an inequality closest to equality be joined to them. Among unequal ratios, the one closest to equality is the **double** <note>original: "dupla"; the 2:1 ratio.</note>. For it is the first species of multiplicity, and the greater part exceeds the smaller by exactly the amount of that smaller part. Just as two exceeds one by one—which is equal to that same unit. Rightly, therefore, the double ratio is assigned to the unison-like sound, that is, the **octave** <term>diapason</term>. The **double octave** <term>bisdiapason</term> is assigned to the "twice-double," that is, the **quadruple** <note>original: "quadruplo"; the 4:1 ratio.</note>. Those ratios which divide the first and greatest double portion must be assigned to the consonances that divide the **octave** <term>diapason</term> harmony. Thus it follows that the **fifth** <term>diapenthe</term> is joined to the **one-and-a-half** <term>sesquialtera</term> <note>original: "sesquialtere"; the 3:2 ratio.</note> ratio, while the **fourth** <term>diatesseron</term> is joined to the **one-and-a-third** <term>sesquitertia</term> <note>original: "sesquitritie"; the 4:3 ratio.</note> comparison. When joined to other unison-like sounds, these consonances produce other compound consonances, such as the **fifth** and **octave** in a **triple** <note>3:1 ratio.</note> ratio. The **fourth** and **octave** are in that ratio which is **8 to 3**. **Melodic intervals** <term>emmelis</term> are those which

<column-break/>

divide the **fifth** and **fourth** as if into smaller parts, as well as other ratios, of which we shall speak a little later in the division of the **four-note scales** <term>tetrachordorum</term>. Consider these at least as the simple ones.

### Which are unison-like, which are consonant, and which are melodic. Cap. XII.

Therefore, the **unison-like** <term>equisone</term> sounds are the **octave** <term>diapason</term> and the **double octave** <term>bisdiapason</term>, because through their temperament and mixture, a somehow single and simple sound is produced. The **consonances** <term>consone</term> are primarily those in the "superparticular" ratios: the **one-and-a-half** <note>3:2</note> and **one-and-a-third** <note>4:3</note>, that is, the **fifth** <term>diapenthe</term> and **fourth** <term>diatesseron</term>; also the **octave plus a fifth**, and the **octave plus a fourth**. These are composed and joined from unison-like and consonant sounds. **Melodic intervals** <term>emmelis</term> are the remaining sounds which can be placed between these, such as the **tone** <note>a whole step.</note>, which is the difference between a fourth and a fifth. In a certain way, unison-like sounds are joined by certain consonances, as the **octave** is made of a **fourth** and a **fifth**. Consonances, in turn, are made from those which are called **melodic** <term>emmelis</term> sounds: as the same fifth and fourth are made of tones and other ratios to be discussed later. But how the ratio of all these may be gathered is to be taken from that place which we have described at the end of this volume, where a **string was stretched over hemispheres** <note>This refers to the monochord, a scientific instrument used to measure the mathematical ratios of musical intervals by moving a bridge (here called "hemispheres") under a single string.</note>. For there one discovers the unison-resonance of the **octave** and **double octave**, the simple consonances of the **fifth** and **fourth**, and the compound consonances of the **octave plus a fifth** and **octave plus a fourth**, as well as those which are **melodic** <term>emmelis</term> sounds, consisting of the distance of a **tone**.

### How Aristoxenus considers intervals. C. XIII.

What **Aristoxenus** <note>Aristoxenus of Tarentum (4th century BCE), a student of Aristotle who argued that music should be judged by the ear rather than just by mathematical ratios.</note> thinks about these things must be briefly opened. For he, who handled the treatment [of this subject] the least...

<summary>This page classifies musical intervals into three categories—unison-like (octaves), consonant (fourths and fifths), and melodic (tones)—based on their mathematical ratios. It explains how larger intervals are composed of smaller ones and introduces the monochord as the tool for proving these ratios before pivoting to the theories of Aristoxenus.</summary>
<keywords>diapason, diatesseron, diapenthe, equisone, emmelis, Boethius, Pythagorean ratios, musical mathematics, monochord, Aristoxenus, tetrachord</keywords>

--- Page 103 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of Aristoxenus's musical theories, specifically his reliance on the judgment of the ear over mathematical measurement. It transitions into a geometric and physical proof using the "octochord" (an eight-stringed instrument) to demonstrate Ptolemy’s argument that six whole tones are mathematically larger than a single octave.</meta>

<unclear>He</unclear> establishes [nothing], but leaves it to the judgment of the ears. For this reason, he marks the pitches themselves with no <term>numbers</term> <note>original: "numeris"; referring to mathematical ratios</note> lest he collect their proportions; instead, he takes the difference in their middle. He does this so that he might place his investigation not in the pitches themselves, but in that by which they differ from one another. He acts far too improvidently, for he thinks he knows the difference of those pitches for which he has established no magnitude or measure. This man [Aristoxenus], therefore, proposes that the **fourth** <term>diatesseron</term> <note>original: "diatesseron"</note> **consonance** consists of two tones and a semitone; and the **fifth** <term>diapenthe</term> <note>original: "diapenthe"</note> of three tones and a semitone; and the **octave** <term>diapason</term> <note>original: "diapason"</note> of six tones. That this is impossible has been demonstrated in the preceding volumes.

### A description of the eight-stringed instrument <term>octochord</term> <note>original: "octochordi"; a theoretical or physical instrument with eight strings used for demonstrations.</note> by which it is shown that the octave consonance is smaller than six tones. Chapter 14.

Ptolemy teaches, by means of a precise division of the octochord, that the octave falls within six tones <note>meaning the octave is slightly smaller than the total sum of six whole tones</note> in this manner. For let eight strings be stretched—that is, A, B, C, D, E, F, G, and H—and let there be a **whole tone ratio** <term>sesquioctaua</term> <note>original: "sesquioctaua"; the 9:8 ratio which defines a whole tone in Pythagorean tuning.</note> of AK to its neighbor BL, and of BL to CM, and of CM to DN, and of DN to EX, and of EX to FO, and of FO to

<column-break/>

GP. There will be, therefore, six tones. Again, let the middle of string H be divided at point R <note>The OCR reads "K" but the logic and subsequent text "hR" implies the division point is R</note>. AK will then be the double of the length of hR. Therefore, when AK and hR are struck together, they will resound with the **octave** <term>diapason</term> **unison-like sound** <term>equisonantia</term>. If, however, one reaches the length of GP, it will always be a little sharper than hR, and through this, the six tones exceed the octave consonance. For if AK and GP resounded as an octave when struck, then six tones would be [exactly] an octave consonance. But if, these not being consonant, AK and hR resounded as an octave, and hR were sharper than GP, the octave consonance would exceed six tones. But now, because when AK and hR are consonant, this same hR is found to be shorter than GP <note>A shorter string produces a higher/sharper pitch; thus the six-tone string GP is flatter than the octave string hR, meaning six tones do not quite reach the octave, or mathematically, the six tones together are larger than the octave ratio.</note>, it cannot be otherwise than that six tones exceed the octave consonance. And thus, by the sense [of hearing] also, it can be gathered that the octave consonance falls within six tones. In this way, the error of Aristoxenus is refuted without any doubt.
