<summary>This page presents a mathematical and physical refutation of Aristoxenus's claim that an octave consists of exactly six whole tones, using Ptolemy's octochord division to prove the octave is slightly smaller. It emphasizes that musical intervals must be defined by mathematical ratios rather than just the subjective judgment of the ear.</summary>
<keywords>Ptolemy, Aristoxenus, Octochord, Diapason, Sesquioctaua, music theory, mathematical ratios, acoustics</keywords>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page follows a critique of Aristoxenus’s reliance on sensory perception. It moves from general principles of the octave into the specific mathematical divisions of the tetrachord (a four-note scale segment) according to the Aristoxenian school, which uses a system of units rather than the geometric ratios preferred by Pythagoreans like Ptolemy.</meta>

### Chapter 15. That the consonance of a fourth is contained within a tetrachord.

**N**ow we must speak about the division of <term>tetrachords</term> <note>A group of four strings or pitches, which formed the basic building block of ancient Greek music systems.</note>. Indeed, the consonance of a **fourth** is produced by four strings. For this reason, it is also called a <term>diatesseron</term> <note>original: "diatesseron"; literally "through four."</note>. Just as if two other strings were placed between two strings that sound the harmony of a fourth, a tetrachord is created. For two strings are established in the middle which create three ratios among themselves and the outer strings.

### Chapter 16. How Aristoxenus divides either the tone or the genres, and a description of his division.

**A**ristoxenus divides this fourth [diatesseron] through the <term>genres</term> <note>original: "genera"; refers to the three types of scales: Enharmonic, Chromatic, and Diatonic.</note> by the following reasoning. He divides the tone into two parts, and thus he calls it a **semitone**. He divides it again, a third part of which he calls the *diesis* of the **soft chromatic**. He divides it into four, however, and the fourth part with its own middle—that is, an eighth of a tone—he calls the *diesis* of the **hemiolic chromatic** <note>original: "chromatis emnolii"; a "shaded" tuning where the intervals are slightly wider than the standard chromatic.</note>. Since these things are so, and since his first division is twofold: one genus is softer, which is the **Enharmonic**, while the "changed" one is the **Diatonic**. Between these stands the **Chromatic**, participating in both the change and the softness. These result in six mixed genres which differ according to this order: one of the Enharmonic; three of the Chromatic (namely, the soft chromatic, the hemiolic chromatic, and the tonic chromatic); and the remaining two are the **Aeolian** and **Ionian** <note>original: "ayolius atque iatyum"; likely referring to the Aeolian and Ionian (Iastian) tunings of the Diatonic genre.</note> Diatonic. The division of all these according to Aristoxenus is as follows. Since it was stated before that the fourth part of a tone is called the **Enharmonic diesis**, and because Aristoxenus does not

<column-break/>

compare the pitches themselves to one another, but instead measures the sound and the interval. For him, a tone consists of **twelve units**. Since the Enharmonic *diesis* was a fourth part, it equaled **3 units**. Because the consonance of a fourth is joined from two tones and a semitone, the whole fourth would be composed of twice 12 and 6 units <note>12 + 12 + 6 = 30 units</note>. But because it often happens that if one reduces the number to eighth-parts, it produces non-integers, we demonstrate it using other small units. For this reason, the whole fourth is fixed at **60 units**, while a single tone is **24**, and a semitone is **12**. The fourth part, which is called the Enharmonic *diesis*, is **6**. An eighth part, however, is **3**. An eighth joined with a fourth (6 plus 3 <insert>added</insert> <note>original: "annis"; likely a scribal error for "additis" (added).</note> so that they make the *diesis* of the hemiolic chromatic) would be **8** <note>The math suggests 9 units (6+3), but the manuscript records 8.</note>.

With these things established, the three genres—Enharmonic, Chromatic, and Diatonic—seem to have these properties for Aristoxenus: some are called **dense** <note>original: "spissa"; referring to the *pyknon*, a cluster of three notes in a tetrachord where the sum of the two smaller intervals is less than the third.</note> and others not at all. The "dense" ones are those where the two lower intervals do not exceed in magnitude the one remaining interval placed at the high end. They are "not dense" when the two lower intervals are able to overcome the one remaining. The Enharmonic and Chromatic are **dense**; the Diatonic is **not dense**. 

Thus, according to Aristoxenus, the Enharmonic is divided as **6, 6, 48**. Between the lowest string and the one next to it is a fourth part of a tone, which is called the Enharmonic *diesis*, since the tone is composed of 24 units. Likewise, for the second interval, between the second string from the bottom and the third, there is that same fourth part of a tone (6). The remaining units out of the 60, which belong to the tonal ratio, are placed between the third string from the bottom and the highest fourth string: **48**. And the two intervals placed at the bottom, that is 6 and 6, [do not exceed] the one remaining at the top, which is 48.

<summary>This page explains Aristoxenus's method of dividing the musical fourth into units rather than ratios, detailing the specific measurements for the Enharmonic, Chromatic, and Diatonic scales. It introduces the concept of the "dense" (pyknon) intervals where the lower two steps of a tetrachord are smaller than the final upper step.</summary>
<keywords>Aristoxenus, tetrachord, diatesseron, diesis, enharmonic, chromatic, diatonic, dense intervals, pyknon, musical units</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical breakdown of Aristoxenus's musical system. While the previous page began explaining the division of the fourth, this page provides the specific numerical values for the different tunings (shades) of the Chromatic and Diatonic scales. The text uses a system where a fourth (diatesseron) is divided into 60 units to represent the mathematical "space" between notes.</meta>

...does not exceed it. But the **soft chromatic** has this division: 8, 8, 44, so that 8 and 8 are third-parts of tones <note>In this system, if a whole tone is considered to be 24 units, 8 units would be exactly one-third. However, the text mentions a tone of 60 units elsewhere; this inconsistency is common in historical copies of Aristoxenus's theories.</note>. For the tone, as was said, consists of 60 units, and the third part of a tone is the <term>diesis</term> <note>original: "diesis"; a very small interval, smaller than a semitone, used in specialized tunings.</note> of the soft chromatic. Likewise, the <term>diatesseron</term> <note>original: "diatesseron"; the interval of a perfect fourth.</note> of the **hemiolic chromatic** is divided thus: 8, 8, 42. For the *diesis* of the hemiolic chromatic is an eighth part of a tone plus a fifth part—that is, from 60 units, seven and a half. Likewise, according to Aristoxenus, the division of the **tonic chromatic** is: 12, 12, 36. Namely, so that in the first two intervals individual semitones are established, and whatever remains is established in the last. And in all of these, the two proportions <note>The intervals between the first and second, and second and third strings.</note> which are closest to the lower string do not exceed the remaining one—which is placed toward the high pitch—in size. For they are, as was said, of the "dense" kind <note>original: "spissa"; refers to a tuning where the sum of the bottom two intervals is smaller than the top interval.</note>. Indeed, the dense <term>genres</term> are the **Enharmonic** and the **Chromatic**.

But the **Diatonic** division is itself also twofold. And the division of the **soft diatonic** is 12, 18, 30, in this manner: so that 12 is a semitone, 18 is a semitone plus a fifth part of a tone, and 30 is what remains; of these, 18 and 12 make 30, and they do not exceed that which remains. Likewise, the division of the **tonic diatonic** is such that it has a semitone and two whole tones: 12, 24, 24. From which 24 and 12—that is, 36—are not exceeded by the remaining part which is toward the high pitch, but rather they surpass it <note>This means the Tonic Diatonic is not "dense," because the first two intervals (36 units) are larger than the final interval (24 units).</note>. Therefore, this is the aforementioned division of <term>tetrachords</term> according to Aristoxenus, which will be shown in the description provided below.

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical tuning systems. Having previously described Aristoxenus's division of the tetrachord based on "parts" of a tone, the author now transitions to Archytas’s mathematical approach. Archytas, a Pythagorean, relied on rigorous numerical ratios (proportions) rather than the "ears" of Aristoxenus, though the text notes that his results were often criticized for being inconsistent with both logic and sound.</meta>

# The Division of the Enharmonic
## How Archytas divides the tetrachords: and a description of them. Chapter 17.

Archytas, establishing everything by reason, not only neglected to observe the role of the senses in the primary consonances, but also followed reason most particularly in the division of the <term>tetrachords</term> <note>original: "tetrachordorum"; a scale of four notes spanning the interval of a perfect fourth.</note>. However, he did so in such a way that he neither effectively resolved the matters he sought, nor did the reason he proposed agree with the evidence of the senses. He believed there were three <term>genres</term> <note>original: "genera"; the three types of scales: Enharmonic, Diatonic, and Chromatic.</note>: the **Enharmonic**, the **Diatonic**, and the **Chromatic**, in which he established the same lowest and highest sounds. In all genres, he made the lowest sound 2016 <note>original: ".II. xvi."; Boethius uses these large integers to represent string lengths or frequencies without using fractions.</note> and the highest sound 1512 <note>original: ".II. xii."; likely a scribal variant or OCR error for 1512 (M.D.XII.), which completes the 4:3 ratio of the fourth with 2016.</note>. Among these three genres, he places the string closest to the lowest note—the one which is 1944—so that it maintains a <term>sesquivicesima septima</term> <note>original: "sesquivicesimam septimam"; a ratio of 28:27.</note> proportion to 2016.

<column-break/>

After this middle string, he places the third string from the lowest in the **enharmonic genre** at 1890, to which 1944 is joined by a <term>sesquivicesimaquinta</term> <note>original: "sesquivicesimaquinta"; a ratio of 26:25. However, mathematically, 1944 to 1890 is actually a ratio of 36:35 (sesquitricesimaquinta), a common error in manuscript transmissions of Archytas.</note> proportion. Likewise, this 1890 is established in a <term>sesquiquarta</term> <note>original: "sesquiquarta"; a ratio of 5:4.</note> proportion to the highest note, which is 1512 <note>original: ".II. DXII."</note>.

Similarly, in the **diatonic genre**, he places the third string from the lowest (and the second from the highest) at 1701. To this, 1944 is joined by a <term>sesquiseptima</term> <note>original: "sesquiseptima"; a ratio of 8:7.</note> proportion. This 1701 itself is in a <term>sesquioctaua</term> <note>original: "sesquioctaua"; a ratio of 9:8.</note> proportion to the highest note, 1512.

In the **chromatic genre**, he places the string that is third from the lowest and second from the highest at 1792 <note>original: ".I. DCC XC II."</note>. This string is placed so that it relates to 1944 by the same proportion that 256 has to 243 <note>original: ".cclvi. ad .ccxl."; a scribe error in the OCR or manuscript, as the chromatic ratio is usually 243:224.</note>. This 1792, which is the second from the highest, has a proportion to the highest note in the diatonic genre—that is, 1701—but is here compared as the second note from the top in the chromatic genre. The following description shows the form of the divisions of those tetrachords according to the opinion of Archytas:

| Diatonic | Chromatic | Enharmonic |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 1512 | 1512 | 1512 |
| 1701 | 1792 | 1890 |
| 1944 | 1944 | 1944 |
| 2016 | 2016 | 2016 |

## How Ptolemy reproached the division of the tetrachords by Aristoxenus and Archytas: Chapter 18.

But Ptolemy reproaches the division of the tetrachords of both men in this way. First, regarding Archytas, because the second string from the

<column-break/>

highest in the chromatic genre—that is, 1792—is positioned so that it forms no <term>superparticular</term> <note>original: "supparticulare"; a ratio where the numerator is exactly one greater than the denominator (e.g., 3:2, 4:3). Pythagoreans believed these were the only "pure" musical ratios.</note> proportion either to the highest note (1512) or to the nearest note of the same genre (1944). This is a fault because Archytas attributed such great dignity to superparticular comparisons that he even used them in his consonances <note>Ptolemy is calling out Archytas for hypocrisy: Archytas claimed all musical intervals should be superparticular ratios, yet his Chromatic scale uses the "ugly" ratio of 243:224.</note>.

<summary>This page details the mathematical tuning ratios for the three musical genres according to the Pythagorean Archytas and begins Ptolemy's critique of these systems for being inconsistent.</summary>
<keywords>Archytas, Ptolemy, tetrachord, Diatonic, Chromatic, Enharmonic, superparticular ratio, musical proportion, 28:27 ratio, 9:8 ratio</keywords>

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of musical tuning systems from the previous discussion of Archytas’s mathematical approach. While the preceding page introduced his use of specific numerical values (like 2016 and 1944), this page critiques the sensory accuracy of his results and transitions into a critique of Aristoxenus’s divisions before introducing Ptolemy’s distinct methodology.</meta>

reason took up the investigation. From this point, one finds that the sense of hearing perceives the first proportion from the lowest string in the **Chromatic** genre to be larger than it ought to be. For in this Chromatic genre, he made the interval between 1944 <note>original: "I. dcccclxxxIIII."; though the OCR shows 1984, in Boethius's standard text this is 1944</note> and 2016 <note>original: "II. xvI."</note> a <term>sesquidecima septima</term> <note>original: "sesquidecimaseptima"; a ratio of 18:17.</note> proportion. However, according to the customary modulation of the Chromatic genre, it should have been a <term>sesquivicesima prima</term> <note>original: "sesquivicesimaprimā"; a ratio of 21:20.</note>. Likewise, in the **Enharmonic** genre, the division he maintains for the first note from the lowest according to Archytas is such that it ought to be far smaller than what is found in the other genres. But here, he established it as equal to the other genres, as he set the first proportions from the low note in all three genres as a <term>sesquivicesima septima</term> <note>original: "sesquimēsimaseptima"; likely a scribal error for sesquivicesima septima, a ratio of 28:27.</note>.

Furthermore, he blames **Aristoxenus** because, in the Soft Chromatic and the Hemiolic Chromatic, he placed such first and second proportions from the lowest notes that they differ by less than a semitone—a distance that cannot be measured by the senses. Indeed, the first proportion in the division of the Soft Chromatic according to Aristoxenus is 8... and in the Hemiolic Chromatic it is 8.5... they differ by a difference of only half a unit. According to this system, a whole tone is set at 24 units, of which one unit is a twenty-fourth part. Thus, the first proportions from the lowest note in the Soft and Hemiolic Chromatics differ from each other by a twenty-fourth part of a tone; because of this smallness, the hearing perceives the difference with the greatest difficulty. He likewise rebukes Aristoxenus for why he made only two divisions of the **Diatonic** genre—dividing it into the "soft" and "tense"—when other species of the Diatonic genre could also be found.

### How
### Ptolemy says the division of the
### tetrachords should be made. Chapter 18.

Ptolemy divides the <term>tetrachords</term> <note>original: "tetrachorda"; scales of four notes.</note>

<column-break/>

by a different reasoning. He first establishes that between two <term>antiphonal</term> <note>original: "antifonos"; here meaning notes an octave apart.</note> sounds, such a rule should be applied that they exceed each other by <term>superparticular proportions</term> <note>original: "supparticularibus pportionibus"; ratios where the numerator is the denominator plus one, such as 3:2 or 4:3. These were considered the most "pure" in ancient theory.</note>, though unequal ones, since a superparticular ratio cannot be divided into two equal parts <note>A fundamental rule in Pythagorean mathematics: you cannot find a middle integer ratio to split a ratio like 3:2 or 4:3 exactly in half.</note>. Next, he requires that every comparison made to the string which is the lowest among the three should be smaller than the others which are joined to the higher notes.

But among these, those called **dense** <note>original: "spissa"; referring to the "pyknon" or the cluster of the two smallest intervals at the bottom of a tetrachord.</note> must be such that the two proportions closest to the low note are smaller than the single proportion remaining for the high note. In the "non-dense" types, however, such as in the **Diatonic** genres, this never occurs.

<summary>The author critiques the mathematical tuning ratios of Archytas and Aristoxenus for being either acoustically illogical or too small for the ear to perceive, then introduces Ptolemy’s requirement for superparticular ratios in tetrachord division.</summary>
<keywords>Archytas, Aristoxenus, Ptolemy, tetrachord, Chromatic, Diatonic, Enharmonic, superparticular proportion, pyknon, musical ratios</keywords>
